## H: home farm–hypolepis

### home farm
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) property where a son or daughter works for parents and usually lives in the family home.

>  <b>GORDON</b> .. those open fire tuck-ins were the envy of many a youth who worked on the home farm ... <i>(2001,Fresh Fields,28</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a principal farm where two or more properties are held by the same owners.

>  The home farm has 50 ha of alluvial ground and 80 ha of pakihi ... <i>(Feb 1990,NZ Dairy Exporter,4</i>)

>  This season, cow numbers on the home farm will number 350 ... <i>(Sep 22, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,Farming 2000 Suppt 13</i>)

>  i hope i'd be able to push up the stocking rate by getting the heifers off the home farm and being able to winter the stock better. Yearling heifers are held on the home farm .. About the end of July the cows were taken from the run-off to the home farm and run as a springing mob. <i>(Nov 1975,NZ Dairy Exporter,11</i>)

>  The home farm fattens around 70 steers to two years which grade out as Ps. <i>(May 26, 1993,NZ Farmer,27</i>)

>  it is unprofitable to graze-off drystock when pasture on the home farm is being poorly utilised. <i>(Sep 1997,NZ Dairy Exporter,29</i>)

>  The home farm covers 513 hectares while a second property of 96 hectares is nearby on the Rawhitiroa Rd. <i>(Mar 15, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,21</i>)

### home freight
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> Traditionally, crews were entitled to what was known as 'home freight', 25 kilograms of fish green weight per crew member to be taken home after each trip' [as a PERK] <i>(2004,Hooked,239</i>)

### Home Government
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... it may be necessary for you also to act to the best of your own judgement in circumstances in which you might have preferred ... the specific directions of the Home Government. <i>(Mar 18, 1848,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Mar 18 Earl Grey to Governor Grey in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 30]

>  ... but anything more treacherous than the Home Govt. [sic] in luring on the Colony to such ... expenditure for the final settlement of this Maori question, ... i never heard of. <i>(Aug 5, 1864,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/117,(1860) ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Jane M. Atkinson)]

>  <b>KNOX</b> [The Maori War] cost the Government a vast amount of money, ... led to bitter quarrels between the civil and military authorities, and very often was a matter of much perplexity to the Home Government of Great Britain; ... <i>(1888,Boy Travellers,241</i>)

### Home Guard
 
 <i>n.</i> Home Defence Force, WWii

>  The Home Guard has atrtracted little attention from historians... There was nothing glamorous about the Home Guard... it was nearly a year after war was declared that the War Cabinet finally laid plans for the formation of a volunteer Home Guard, to comprise men who were too old or to young to serve overseas, or who were being kept at home in essential occupations... The urgency and seriousness of the war eventually forced Home Guard units to be set up in almost every settlement and town in the country... The Home Guard in New Zealand eventually numbered 12,000 in its ranks. <i>(Oct 2003,NZ Memories,No. 44/28</i>)

### home invader
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Prime Minister Jenny Shipley will announce tougher penalties for home invaders today... The measures "to deal to" home invaders were flagged by Police Minister Clem Simich in a speech distributed yesterday. <i>(Feb 16, 1999,Dominion,1</i>)

### home invasion
 
 <i>n.</i> [Jesse Sheidlower (jester@panix.com) says a quick US search turned up this term in 1989 (<i>Chicago Tribune</i> Nov. 23, p. C26 "Suburb Cops Zero in on Ring Involved in Home Invasions" and believes there would be much earlier examples; his earliest was a 1978 article in a trade magazine about credit cards. Michael Proffitt (mproffitt@oup.co.uk) has this from 1988 Sara Paretsky <i>Toxic Shock</i> (1990) xxix. 214 "The steel-lined door I'd had installed after my last home invasion had held" and says they have Canadian evidence from 1993 and "home invasion" and "home invader" from Australian newspapers from 1994 onwards. Wayne Glowka (Georgia College & State University; wglowka@mail.gcsu.edu) has a Canadian usage from 1997 <i>Globe and Mail</i> 9 Dec. p. A7 "The terms home invasion or reesidential robbery surfaced in Winnipeg about 18 months ago after a series of violent break-ins where seniors, who happened to be at home at the time, were injured." He also quotes a correspondent who says the term has been common in British Columbia for perhaps 10 years.]

>  He was awoken by four people wearing balaclavas before "home invasions" became a catchphrase. <i>(Jan 23, 1999,Dominion,19</i>)

>  Mrs Shipley will announce the Government's response to the "home invasions" when she outlines the Government's legislative programme at Parliament's first sitting... Jenny Shipley announced the Government would draft a new definition of home invasion following a series of high profile incidents in the last three months. Sentences for home invasion crimes.. would be increased by a maximum of five years. <i>(Feb 16, 1999,Dominion,1</i>)

>  i move, <i>That the House take note of the report of the Justice and Law Reform Committee on the Crimes (Home invasion) Amendment Bill</i>... Home invasion is not an everyday offence. <i>(1999,NZ Parliamentary Debates,575 : 17550</i>)

>  The definition of home invasion.. is intended to cover all situations where an offender commits an offence in the process of entering, while inside, and while leaving, places where people live. in the bill as introduced, a home invasion must occur in a "dwellinghouse"... The definition of "dwellinghouse" is.. .intended to encompass the variety of places in which people can live, including caravans, mobile homes and other structures. it focuses on the use to which a building is put, and is therefore not intended to include buildings or parts of buildings that have some other primary purpose, such as a commercial buildings or hospitals... 17A.. 'Dwellinghouse' means (a) a building or other structure, or part of a building or other structure, that is used by the occupant principally as a residence; or (b) A mobile home, caravan, or houseboat, that is used by the occupant principally as a residence; "Home invasion", when used in the expression 'the offence involved home invasion'.. means that the person who committed the offence did so... (a) While breaking and entering, or otherwise unlawfully entering, an occupied dwellinghouse; or (b) While in an occupied dwellinghouse, after having broken and entered, or otherwise unlawfully entered, the dwellinghouse; or (c) While breaking out of an occupied dwellinghouse; or (d) While otherwise unlawfully in an occupied dwellinghouse <i>(1999,NZ Statutes,262</i>) [NOTES: Report from the Justice & Law Committee]

>  The agency is reviewing its policy.. after a 13-year-old responsible for nine previous aggravated robberies led a brutal home invasion attack in a Gisborne couple earlier this year. <i>(Aug 14, 1999,Dominion,25</i>)

>  A 92-year-old woman was attacked in a vicious home invasion in Paekakariki last night... A senior officer said the attackers could be dealt with under new home invasion laws if they were caught. <i>(Sep 14, 1999,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  Police don't record "home invasions" in their statistics, but talk instead of aggravated burglaries...Young blames the media use of the imported "home invasion" tag, which he believes "gives the impression that this is a new wave of criminal activity".... More than simply expressing the fundamentals of the crime, "home invasion conveys something of that moment when a gang bursts through the door. <i>(Jan 30, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  A brutal home invasion has triggered an unfamiliar sense of vulnerability in isolated rural areas. <i>(Dec 19, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  On the night of November 30, 1988, a murder was committed that shocked and outraged the nation. The murder of Beverly Bouma at her farmhouse became synonymous with the term "home invasion". <i>(Nov 6, 2000,Evening Post,TV Week : 6</i>)

### home kid
 
 <i>n.</i> a child from an orphanage

>  There was a large proportion of attendees from two orphanages in the area... The children were known as "home kids." <i>(Feb 1989,North & South,32</i>)

>  i grew up in Masterton in a foster home in the 1930s and 40s... Recently we had a class reunion, 49 years on, and guess what? None of us home kids had been in jail and none of us needed to excuse our backgrounds. <i>(May 24, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

### home kill
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to slaughter animals for homestead consumption. (See [home-kill](hom-hyp.md#home-kill))

>  Homekill, beat the Xmas rush, call Country Meats now. <i>(Nov 17, 2001,Manawatu Evening Standard,26</i>)

>  Do you home kill? <i>(Feb 14, 2001,Fencepost.com,Expert Farming Page</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an animal to be slaughtered for on-farm consumption. (See [home-kill](hom-hyp.md#home-kill))

>  sheep to be killed for household consumption - a home kill. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### Home mail
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> But sometimes Marigold found Home mail days not quite enough. <i>ibid.</i> 173 Yesterday the Home mails had arrived ... <i>(1929,Riven,92</i>)

>  [Hdg] The Home Mails. <i>(Oct 21, 1924,Otago Witness,27</i>)

### Home of Compassion
 
 <i>n.</i> any of the homes and hospitals run by the Sisters of Compassion esp. for incurables and young children

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> She had established that their mother had passed away at the Home of Compassion in Wellington in '79. <i>(1990,Bad Music,167</i>)

### home of gold
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>LADY BARKER</b> One [of the bushmen] had been a digger, and was pronouncing an opinion that this creek was very likely to prove a `home of gold' some day. ... At this time of the year the water is very shallow, ...; and they talk of having `found the colour' (of gold) in some places. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,186</i>)

### home on the pig's back
 
 <i>phr.</i> successful, victorious (cf. home and dry/hosed etc.) [[W Ramson: NSW rural sport of riding pigs]] (See [also pig's back](../A#also-pig's-back),[on the](../O#on-the))

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>Home on the pig's back</i>. Very successful!; thoroughly (and easily): a c.p., mostly among New Zealanders and Australians: from ca. 1910. Cf. (?) <i>save one's bacon. </i> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,400</i>)

>  <b>HiSLOP</b> Sir Swithen [a horse] was not home and dried, nor was he home on the pig's back, as some would also say of an easy win. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,34</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> We were home, if not dry, on the pig's back. <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,127</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Got into a good team in the city, got into the reps and when they won the Shield he was home on the pig's back. Never looked back. Everything went right <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,158</i>)

>  <b>MUiR</b> After that we're home on the pig's back. if the books sell and the customers pay, that is. <i>(1960,Word for Word,171</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> This is a happy state of affairs; any and every difficulty is now past; good luck attends and the enterprise will continue to prosper, as it is now doing. .in 4;`He put in the irrigation; there was a lot of hard work but now buyers come from everywhere for his garden-stuff and he's getting rich fast. He's home on the pig's back.' P: <i>home on the pig's back </i>very successful!; thoroughly (and easily) ca1910: a catchphrase, mostly among NZders and Australians. <i>(1962,Australian & NZ English [TS],519</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> <i>home on the pig's back</i> very successful or easy; eg `if this horse wins, we're home on the pig's back.' Mostly NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,57</i>)

### home paddock
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the farming ancestral hearth

>  <b>KERR</b> Some farmers' daughters are business or professional women and have left the 'home paddock' for countries around the world. <i>(1976,From the Beginning: chronicles of a county,331</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Sport) 'home' rugby field [rugby.]

>  This is a good chance to get out on our home paddock and put a pretty passionate performance together... <i>(Aug 12, 2005,NZ Herald,Super Sport D4</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a paddock in close proximity to a homestead area on a station or farm.

>  Young Quail-hawks, near the home paddocks on the Rangitata River. Notes (Potts Birds) <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/51</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... the wethers for home consumption, which consisted of a good-sized flock of many hundred sheep, turned into the home-paddock - an enclosure of some five or six hundred acres ... <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,251</i>)

>  <b>DONEY</b> in selecting the spot for the yards, sheds and home paddocks, it will be necessary, in the first instance, to see that there is a good water supply. <i>(1891,Essays on Bush-Farming,9</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> The home paddocks caught and held his imagination. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,Foreword x</i>)

>  <b>ASPiNALL</b> in due course we got the biggest and the worst into the home paddocks and partially trained them. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,179</i>)

>  <b>LADY BARKER</b> .. the country outside the home paddock is too rough for thin material. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,66</i>)

>  She was halfway across the home paddock before she remembered <i>(Nov 6, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)

### home run
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a principal property of two or more of the same owner, where the main homestead is located or where the owner resides.

>  <b>BAGNALL</b> ... Te Awaite, as well as the home run of 10,000 ... <i>(1976,Wairarapa : An Historical Excursion,340</i>)

>  i rode the length and breadth of the Home run but could not find a sheep. <i>(Feb 18, 1868,Chudleigh Diary,216,1950 ed.</i>)

### home section
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mr G. H. Lightfoot (Timaru) and Mr J. Hollows (Dunedin), of the Labour Department, met the applicants for the Willowbridge workers' home sections at Waimate Courthouse last evening and balloted for the sections ... <i>(Sep 4, 1913,Ashburton Guardian,6</i>)

>  FOR SALE, for one week only, at GBP6 per foot; adjoining kind, GBP9 per foot. Ten per cent, deposit; balance in 10 years; interest 6 1/4 per cent. The Finest Residential Sites in the City. We recommend them specially [sic] to those who want a Real Home Section. <i>(Oct 27, 1894,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  THE LAND COMMiSSiON. .. Frederick Bryane, labourer, holder of a workmen's home section and lease in perpetuity, expresed [sic] his entire satisfaction with the tenure and of the way he had been treated. <i>(May 3, 1905,Otago Witness,16</i>)

>  Steady demand continues for the workers' home sections at island Bay. <i>(Sep 11, 1911,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  These two Properties work admirably together, and anyone desiring a home section and another property further away to work, this is indeed a valuable proposition. <i>(Jan 2, 1920,Poverty Bay Herald,10</i>)

### home separation
 
 <i>n.</i> the skimming or separating of cream from milk at the farm where the milk has been produced (rather than being sent as whole milk to a skimming station or dairy factory).

>  By 'home separation' is meant the skimming of milk by small cream-separators on the farm and supplying the cream to butter-factories .. the butter generally made at 'home separation' factories is of a distinctly lower grade than the butter made from the cream at factories and skimming-stations where the whole milk is delivered. <i>(Jun 15, 1910,NZ Journal of Agriculture,34</i>)

>  <b>PHiLPOTT</b> Although home separation has been a most important factor in the working of the butter side of our dairy industry, and today over 95 per cent of our butter is made from cream separated on the farms, the system did not come into practice without strong opposition. Home-separation developed in the first instance on farms remote from a factory or skimming-station ... <i>(1937,History of the NZ Dairy industry,296</i>)

>  <b>YEREX</b> The first separators went into the early factories and creameries but it was 'home separation' that made the really radical change. [home-separation] <i>(1989,Empire of the Dairy Farmers,50</i>)

### home station
 
 <i>n.</i> the homestead and outbuildings of a station.

>  <b>ADAMS</b> The home-station was situated at the mouth of the river Motunau. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,69</i>)

>  ... returned to Home Station <i>(Mar 2, 1855,St Leonard's Journal</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> if you have not decide on the site of your home-station on a previous visit to the run .... <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,88</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> He [the shepherd] was in a bad way, so i went off as fast as i could to the home station, whence the overseer sent men to fetch him, ... <i>(Jul 20, 1861,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,54,1914 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We reach the home paddock without further trouble <i>(Dec 15, 1923,Aussie (NZ Section),iii</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> One evening, i remember a neighbouring back- station holder, after a long day's journey, rode up late along the fence-side of our home-paddocks, amidst a chorus of challenge from the sheep-dogs. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,208</i>)

>  There is no food to be seen on the run outside the home paddocks. <i>ibid.</i> Sep 20, 407 The action ... in driving my stock from Mairangi and so overcrowding the home paddocks cuts into me in every way. <i>(Apr 28, 1888,Chudleigh Diary,361,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Anthoxanthum odoratum</i> appeared in 1885 on a low clay hillock in the home paddock .. <i>(1908,TrNZi,40/513</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Sometimes he would be lured temporarily into an adjoining paddock for a time, but before long he'd be back in the home paddock again waiting hopefully for a visitor to bolt. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,194,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> i left the house, crossed the home paddocks. <i>(1971,Plekhov Place, The,54</i>)

>  <b>DAViES</b> Get a few lousy bags of fertiliser ... and do a few of the home paddocks ... <i>(1975,Outback,72</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> When the season is over the rams will congregate and hang around the gate, waiting to return to their home paddock to build up their ... exhausted bodies in readiness for the next season. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,149</i>)

>  [cf lower station] i found Swan [Thompson's] overseer at the lower station ... it was very late before the cattle got to the station ... Swan went back to the home station. 24th. Fine. We tailed the cattle all day and Swan took delivery of them in the evening. <i>(May 23, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,88</i>)

>  [cf out-station] We ... got some twenty miles up ... as far as Jack's hut, the last hut up the river, Jack being at the home station, Butler's old run. <i>(Aug 13, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,197,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... by the time we had reached the Home [32] Station we were quite ready for luncheon. All the work connected with the sheep is carried on here. The manager has a nice house; and the wool-shed, men's huts, dip &c., are near each other. <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,31,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOYLE</b> ... the stock of horses was overhauded for the purpose of selecting one or two to take back to the Home Station. <i>(1868,Fragments of a Journal,33</i>)

>  <b>PRENTiCE</b> There was one outlying shepherd who slept three or four miles from the Home-Station. <i>(1870,Tale of NZ,30</i>)

>  <b>WiERZBiCK</b> Webster has another Station which is called the home station of another settler, Cameron, ... <i>(Jan 25, 1873,Wilson Family,149</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Jan 25 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 149]

>  <b>HAAST</b> Having decided to ascend, first the principal branch of the Rakaia, of which the original name has been preserved, i followed the track leading from Mr. Neave's home station along the base of Mount Aegidas [sic] to the Mathias branch. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,124</i>)

>  <b>iNGLiS</b> Waimate, too! i remember when there was but the home-station here [and] one `bush pub'. . <i>(1887,Our NZ Cousins,167</i>)

>  <b>DUNCAN</b> The buildings of the home-station were represented by a `futter' and a long narrow hut. <i>(1888,Wakatipians,22</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> The custom in the old days was to call the buildings on a run `the station,' and the land that went with it was `the run.' On a big property you could find several `stations,' and the principal one was called `the home station,' while the others were `out-stations.' This was the old Australian usage which was carried over to New Zealand at first, but gradually the signification was altered by loosely calling the whole run a station, and then the real station became a homestead and the out-stations became shepherds' huts. in this present book it will be noticed that generally the writer has preserved the olden custom of calling the main buildings the station and the land the run. The runs over in Australia being, as a rule, much larger than those in New Zealand, the `station' and `out-station' phraseology was preserved much longer than over here, where even in advertisements in the late 'fifties we see the English word `homestead' ousting the colonial word `station.' <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,11</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> it is usually understood that in the early days the word `station' denoted the owner's house and its immediate buildings, while `run' meant the whole property. Strange to say, i only noted the word `station' twice, and each time with a diverse meaning. On July 21, 1865, the diary says: `Sold 17 head of cattle - remaining on station 15 head.' Then on September 11, 1865, we read: `Wilkinson returned to the Home Station.' The first of these entries means the whole run - the second the homestead or main buildings. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,115</i>) [NOTES: -->1947 Beattie Early Runholding 115 quoting 1856 July 21 Puketoi Station Diary]

>  <b>GRiFFiTHS KiNG</b> The Rees family barely had time to get settled again at their home station before the quiet pastoral barony ... was invaded by a noisy and often drunken avalanche of gold-seekers. <i>(1971,Wakatip,73</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a principal station, or one where the main homestead stands.

>  <b>ADAMS</b> The home-station was situated at the mouth of the river Motunau, a small stream which affords considerable facilities for the important operation of sheep-washing ... <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,69</i>)

>  On Tuesday last he slept in an out whare on the run, about two miles distant from the home station. <i>(Aug 27, 1872,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> A large number were kept on the Taiko Flat ... about seven miles westward of the home station. <i>(1937,George Rhodes of the Levels and His Brothers,103</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> As the track was still unfinished they decided to leave the sheep in the Wilberforce in the care of a shepherd and return to the home station to shear ... [home-station] <i>(1959,Great Days in NZ Exploration,123</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> if you have not decided on the site of your home station on a previous visit to the run, the first step will be to ride well over it. <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,89</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The former practised a totally different management policy to the home station. The home station ran all merinos ... <i>(1949,High Country Days,181</i>)

### Home steamer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Home steamers can enter the roadstead and pick up cargo without difficulty <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,42</i>)

### home unit
 
 <i>n.</i> any of a group of similar, small dwellings which may be bought or rented to live in, ?NZ unit.

>  Less than desirable properties such as home units, leasehold properties or those that have been built by a bodger par excellence sell for a fraction of their architecturally-designed neighbours. <i>(Feb 22, 2006,NZ Herald,H3</i>) [NOTES: Cross-referenced with <i>bodger</i>]

>  home unit n. Austral. & N.Z. a self-contained residence which is part of a series of similar residences. Often shortened to unit. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,535</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 358; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 234]]

>  That was what her home unit in Onehunga was worth shared among her four siblings. <i>(May 1987,Metro,70</i>) [NOTES: Roger `Auckland Life]

>  Review NZPOD Town house is in, and uneasily acceptable as a `residence in town esp. of person with house in the country' although `in the country' could be better phrased; but `house in (esp. terraced) group in town' is not apt, and increasingly the style for this type of dwelling is townhouse. That is, `My town house is a townhouse', correctly distinguishes, on the page, and will be distinguished in speech; tow stresses for the one, and one stress (TOWNhouse) for the other. There is as well a question of nuance to be noticed. A townhouse is emphatically upmarket from a home unit. <i>(1987,Te Reo,30/132</i>) [NOTES: Smithyman]

### home-aid
 
 <i>n.</i> A girl or woman, usually part-time or temporary, domestic servant; a person employed, often by a public organisation, to help with housework, etc; home-aid has often denoted spec. the official domestic servants, employed by the Home-aid Service of Labour Department, hired temporarily by families when a parent is ill, having children, or in other difficulties. (There have been similar services run by, eg. the Women's Division of the Federated Farmers, and by the Churches.) [since ca195?.]

### home-alone
 
 <i>adj.</i> [probably US; the English equivalent seems to be "latchkey kids".]

>  Hollywood ensured the words Home Alone were something to chuckle about. A 10-year-old's mischievous romps while left to his own devices were laughed at by millions. However, in New Zealand the phrase has a more depressing definition. "Home alone" is synonymous with the neglect of children for their welfare or the foolhardy belief "nothing can go wrong" even if mum and dad are just popping to the shops for 15 minutes.... The home-alone law has been accused of hurting children because fining parents meant there would be less money for the family. <i>(Oct 26, 1997,Sunday Star Times,A8</i>)

>  Nobody knows if the home alone problem is worse than it used to be, because no statistics are kept. <i>(Oct 1, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  [Hdg] Home-alone case dropped. No charges will be laid in a Christchurch case in which four children were left alone last weekend. <i>(Apr 10, 1995,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  in August, Social Welfare discovered 22 home alone cases in the lower North island. <i>(Oct 1, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  Home Alone: A woman faces neglect charges after two sisters aged 10 and 12 were left alone in their Rotorua home. <i>(May 7, 1995,Sunday Star Times,A2</i>)

>  [Hdg] Home-alone girls had only cereal to eat. Two young girls found "home alone" in a Rotorua house last night had nothing to eat but breakfast cereal. <i>(May 6, 1995,Dominion,1</i>)

### home-brew
 
 <i>n.</i> home-produced tobacco [(Nelson-Motueka)]

>  And just like us, sooner or later they worry and give up smoking ... A dogged minority experiments manfully in home-produced tobacco. They call it `home-brew'. <i>(Mar 19, 1952,Evening Post,8/5</i>) [NOTES: [Jim Henderson; Tobacco Growers in Riwaka Valley]]

### Home-going
 
 <i>adj.</i> (of a ship) bound for Britain

>  in the early nineties, however, Auckland seldom saw those Home-going steamers. <i>(Jun 1955,Landfall,9(9): 132</i>)

>  The early shipments of this class of cheese are now being marketed in London, and cable advice has reached us within the last few days from Mr. Wright, inspector of New Zealand Dairy-produce in London, which confirms the judgment of our officers on the quality of the produce before it was loaded into the Home-going steamers. <i>(Jan 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,316</i>)

### home-hosting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He says the city may have to consider creative solutions such as "home-hosting". <i>(Apr 15, 2004,Wellington Contact,2</i>)

### home-kill
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) meat that is slaughtered, dressed and processed on the farm for human or dog consumption. (See [farmkill](f69-fin.md#farmkill))

>  Homekill includes the slaughter, dressing and processing of animals .. Homekill meat can only be used by the animal's owner, their family or household and those actively engaged in running the farm. <i>(Nov 11, 1999,NZ Farmer,20</i>)

>  What is homekill? <i>(2001,Lifestyle Farmer,Aug/Sept 52</i>)

>  Home-kill is relatively straight forward if you observe some basic rules. <i>(2001,Lifestyle Farmer,Oct/Nov 65</i>)

>  i have checked out the MAF site and found a list of licensed Homekill operators. <i>(Nov 4, 2001,Lifestyleblock.co.nz,Gary</i>)

>  Most farmers opt for a combination of home-kill and a good quality supermarket/feed store dry diet. Options for farmers feeding hill country dogs include..a hugh fat/high protein food (eg home-kill mutton or some pet rolls during times of work stress (eg mustering.) <i>(Apr 2, 2001,Fencepost.com,Expert Farming</i>)

### homebake
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  `We think it must be made clear that imprisonment will be the normal penalty for `homebaking' morphine or heroin,' said his Honour. <i>(May 27, 1985,Press, The,22</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an opiate derived illicitly from codeine-based pharmaceuticals [Internet search Feb 2006: also used in Austral, UK, N Amer. NZ hits were prevalent.]

>  The "No 8 wire" mentality also shone through. When police stopped the importation of heroin in the early 1980s, drug users invented homebake. <i>(Jul 18, 2005,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  i woke up in a skanky hotel room ... Everything i held dear to me was falling apart. <i>(May 5, 2001,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  Homebake, opiate created from over-the-counter medicine containing codeine, merely fed an existing demand, but not for long, because drug companies changed the formnula to make it a less appealing option. <i>(Apr 5, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  "in New Zealand we make our own. it isn't heroin, it's something called homebake, which is usually produced from medicines used for legitimate purposes," executive director Sally Jackman says. <i>(Apr 12, 2002,Dominion,9</i>)

>  `Home bake' - it's dicing with death [headline] Addicts shooting home bake are `dicing with death'...The druggies use them [codeine, etc., tablets] to make heroin or morphine. They call their home made drugs `home bake'. <i>(Dec 4, 1984,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  The manufacture of `homebake' was fairly simple for someone with experience, he said. Codeine could be extracted from commonly sold pain-killing tablets, either bought or stolen, and converted to morphine. <i>(May 27, 1985,Press, The,22</i>)

>  Homebake's popularity has also declined with the influx of bakers and subsequent plummet in quality. <i>(Apr 5, 1986,Evening Post,19</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  `Homebake' morphine and heroin manufacturers should clearly be punished by imprisonment, ... `Homebake manufacture appeared to be widespread partly because of the shortage of illegally imported morphine and heroin, he said. <i>(May 25, 1985,Press, The,22</i>)

>  Before going to the supermarket to rob it, Ward injected herself with a homebake drug. <i>(Apr 15, 1989,Dominion,3</i>)

### homebaker
 
 <i>n.</i> one who manufactures (? and/or uses) homebake morphine.

>  Jail Promised All `Homebakers' [headline] <i>(May 25, 1985,Press, The,22</i>)

>  [drugusers'] Homebakers usually sell by the millilitre. That amount is considered to be a `hit'. <i>(Apr 5, 1986,Evening Post,19</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [also Home boat](../A#also-home-boat))

 <i>n.</i> sliced bread

### Homeland, the
 
 <i>n.</i> Britain

### homer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Some Telecom linesmen are stealing push-button telephones from their employers and illegally installing them in the homes of friends ... for a [dollar]50 fee. Most of the jobs, which Telecom linesmen call `homers', involve replacing an old dial telephone with two push-button models, one on an extension. <i>(Dec 20, 1987,Sunday Star (Auckland),M1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a wound bad enough to send one home to New Zealand, cf WW1 blighty @n, buckshee n2..

>  He wagged his stumps at me. `Look at me ... . i've got a homer'. <i>(Oct 5, 1942,2NZEF Times,5</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> He ... collected a nasty thigh wound. `A homer', said several gunners enviously. <i>(1944,Troop Target,27</i>)

>  <b>DAViN</b> [WW1] He looked at my hand [which had been injured]. `i heard you'd got a homer,' he said. <i>(1959,No Remittance,151</i>)

>  homer n. World War 1 slang for a bad wound. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> One of the injured hopefully hobbled to the M.O. and asked anxiously, `Do you think she's a homer, Doc?' <i>(1970,On the Ball,153</i>)

>  i've got a homer. <i>(Oct 5, 1942,NZEF Times,ii/67 : 5</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a horse that can be relied on to return home alone if necessary.

>  <b>PORTER</b> The horse would make the return to the Point himself; he was a good homer and knew where the chaff bin was. <i>(1970,Under the Nor'West Arch,49</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  At first sight he seemed well in the running for a "homer". <i>(Aug 14, 1944,NZEF Times,iV/164 : 5</i>)

>  He ought to get a homer out of that <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in the Middle East,11</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> [WW2] `Everyone does [crack up sometime],' agrees Cadman briefly. Cryptically he adds, `Some get homers. Others get base jobs. The rest?' <i>(1951,Brave Company,93</i>)

### homestay
 
 <i>n.</i> a home that takes paying guests

>  When i travel, i become aware that our economic downturn has produced cottage industries; homestays where you can eat food from the garden; a huge growth in the individuality of each region's fresh food and wine and wonderful ethnic restaurants. <i>(Sep 1999,Cuisine,No. 76 : 161</i>)

>  <b>McLauchlan ed.</b> That was how the Sumerbottoms planned to make a go of it all: an elegant, patrician home-stay property for wealthy people from the cities. <i>(2000,Morrieson's Motel,140</i>)

>  Two illegal home-stays on Kapiti island have been closed by Kapiti Coast District Council. <i>(Oct 22, 1999,Dominion,3</i>)

>  Locals often bring their homestay guests to have a look. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/94</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  She lives with her husband on a farm a few miles out of Rangiora, where they breed racehorses, run deer and a few sheep and take in homestays. <i>(Nov 23, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

### homestead
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the central compound of buildings on a large station.

>  <b>TiFFEN</b> This vast property was unique in having three homesteads. Molesworth was the head station and there were houses at Tarndale, twenty-five miles away, and at Rainbow, forty miles distant. <i>(1845,Journal,119</i>) [NOTES: in Cresswell (1949) Early New Zealand Families]

>  <b>MCDONALD</b> The run-holder had a pre-emptive right over eighty acres for his homestead. <i>(1962,White Stone Country,35</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> All told they keep a permanent staff of about ten including a married couple in the cookhouse at the homestead; another married man is stationed at an out-station over by the Rangitikei bridge on the Moawhango-Taihape road. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North island,145</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> All the back stations had a store or shop attached to the homestead where they kept a supply of clothing, towels, soap, tobacco, papers, matches and the like. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,46</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> ... and the kingfisher, the chiming bell-bird, black robin, flycatcher, ... are common round every homestead. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/120</i>)

>  <b>ALGAR</b> .. stock-stations, shepherds' huts, squatters' homesteads. ... . <i>(1863,Handbook to Canterbury,5</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Every [Canterbury] run-holder has a preemptive right over 250 acres round his homestead, and 50 acres round any other buildings he may have upon his run. <i>ibid.</i> x/149 The first question is, Where shall you place your homestead? <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,viii/115</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... most stations have a bush near to the homestead, or greater facilities for draying than we possess. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,62,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> He was one of the owners of Tarndale, the largest cattle-station in the country, and the next day i passed the homestead, and saw a party of his hands preparing for a mustering expedition. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,85</i>)

>  A man who was engaged `rabbiting' on the run, had camped on the [240] Maruroa Flat, not far from the homestead. <i>(1882,TrNZi,14/239</i>)

>  To facilitate the working of the station, there were three places where the hands resided. The Homestead, ... the chief one, was at the end of the station nearest civilisation. About eight miles further back was a cottage, known as the `Out-Station' ... [Further back] the Ruahine whare ... This whare was therefore the furthest back habitation ... it was erected for the accommodation of shepherds when mustering the back-blocks. <i>(Apr 1904,NZ illustrated Magazine,48</i>)

>  <b>MANSFiELD</b> Here we drive in and ask for a paddock [sic no stop] Past the shearing shed - past the homestead to a beautiful place with a little patch of bush - tuis - magpies -cattle - <i>(1907,Urewera Notebook,51,1978 Gordon ed.</i>)

>  <i>homestead n.</i> centre of management on sheep or cattle station. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> Barbara ... dawdled across the horse paddock by the homestead heading for the dusty, noisy, drafting yards. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,1</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> [cp station] The homestead is situated on the banks of the Wilberforce River at the foot of a bush-clad mountain after which the station [Mount Algidus] is named. ... Mount Oakden. Oakden homestead is Mount Algidus's nearest neighbour. <i>(1981,Both Sides of the River,9</i>)

>  <i>homestead ... 3. Austral. & N.Z.</i> (on a sheep or cattle station) the owner's or manager's residence; in New Zealand, the term includes all outbuildings. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,535</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 358; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 234]]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> After living for a time in the sod hut, the Grays built a more ambitious homestead. <i>(1985,Coldstream,9</i>)

>  <b>SLiGO</b> He could look over his land, well enough kept what with a manager and then ... the homestead! He climbed the boundary fence. <i>(1987,Final Things,12</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> The house and outbuildings on a farm or property [cf Acland's notes (lexicographical evidence does not entirely support him):] (See [also station](../A#also-station),[station homestead](spa-sys.md#station-homestead))

>  [South Mauku] is fast becoming open country, with comfortable homesteads and paddocks of good grass. <i>(Mar 22, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

>  Fifty-two men, eight women, and twenty-five children are on the ground, the men doing the preparatory work, cutting roads through the forest, felling bush for burning, planting orchards, &c., getting ready for starting the permanent homestead. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,243</i>)

>  <b>42</b> There are two woolsheds on the station: one at the homestead and the other at the outstation, where shearing commenced this season with the wether flock. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,1935</i>)

>  <b>PRiCE</b> As an out-station is to the homestead of a sheep run so is this little fort to Te Teko. <i>(1891,Through the Uriwera [sic] Country,61</i>)

>  it [sc. gum-digging territory] is a wild country. There are no promenades. Settlers usually stay on their own homesteads. <i>(Nov 13, 1909,NZ Truth,229/7/5</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> The station [c1859], or homestead, stood on a plateau some fifty feet above the plain; it consisted of two huts, mud-walled and thatched with snow grass. One of these contained the general kitchen ..., the other was the residence of the squatter and his overseer <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,18</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> The custom in the old days was to call the buildings on a run `the station,' and the land that went with it was `the run.' On a big property you could find several `stations,' and the principal one was called `the home station,' while the others were `out-stations.' This was the old Australian usage which was carried over to New Zealand at first, but gradually the signification was altered by loosely calling the whole run a station, and then the real station became a homestead and the out-stations became shepherds' huts. in this present book it will be noticed that generally the writer has preserved the olden custom of calling the main buildings the station and the land the run. The runs over in Australia being, as a rule, much larger than those in New Zealand, the `station' and `out-station' phraseology was preserved much longer than over here, where even in advertisements in the late 'fifties we see the English word `homestead' ousting the colonial word `station.' <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,11</i>)

>  <b>SWAiNSON</b> Surrounded by these ornamental woods, ... are here and there clear open spots of ground ... choice sites for homestead, park or garden. <i>(1853,Auckland,38</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> At first the homestead was just sitting in a bare paddock or `burn'. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,25</i>)

>  it was a wing of the nikau "homestead" of Alf. Woodham. <i>(Sep 21, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,12</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> They returned to the Homestead only when it was necessary to get fresh stores. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,103</i>) [NOTES: Pagination from 1954 edition]

>  He [sc. Mr Buchanan] remembered walking along a track made by wild pigs through tall fern and flax to the site of the present homestead. <i>(1982,Blue Cliffs,7</i>)

>  Before the oats were finished, the tents were wanted, so we "struck" them and shifted into the homestead. <i>(Aug 21, 1890,Otago Witness,11</i>)

>  Wonderful old world craftsmanship is stamped throughout one of only a handful of surviving sheep station homesteads. <i>(Sep 15, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C10</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> 250 acres - a block of land 50 chains square to include homestead, woolshed, fenced land, etc. <i>(1982,Blue Cliffs,5</i>) [NOTES: Quotes Poingdestre (1857)]

>  <b>PASCOE</b> They spend a night at a homestead at lake Heron with a sheeprun manager who took their gear on a pack-horse the following morning. <i>(1958,Great Days in NZ Mountaineering,68</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> The squatter chose a place for his homestead, pitched a tent, built a temporary scrub yard to hold the sheep at night until they were used to the country. <i>(1946,Early Canterbury Runs,249</i>)

>  No portion of the land occupied by a holder of a pasturage license whereon a homestead shall have been erected or improvements made shall be offered for sale until the offer of purchasing such land shall have been made to such license-holder at a price per acre assessed within the limits of the upset price of the class of land to which the land in particular may belong. [Marginal note: Homestead to be offered to license holder previously to sale]. <i>(1863,NZ Statutes,27 Vict. 39/157</i>) [NOTES: [Nelson Waste Lands Act, 1863]]

>  it shall be lawful for the Board to permit the original holder of a pastoral license.. to apply for and purchase in one block.. an allotment of land not exceeding three hundred and twenty acres on which to erect buildings, yards, or such other improvements as may be necessary for working the run. [Marginal note: Licensee may purchase land on run for homestead]. <i>(1877,NZ Statutes,41 Vict. 29/185</i>) [NOTES: [Land Act, 1877]]

>  Wherever a lessee or licensee has erected a homestead on the land included in his lease or license, he shall have the right, with the consent of the Board, to select and occupy, during the currency ofhis lease or license, an area of not exceeding in the whole one hundred and fifty acres adjacent to such homestead, which shall be exempt from the right of determination as provided in section onehundred and seventy. <i>(1885,NZ Statutes,49 Vict. 56/294</i>) [NOTES: [Land Act, 1885]]

>  Where a lessee or licensee of a run exceeding five thousand acres in area has erected a homestead on the land included in his lease or license, he shall have the right, with the consent of the Board, to select and occupy, during the currency of his lease or license, an area of not exceeding in the whole one hundred and fifty acres adjacent to such homestead, which shall be exempt from the right of determination as provided in section onehundred and ninety-two. <i>(1892,NZ Statutes,56 Vict. 37/258</i>) [NOTES: [Land Act, 1892]]

>  The Minister may from time to time direct the Board to negotiate with any owner of private lands.. for the purchase or exchange of any lands.. For the acquisition of sites for homesteads for neighbouring high-lying pastoral land. <i>(1894,NZ Statutes,58 Vict. 37/176</i>)

>  Private land may.. be acquired on behalf of Her Majesty under this Act for any of the following purposes.. Providing land as sites for homesteads for neighbouring pastoral land. <i>(1900,NZ Statutes,64 Vict. 52/443</i>) [NOTES: [Land for Settlements Consolidation Act, 1900]]

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> At first the homestead was just sitting in a bare paddock or "burn".<i> ibid. </i> 104 The "homestead" was on the Te Akau line, which meant a toll call if we wished any information from that quarter. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,25</i>)

>  Single-handed built a homestead, just to make a start in life. <i>(May 24, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,iV/44 : 181</i>)

>  Homestead. in the old days the owner's residence, but only if it was some distance from the rest of the station buildings (see big house). when the Goverment began cutting up the runs in the 'nineties, they or their surveyors adopted the word to signify what had been formerly called the station, and the new settlers followed them, so that the new sense of the word is now widely used. Butler and Sir F. Weld also use it in the modern sense, but in writing for Englishmen. (C.R.) <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The,17</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  Sheep or cattle owners, who, establishing their temporary homesteads or stations, near or in the bush, might run their flocks and herds amongst the hills. <i>(1849,Handbook to Suburban and Rural Dists. Otago,6</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> Farmers and stock-keepers, however, who have their homesteads on the plain. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ, The,263</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDSON</b> There are equitable arrangements for the purchase or sale of homesteads built on such runs if not exceeding 80 acres. <i>(1854,Summer's Excursion,27</i>)

>  <b>FiTTON</b> [cf station] A dairy is ... a more interesting, and, generally, a more prettily situated homestead than a sheep station, which ought not to be placed ... where there is low underwood to catch the fleeces of the sheep ... <i>ibid.</i> 270 On first arriving at any station or homestead .. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),237</i>)

>  <b>McLEAN</b> He now presses his claim to his Homestead. <i>(1858,Papers,XV/156 [??2.],1839 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> [?the previously described home station, where the manager lives and the ancillary buildings are] i am glad we have chosen to build our house here instead of at the homestead two miles off; for i like to be removed from the immediate neighbourhood of all the work of the station, especially from that of the `gallows,' - a high wooden frame from which the carcases of the butchered sheep dangle; ... <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,64,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PRENTiCE</b> [Refers to 1850's; Otago] Add to [the Whata] a small brush sheep-pen and you have the homestead or station complete. <i>(1870,Tale of NZ,29</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [2x attrib (- gardens, - days)]

>  it will also open up another "homestead" block. <i>(Mar 15, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

>  The homestead settler earns nothing for the first two years. <i>(Mar 5, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  i expected to find both of the Chatham island tree daisies, Olearia chathamica and O. semidentata, growing in homestead gardens. <i>(Aug 1991,NZ Gardener,29</i>)

>  <b>HiSLOP</b> Travellers are coming and going as in the old homestead days, and very seldom does one pass without a cup of tea and many a time a meal. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,129</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to be a tenant on (part of) the land of a large sheep or cattle station.

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> [a landowner] i have ... quite a number of my men ... . homesteading at a merely nominal rent. <i>(1926,Lenore Divine,160</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the residence of the owner or manager of a large farm or station. (orig. station house.)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> This homestead much resembles a small English farm-house, save the sleeping loft had seldom fewer than ten occupants. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,70</i>)

>  Every run-holder has a pre-emptive right over 250 acres round his homestead, and 50 acres round any other buildings he may have upon his run. <i>(1860,BLANK ENTRY,95</i>) [NOTES: in BUTLER First Year (1863)]

>  <b>BARKER</b> Most stations have a bush near the homestead. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,62</i>) [NOTES: (1870)]

>  <b>THORNTON</b> At first the term 'big house' was used to denote the owner's residence on a station and the homestead referred to his residence if it was sited some distance from other farm buildings. <i>(1986,NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings,22</i>)

>  ... picturesque crags and wooded gullies, among which Messrs. Rhodes's homestead and farm-buildings are perched, - ... in all cases i believe the squatter is allowed compensation for all improvements he may have made in a homestead on his run. <i>(Jan 1, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> This homestead much resembles a small English farm-house save that the sleeping loft had seldom fewer than ten occupants. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,70</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> On that morning the open country stretched away in vast fields of fern, and Nature reigned supreme. it is fern-clad now no longer, but green fields gladden the eye; the white gleam of the farmer's homestead dots the landscape, there are villas on the height, and cottages on the shore. <i>(1881,Poenamo,2/5/99</i>)

>  [on Station] But a glimmer of light from the homestead tells of one whose rest is broken,/ <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,77</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> in this high country he met some of the original selectors - men who had pushed out into the unknown in bullock drays and had seen tent give way to mud or stone hut, and that replaced by the long, low wooden homestead, with its deep verandah, its big, comfortably furnished rooms ... <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,261</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> The station buildings here may have been useful but they were not ornamental, the homestead being a hut, with a rough and ready woolshed nearby. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,116</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> She was a member of the third generation of a famous pioneer family, and lived ... in a large and prosperous white homestead near the town. The homestead ... was often visited by those who wished to find an authentic colonial atmosphere. <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,18,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> if you show an experienced dog a butcher's knife ... he will ... round up a mob of killers (sheep to be used as mutton for the homestead) without any further word of command. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,68</i>)

>  <i>homestead n. </i> owner's residence on a sheep or cattle station. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Here and there a sheepfarmer's homestead, a word commoner in New Zealand than in England, will be surrounded by trees. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,171</i>)

>  <b>GRUNDY</b> The homestead was a lovely old building with an enormous, well equipped kitchen and pleasant tastefully furnished living rooms. <i>(1968,Who'd Marry a Doctor?,113</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> The owner's, or main house on a station or farm (as distinct from other out-buildings or employees' house)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> <i>(1940,Te Waimate,103,1954 ed.</i>)

>  ~ The main house on a farm, or on a sheep or cattle station. - <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),199,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,73</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> . a small rural land-holding under the land acts 1832 2. comb. -block 1890; -farm 1897; -selectio, settler etc.] ?Following the US usage.

>  <b>FiTTON</b> ... you could purchase a homestead, and employ a man and a boy to farm and look after a few cattle at home; ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),183</i>) [NOTES: 1855 March Letter in 1856 Fitton NZ 183]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> This [Wairarapa] is one of the finest pastoral districts in New Zealand, now exhibiting several little village-towns, and the homesteads and stock-stations of numbers of thriving settlers, rich in sheep and cattle. <i>(1866,Polynesia,111</i>)

>  <b>MacLEOD</b> When Robert Mackay had spent five years at Redcliff he was made manager of the whole Double Hill Station, and the family moved to a homestead further up the Gorge. <i>(1955,Voice on the Wind,21</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> My father ... saved enough to buy a homestead of twenty-nine acres of unimproved Crown land. <i>(1961,North Cape to Bluff,107</i>)

### Homestead Act
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  i am quite convinced that the Homestead Act is a very practicable and wise measure. <i>(Mar 5, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an early Land Grant law where permanent dwellings had to be erected within twelve months of land occupancy.

>  <b>KEENE</b> in the 1870s the Homestead Act provided for free grants of 50 acres of first-class land or 75 acres of second-class land, plus certain acreage to children, conditioned on the cost of surveys being paid and on specified improvements being done within a certain number of years. <i>(1977,To the Northward,128</i>)

### homestead block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a section of a farm or station in which a homestead is located [formerly known as a station.]

>  A fine shed was built on the homestead block, with two boards and eight shearers on each board and the shed itself held 1000 sheep. <i>(Sep 1, 1948,NZ Farmer,7</i>)

>  The milking shed is on the homestead block. <i>(Mar 1, 1955,NZ Dairy Exporter,7</i>)

>  <b>ELLiSON</b> We went down to visit our neighbours, the Gills, who had a homestead block eleven miles away. <i>(1956,Whirinaki Valley, The,46</i>)

>  The homestead block consists of 171.75 ha ... <i>(Mar 22, 2002,Hawke's Bay Property Guide,2</i>)

>  it will also open up another "homestead" block. <i>(Mar 15, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

>  The homestead settler earns nothing for the first two years. <i>(Mar 5, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  [Regulations] legalized at that time [1856], allowed land outside the original Otago Block to be bought for grazing-runs at a low cost. (Runholders were allowed 80 acres for the homestead block with 10 acres for each outstation.) <i>(1948,Our Own Country,135</i>) [NOTES: 1948 Our Own Country (from Korero, AEWS magazine) 135 (`Central Otago')]

>  <b>SCOTTER</b> Edward Menlove died in 1903. His homestead block of 1,241 acres was then entered in the roll under the name of his son-in-law ... <i>(1948,Run, Estate and Farm,65</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> On his homestead block of 8 acres he erected a family residence, ... <i>(1984,City of the Plains,28</i>)

### homestead paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a paddock surrounding, or in close proximity to the homestead compound on a station. [incl homestead horse paddock]

>  A homestead paddock encloses over an acre. <i>(Oct 1891,NZ Farmer,418</i>)

>  <b>MACiNTOSH</b> One of my favourite shooting places was to climb one of the big cabbage trees on the sunny slope behind the homestead paddock. <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,122</i>)

>  <b>GiBSON</b> He was there for four to five months and spent most of his time round the homestead paddocks with the stud shepherd ... <i>(1990,Among the High Hills of Canterbury,18</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> Merinos were run on the higher country, half breds on the lower country, and the stud sheep ran in the homestead paddocks. <i>(1982,Blue Cliffs,43</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> The girls skirted the plantation as far as the gate and soon entered the homestead horse paddock. <i>ibid.,</i> 101 Barbara elected to catch the hacks for the three homestead riders. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,11</i>)

### homestead settler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The homestead settler earns nothing for the first two years. <i>(Mar 5, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  Here we met Mr Berney, another of the homestead settlers, who invited us to partake of his hospitality which we did, and left at his place part of our provision. <i>(May 18, 1888,Clutha Leader,6</i>)

### homestead system
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The "homestead" system means a peculiar mode of dealing with lands open for selection without payment, but subject to provisions for cultivation and residence. <i>(Mar 15, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,13</i>)

>  HOMESTEAD SYSTEM. [heading] Under this system the settler makes no payment for the land, the only cost to him being the expenses of survey. On the fulfilment of conditions, which are five years' residence, the erection of a house, and the cultivation of one-third of the selection if open land, and one-fifth if bush-land, the Crown grant is issued. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,284</i>)

### homesteader
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  i met one settler amongst these homesteaders who had previously been employed as a manager. <i>(Mar 5, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  How delightful - very - the health and freedom of a country life, where the happy homesteaders breathe the pure air of heaven heated and thickened to a degree sufficient to crack the leather lungs of a blacksmith's bellows. <i>(Mar 5, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  <b>McCASKiLL</b> The Jack Fitch mentioned in the story has been identified as a `homesteader' on Tarndale [Station] in 1876. <i>(1969,Molesworth,45</i>) [NOTES: 1876]

### homestroke
 
 <i>n.</i> Esp. suffer a homestroke, a breakage of china, a broken cup, etc. [ca1930s p.c. M.P. Monaghan 1988 Jan 2 Used in her family]

### homette
 
 <i>n.</i> [(? nonce, or trade)]

>  More accommodation in the shape of houses, homettes and caravans was provided by the [rabbit] boards. <i>(Mar 6, 1958,NZ Farmer,4</i>)

### homeward bounder
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MAY</b> it was a matter of nice judgement. The digger recognized four classes of payable ground: `tucker ground' kept him in food with nothing to spare; a `wages claim' gave him a pound or two above living expenses; a `riser' was a rich claim; and a `piler' or `homeward bounder' the richest of all. Probably most of the `pilers' were beach-claims, where the leads were shallow and rich, the outlay small and provisions cheap because of easy access. <i>ibid.</i> 275 The richest claim was always a `homeward-bounder'. A Nelson digger recalled how at work his mates talked of `trips to Europe, going back for a week, and astonishing the Natives generally'. <i>ibid.</i> 329 J. F. Byrne & Co. showed off their wares by the light of six kerosene chandeliers and advertised, THE REAL HOMEWARD BOUNDER!/An ounce to the Dish can be Saved/ BY BUYiNG YOUR/CLOTHiNG AND BOOTS AT THE CORiNTHiAN HALL/ NEW RUSH NEW RUSH /Peg out your claims at the Corinthian Hall if you want to strike a good lead/Dead on the gutter/ BUY YOUR CLOTHiNG AND BOOTS AN THE CORiNTHiAN HALL/ <i>(1962,West Coast Gold Rushes,244,1967 ed.</i>)

### homeward-bound
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> She drove and rode well; but she sewed with `homeward bound stitch', as sailors say; there was neither newness nor permanence about it. <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,18</i>)

### homeware
 
 <i>n.</i> household goods

>  [A Napier correspondent]objects to shopkeeper's increasing use of the word "homewares". if we are going to use this new word we should at least refer to homeware in the singular, on the analogy of hardware, software and whiteware. These never become hardwares, softwares and whitewares. <i>(Nov 27, 2003,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

### Homey/Homie
 
 <i>n.</i> an English person or Briton; a person from home; a person (poss. became obsolesc. as Pommy became more popular) with a non-NZ, British accent. [n (obsolesc) an old word (informal, often capitca1950 Used frequy by immigrants to refer to themselves and by polite NZders (eg a 2ZB Woman's panel) and perhaps felt as a familiar or euphemistic replacement for POMMY. --1978 July 33 Mr George Gosling 78 years old letter<i>[Pommy</i> is] a late Australian import. ... In my youth [ca1920] the term for the non-native born was Homey.]

>  Every Home boat that comes in they say "More Homies! More of your friends!" <i>(Dec 7, 1927,NZ Tablet,58</i>) [NOTES: n (obsolesc) an old word (informal, often capitca1950 Used frequy by immigrants to refer to themselves and by polite NZders (eg a 2ZB Woman's panel) and perhaps felt as a familiar or euphemistic replacement for POMMY. -- 1978 July 33 Mr George Gosling 78 years old letter <i>[Pommy</i> is] a late Australian import. ... In my youth [ca1920] the term for the non-native born was Homey.]

>  i have never felt any hostility towards any "homie" or "pommie" until recently. <i>(Apr 8, 1953,NZ Truth,19</i>)

>  <b>MUiR</b> `Some of you jokers could do with a stir-up,' Fred said in his slow, part-Midlands, part-Kiwi accent. He knew the men still regarded him as a Homey, though it was well over thirty years since he'd left the Old Country. <i>(1960,Word for Word,181</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> But they call me Fritzie! Next they call me Dutchie or Polie or Homie, they will say i haf no right to be in - in - Himmel, which God's Own Country am i in? <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,99</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> is it any wonder that for many years colonists would call Britain `home', whereas now the native born call new arrivals `homies'? <i>(1964,Shining with the Shiner,17</i>)

>  <i>homey n.</i> Englishman. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  <b>GiLDERDALE</b> <i>homie </i>Recent arrival from the U.K. Throughout T.T. Garland's Old Joe (1937). <i>(1982,Sea Change,241</i>)

>  <b>O'SULLiVAN</b> TiNY: A Pom? JACKO: Yeah. A homey. <i>(1985,Shuriken,23</i>)

>  <i>homey 1 n. NZ & Austral.</i> native of Great Britain, esp. as newly-arrived immigrant. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,358</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>OWEN & PERKiNS</b> (Bob Edwards, bushman) [The immigrants] were `Homeys' - the word `Pommy' wasn't in use then [early 1930s depression], and [79] we called them `Homeys', and this was used in a derogatory manner ... as if it was something contemptible. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,78</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> <i>homey</i> dismissive name for a person, usually a migrant, who nostalgically looks to Britain as home. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,57</i>)

>  (Meikle) inferior English residents, called `Homies' more often than `Pommies', spoke in a variety of non-U accents and occupied a variety of non-U jobs [in Tauranga in the 1930s]. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,130</i>)

>  He was a "Homie", and had got work as a "chainman" with a survey party near us. <i>(Aug 27, 1927,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,48</i>)

>  i am a "Homie". i came from London. <i>(Jul 11, 1928,NZ Free Lance,7</i>)

>  <b>CADEY</b> Another homey. <i>(1933,Broken Pattern,84</i>)

>  <b>iRELAND</b> 'i'm as good as broke and i'd just come all the way back from England; and what's the first thing that happens?' my father says to me years later. 'A homey comes up and puts the bite on me.' <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,41</i>)

>  <b>GiFKiN</b> inferior English residents called 'Homies' more often than 'Pommies', spoke in a variety of non-U accents and occupied a variety of non-U jobs. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,130</i>)

>  <b>Lee</b> i have been referring to a Homie attitude. is New Zealand to stand on its own feet or become a dependency of Britain? <i>(Dec 10, 1937,Diary,50,1981 ed.</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKiNS</b> We got into this hullabaloo with some poor damned immigrants. What system, they were brought out under, i don't know, but they were 'Homeys' - the word 'Pommy' wasn't in use then. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,78</i>)

>  The 'tecs were down looking for a "homey". <i>(May 26, 1928,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,51</i>)

>  inferior English residents, called "Homies" more often than "Pommies", spoke in a variety of non-U accents and occupied a variety of non-U jobs. <i>(Aug 1994,Quote Unquote,16</i>)

>  in those days we called UK migrants "homies"; "Poms" came after World War ii. <i>(Sep 1995,Metro,114</i>)

>  <b>FAiRBURN</b> He is a `homey' who came out to Australia, buggered up a grocer's shop, and came on to Norfolk. <i>(Dec 1926,Letters,15,1981 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> The crowd at the house were mostly `homies' <i>(1927,Old Savage,170</i>)

>  <b>DRESCHFELT</b> Baker's got a couple of `homeys' with him. <i>(1933,Broken Pattern,130</i>)

>  <b>ADAiR</b> .. chaffing each other about `Colonials' and `homies'. <i>(1935,Wanted A Son,171</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> They [English immigrants] loathed the New Zealanders, who called them Homies. <i>ibid.</i> 155 We don't call them green-horns out here. We call them new chums, or else Homies. <i>ibid.</i> 164 `if you're in any way different, in this country, ... you're made to feel such a fool. ... They call us Homies.' `A lot of people called the New Zealanders Pig islanders, and the Australians the Aussies. <i>ibid.</i> 165 ... don't mind if you're called a Homie. After all, what does it mean? Somebody from Home. A bit sissy, but it could be worse. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,118</i>)

>  <b>BE.LTON</b> You're a dirty low homie and you work for scab wages. <i>(1939,Outside the Law,45</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> [cp Pommy] Mr Massey, however, gave his name to the army boot. `Homie' and `Pommy' are, i believe Australian [120] words, and so is `cocky', in the sense of small farmer, a term that is coming into general use, and some day will be recognised even in the schools. <i>(1939,First With the Sun,119</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> We may remark on the slang use of homey for an Englishman <i>(1941,NZ Slang,43</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> A variant [of Pommy] is Homey (Great Britain being `Home'), though in Australia this connotes `homely'. <i>(1943,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,88</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> But this homey Simmons was a loudmouth with a big opinion of himself. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,21,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>Homey</i>. 3. An Englishman: New Zealanders' and Australians': C. 20. B., 1941, 1942. Semantics as at <i>home, have gone. </i> <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1077</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> They're saying: `Look at 'em! Homies - hundreds of 'em. Another batch of ruddy Homies come to take the jobs of good pig-islanders. ...' <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,18,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DAViN</b> Being a pommy or a homey as they called it i was just as much a freak for the rest as i was for the irish. <i>(1959,No Remittance,104</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> .. this joker's wife, she was a Homey, ... [194] ... ' `A real Pommie, eh?' <i>ibid.</i> 219-220 `They're all right really. The Pommies, i mean. The Homey's, [220] the Chooms, the Pongos.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,193</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a child from a home or orphanage

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

### honeypot
 
 <i>n.</i> a jump into water with one's hands clasped around knees, landing noisily on one's backside. [(children's, esp. girls) ca1935 (Havelock)]

>  <b>Anderson</b> This time he does a honeypot, skiting i bet. He goes back on the grass and runs fast across the rock so's not to slip, then jumps as far as he can into the pond with his arms tight round his knees and shouts 'Yeh!', then comes up spluttering right in the deepest part of all. <i>(1999,Long Hot Summer,46</i>)

### honeysuckle
 
 <i>n.</i> [[check Maori].]

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Knightia excelsa (Maori Honey-suckle)</i>... Maori name, <i>Rewa-rewa.</i> it is sometimes called the <i>Bucket-of-water-tree, because it is so slow of combustion.</i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,150</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DUNCAN & DAViES</b> (And) <i>Knightia excelsa</i> native honey-suckle <i>(1917,Descriptive Catalogue</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Native and introduced spp.; 1. any of various climbing shrubs with small, fragrant, trumpet-shaped, yellow or pink flowers; 2. a) any of various similar but unrelated plants, such as banksia; b) in New Zealand; rewarewa. 1. <i>Knightia excelsa</i> (Morris: applied in Austr. to Banksias, the `Bottle-brushes' to which the NZ plant is related.) rewa-rewa, bucket-of-water-tree [-->[1919 Jul 29] Ver. (WB).= Vernacular names used by settlers supplied by W. Best, Otaki (And) <i>Knightia excelsa</i>]

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> A tree, called by the English here, the honeysuckle furnishes excellent wood for boat-building. <i>(1839,Journal</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> (And) <i>Knightia excelsa</i> <i>(1842,Narrative of a Nine Months Residence in NZ</i>)

>  <b>MARTiN</b> Honeysuckle, and passion-flowers, and cluster-roses hung in masses over the verandah, and here and there a tall aloe or native palm-tree rose towering up, ... <i>(1884,Our Maoris,41</i>)

>  <b>KiRK</b> [Native name] Rewarewa .. [Settlers' name] Honeysuckle .. [Family] Proteaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  1889 Canterbury [Catalogue Canterbury Court, Exhibition) 35 <i>(1889,Catalogue NZ Exhibition,35</i>)

>  Rewarewa (honey-suckle) .. Knightia excelsa .. Soft wood, straight grained, prettily mottled, and used largely for ornamental purposes of all descriptions <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,14</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>K[nightia] excelsa, R.Br. ... Honeysuckle; Rewarewa. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,606</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> A tree sometimes reaching the height of 100ft. ... found only in the North island and on the southern shores of Cook Strait ...
