### Hongi
 
 <i>n.</i> (offensive, obsolete) a Maori. Usu. in pl. (the Hongis); prob. from Hongi Hika, name of an early Maori chief well known from school history books; poss. chiefly a Si term, as unlike Hori, it is not used by Maoris among themselves. [ORIGIN: Maori, ca1949 common use in Havelock, and on the West Coast and also as an attrib: eg. `That hongi kid.']

### hongi
 
 <i>v.</i> to press noses in greeting [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Powhiri i had seen but i'd never hongied so many people in one go! <i>(Jun 2003,Wel-com (Wellington),3</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> They both stood and the three of them hongi'd, the old women's skin like rice paper... <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,62</i>)

>  Then all shook hands and hongied (rubbed noses) and so concluded a very interesting meeting. <i>(Jan 31, 1914,Hawera & Normanby Star,4</i>)

>  <b>T.S.GRACE</b> ... they should shake hands and `hongi' (press noses) in the presence of all, be good friends and neighbours, ... <i>(Dec 29, 1853,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Dec 29 T.S.Grace letter in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 35]

>  As each arrives, she goes through the bus, shaking hands and hongi-ing everyone. There is a set patttern for the hongi - clasp hands, briefly press together the tips of the noses, make a wailing sound, press noses briefly for a second time ... Close relatives hongi for a longer period than the formal brief nose-press. <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/97</i>) [NOTES: (Beaglehole)]

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> hongi to greet by pressing the noses together <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,xxii</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... Haoratipa's wife went and brought Hineato out of the bush and she hongied with all her Tapapakanga friends ... <i>(1948,Taina,111</i>)

>  Today he `hongi-ed' (pressed noses) with all those at the marae and looked at the new meeting house being built.... <i>(Jul 1, 1983,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> He comes across and hongis. <i>ibid.</i> 327 Everything has been packed away now ... Two stretchers for sleeping on (Piri brought them one morning: he said very little, but hongied with her for the first time); ... <i>(1983,Bone People, The,120</i>)

>  Paul Temm feels essentially unchanged by his experience on the tribunal, sitting at marae around the country, listening to the elders vent their frustrations in Maori, hongi-ing with both tangata whenua and manuhiri (visitors), chatting over tea and kai. <i>(Jun 21, 1986,Evening Post</i>)

>  if we can hongi a bro, then hongi a Pakeha as well, no worries. <i>(Jun 6, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),17</i>)

>  The Tainui elder said they had just hongied when Hineware Harawira (34) came up beside Mr Curtis and punched him in the head. <i>(Oct 9, 1988,Sunday Star (Auckland),A1`</i>)

>  [Hdg] We didn't come to hongi Maori. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,11/38</i>)

>  Parekura Horomia hongis Regan Sword, from St Brigid's School, Johnsonville, during the launch of draft guidelines for Maori language in school's mainstream curriculum. <i>(Mar 22, 2007,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  Some of the singers hongi with the host families and dignitaries on the marae - a warm and emotional moment. <i>(Dec 24, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),118</i>)

>  "What sort if message does it send to victims in this country when a political leader is happily hongi-ing with someone who is a beneficiary of the Bail Act, out on bail though he is facing some very serious charges? <i>(Feb 14, 2008,Dominion Post,A2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori greeting by pressing noses [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  i was eager to rant about people selling hongis. <i>(Sep 26, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C5</i>)

>  "i was the first person on earth to do a hongi with the US President!" <i>(Sep 26, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C6</i>)

>  it needs kaumatua clearance because of the spiritual significance of the hongi. <i>(Sep 21, 1999,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  Players performed hongi with the school group before leaving. <i>(Jan 31, 2006,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  Historically, Te Puia guides greet visitors with a hongi but the practice was stopped about two years ago during the Sars crisis. <i>(Dec 7, 2005,NZ Herald,A8</i>)

>  The "hongi" caused a large-sized diversion. <i>(Oct 11, 1913,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  <b>ihimaera</b> Tane took [Hineahuone] in his arms, pressed his nose to hers in the first hongi, shared his godly breath with her, and she passed from inanimate to animate being. <i>(2007,Ask the Posts,47</i>)

>  There was heard.. the characteristic waiata (song of lament). <i>(1900,Auckland Weekly News,Xmas No. / 12</i>)

>  Speaker Margaret Wilson ruled that the minister had no responsibility for the hongi [between Tama iti and John Key\}. <i>(Feb 14, 2008,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  <b>KiNG</b> The old Chief who appeared to be about seventy years of age, had not a visible feature, the whole of his face being covered with spiral lines; on his coming on board he embraced Tooke with great affection; after which Tooke introduced me to him, and after the ceremony of Ehonge (i.e., joining noses) he took off his how, how, [sic] or mantle, and put it on my shoulders; ... <i>(Nov 1793,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1793 Nov King's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/547]

>  <b>KiNG</b> Tookee then introduced him to me, and after the ceremony of etrouge (joining noses) he took off his ha-a-how ... and put it on my shoulders. in return, i made him a present of a ha-a-how made of green baize decorated with broad arrows. <i>(Nov 19, 1793,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1793 Nov 19 Lieut.-Gov. King to Henry Dundas in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/171-172]

>  <b>COLLiNS</b> E-hon-gi, The ceremony of joining noses as a salute. <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,558</i>)

>  <b>LEE \& KENDALL</b> O'ngi, s. Salute, (performed by touching noses); <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,142</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The ongi, or native salute of touching noses ... immediately proved they were of New Zealand origin. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/47</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Each presentation was accompanied by the pressing of noses (e'ongi) and expressions of `Friend, welcome; how are you all,' &c. `Ekoro airamai tenarakokoe[a2-acute],' in a whining tone, as if each were about to condole for the loss of their entire generation. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/85</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> Were these missionaries, who shook hands and gave the hongi to my people, and then put them to death? <i>(Feb 20, 1841,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Feb 20 E.J. Wakefield Journal in 1842 Heaphy Res. in NZ 58]

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> Observing his embarrassment, we withdrew to some distance, leaving him to indulge his natural feeling in hongi and tangi, or nose-rubbing and crying. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/61</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> ... the cordial salutation of hungi, or `pressing noses', took place; ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/21</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> At meeting men and women pressed their noses together ... This salutation is called hongi, and is defined as a smelling ... During the hongi the lips never met, there was no kissing. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/200</i>)

>  <b>T.S. GRACE</b> `Where?' (his back being towards me), and immediately jumping up he hongied (touched noses) with me, and saluted me as `the face of Te Heuheu.' <i>(Aug 1877,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1877 Aug T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 264]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The Maori salutation (hongi) was to press the nose against the nose of the person greeted; sometimes with a low crooning song of welcome or lament. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,142</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> `Ho!' i cried to myself, `i will give her the `hongi' (nose rub).' <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,250</i>)

>  <b>SHAND</b> After the recital of this, they then saluted by rubbing noses (hongi), as with the Maori, and wailed over their losses. <i>(1911,Moriori People of the Chatham islands, The,5</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> They shook hands with the Captain who met them at the top of the gangway and greeted ... Wi Tako with a courteous hongi. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,117</i>)

>  As each arrives, she goes through the bus, shaking hands and hongi-ing everyone. There is a set patttern for the hongi - clasp hands, briefly press together the tips of the noses, make a wailing sound, press noses briefly for a second time ... Close relatives hongi for a longer period than the formal brief nose-press. <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/97</i>) [NOTES: (Beaglehole)]

>  hongi, salutation by pressing (not rubbing) noses: also means `to smell.' <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/151</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  hongi: salutation, nose-pressing. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> On the day appointed hundreds of Maoris ... assembled at the tapu plot, and, with much hongi and wailing, set about digging. <i>(1951,Confessions,38</i>)

>  hongi The Maori salute commonly called `rubbing noses'. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),200,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> SOME MAORi WORDS ... hongi pressing noses in greeting <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,192</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> SOME MAORi WORDS ... hongi pressing noses in greeting <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,192</i>)

>  hongi n. Maori term for rubbing noses as a greeting. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  hongi ... n. Maori term for rubbing noses as a greeting. [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  <b>ROSiER-JONES</b> Maude turns to Tui. `How are you?' `Kapai. Ke te pai.' Tui presses noses with the old lady, then Maude. ... `Whenever i'm out here in the wop-wops with youse jokers, the hori comes out in me. Kapai and hongi..' <i>(1985,Cast Two Shadows,88</i>)

>  hongi n. NZ the pressing of noses together as a greeting. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,359</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 235]]

>  Sir Graham has a hongi with Mr Alan Napier, a member of the Wellington Mongrel Mob who works at Tapu Te Ranga. <i>(Jul 1, 1983,Evening Post,14</i>)

### hongi-ing
 
 <i>n.</i> doing hongi

>  <b>A5</b> Hand-shaking and hugging should be avoided in a flu pandemic, the guide says. (Kissing and hongi-ing are not mentioned but can be assumed to be risky.) <i>(Oct 21, 2005,NZ Herald</i>)

### honking
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The name Haggas Honking Holes is a combination of the speed at which cavers abseil down into the caves (honking) and the Haggas farm the caves lie under. <i>(Dec 19, 1992,Evening Post,1</i>)

### hono
 
 <i>n.</i> a blessing [ORIGIN: Maori, Ryan: a prayer to mend limbs]

>  His grandfather, who grew up in Huntly "with full capacity" until he was well over 100, was a healer who would perform hono - a blessing - over local sportsmen's injuries. <i>(Oct 6, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

### honour fee
 
 <i>n.</i> [called a "dishonour fee" in the UK]

>  One of the most initially shocking [fees] is the honour fee introduced by ANZ in June. The way it works, on certain accounts, is that for every day the account is improperly overdrawn - i.e. if you haven't previously arranged an overdraft facility - the first two transactions that day each attract a $15 penalty. ... "Fundamentally, " Shaw points out, "there's no reason for a customer to incur an honour fee. An overdraft facility costs you less a year [$28] than two honour fees." <i>(Dec 4, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

### honourville
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Why don't you dribble on back to your little honourville and talk symbolism and underlying meaning with all your proud-ass mates who aren't doing a single thing that is actually visibly, touchably worthwhile. <i>(Dec 16, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

### hoo
 
 <i>n.</i> a bend or a kink [common use in Taranaki in 1940s (DEH)]

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hoo a bend or a kink (rhymes with `shoe'); eg `That track's got a hoo in it and a sheer drop, so watch out for it, just round the bend ahead.' South island usage. [G. Johnston] <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,46</i>)

### hoo-boy
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  The marketing and imaging of rugby league's Auckland Warriors, for instance, has been relentlessly macho, from the simulated war games before the first game last year against the Broncos.. through to the chanting hoo-boy warriors of the TV ads. <i>(Sep 14, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),25</i>)

### hooch
 
 <i>n.</i> marijuana [Info from Chris Fowlie]

>  On the other hand, cannabis may have been smoked as well... Certainly no one denies that he smoked a great deal of hooch, as they call it in those parts. <i>(Jan 15, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [hunting] a makeshift shelter. [in ODE and SOED (perhaps from Japanese uchi a dwelling)]

>  <b>BURDON</b> Started the construction of a hooch - punga fronds for the roof and walls and on the ground for bedding. <i>(1996,Hunting for DOC,17</i>)

### hoochy weed
 
 <i>n.</i> marijuana

>  `He's [a drunk driver] probably been smoking hoochy weed too. i might just have a shifty inside that car,' says Duncan [a traffic cop]. <i>(Sep 1986,Metro,75</i>)

### hoodacky
 
 <i>n.</i> A thingamebob; one of the many variants or re-formations of and all heard from (mainly primary) school children from ca1940: doodacky, doohickey, hoodle-lacky, hoojacky, hoojah, hoojit, howdzacky, etc. [(mainly children's) ca1941 (St Patrick's, Silverstream) ca1944 Les Cleveland]

>  There's hoodlums, hoodackys and hoodunnits Funny money, down the dunny, join the club The guy with dry horrors - drowning his sorrows little Johnny, Little horror, the Little grub. <i>(Dec 2004,NZ Memories,p18</i>)

>  `My oath,' i said. `Many a Jap binjie is going to get a hoodickey stuck in it before sparrow-hiccough. ...' <i>(1945,Sixes & Sevens,7</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hoodackie thingummygig; variant of `doodackie'. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,57</i>)

### hoodat
 
 <i>exclam.</i> who say hoodat, a catchphrase popular among students etc. in 1950's and early 60's, said especially as a welcome to a newcomer to a group, or outsider. Often a meaningless saying. (from who dat say who dat?, from the coloured joke about Rastus / Hori / Jacky etc, who comes home suddenly when a friend is in bed with his wife. The adulterer gets out of his bed, and tries to creep out under cover of darkness. Rastus hears him,in the dark, and says `Hoo dat?' being answered by silence, until the adulterer bumps into him in the dark, and says himself `Hoo dat?' Rastus immediately says `Hoo dat who say hoo dat'. The story has many variants, and is variously placed in stage Black, Maori or Aboriginal contexts.

### hoodickey
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [hoodacky](hom-hyp.md#hoodacky))

### hoodledum
 
 <i>n.</i> [(Christchurch and Sth Canterbury, occas. slang) ca1947 Les Cleveland A foolish, incompetent, bumbling fellow.]

### hooer
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal, derogatory) a contemptible person, difficult thing, etc. [a var. pron. of whore] [1953 16 M A34 Thames DHS 27 HOORIN (HOURIN) 1953 17 M A39 Thames DHS 27 HOORIN (HOURIN).] ca1926 Dunedin and Central Otago as an address to men etc.] (See [also hua](../A#also-hua))

>  <b>KiDMAN</b> Mrs Mawson called her a whore then. Only she said it `whoo-er' the way the children did at school, or over at the [Frankton] Junction. She said [82] Clara was `a dirty little whoo-er', the same as in a swearword, ... <i>(1983,Paddy's Puzzle,81</i>)

>  <b>MUiR</b> `About this Stevens book. i thought i'd mention it now though it's not due out for a while yet.' `is that the fat hooer?' [185] `Yes, it'll be fairly thick. Not many copies, though.' <i>(1960,Word for Word,184</i>)

>  YOUNG MAN: Come off it, i'm an ignorant hooer. <i>(1969,Solo,211</i>) [NOTES: 1969 Mason Television Script in 1981 Solo 211]

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> They were too busy haring through the bush looking for me. They pretended they actually wanted to find me, the lying whores. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,82</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> FARMER 1: ... an' we all look along at Archie, ... dozy-looking old hooer, an' he comes slowly up to Barney, and ... says ... <i>ibid.</i> 36-37 JUNE: ... Jimmy was the on'y friend he ever had. [37] On'y one! Then he had to go and clock him. Silly hooer. <i>(1974,Hand on the Rail, The,1</i>) [NOTES: (1981 TS)]

>  `What are they, James?' yells Strawberry ... `Big hooers! Ewes!' `Oh shit,' says Porridge ... `All right, you hori hooer! Send us one [frozen carcase] down for a try. ... if i get one of those big hooers up my arse, you'll get one of my size nine kadoodies up yours! Oh, i'll root you a bewdy with me size nine kadoody.' <i>(1979,islands,27/461</i>) [NOTES: McGee (Out in the Cold)]

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> `Spalpeen! Dirty little whoo-whaa! Oh, my God! The shame you've brought on your family. ... Dirty little slut! ...' <i>(1981,Fish Factory,65</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hooer/hua anybody you don't like; literally a whore; appears in Bruce Mason's The End of the Golden Weather; Scottish or mock-Scottish; NZ and elsewhere. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,57</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> like a hooer at a christening confused milling; eg `The starting gun failed to go off and left the yachts dodging each other like hooers at a christening.' [Jim Henderson] ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,69</i>)

>  Come off it. i'm an ignorant hooer. <i>(1970,End of the Golden Weather, The,26</i>) [NOTES: 2nd edition]

>  You'd call it a young man's place because there was a hooer of a lot of work in it," he says. <i>(Aug 3, 2006,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

### hooering
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>HAWES</b> Hooering great brute of a thing, they say, Feef. <i>ibid.</i> 44 They were called the Miner's Dark Gas Band because Ally Wright, who'd been a signwriter before he bought into the Miners' Dark brewery, had written this hooerin' big sign with that name on it in the corner at the Gren where the stage was. <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,21 ed.</i>)

### hoof and tooth
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to mobstock land with cattle or sheep in order to have weeds, fern etc trampled or eaten.

>  in such conditions, it may be preferable to hoof and tooth, rather than burn ... <i>(Mar 1986,Proceedings 1985 Hill & High Country Seminar,113</i>) [NOTES: 1985 Hill & High Country Seminar]

>  Glenn and Linda haven't waited for 'hoof and tooth' to do the job alone. <i>(Sep 1997,NZ Dairy Exporter,41</i>)

>  Again, we used the stock to improve the pastures - hoof and tooth. <i>(May 3, 2007,Dominion Post,C9</i>)

### hoof and tooth treatment
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) mob-stocking; when cattle or sheep are used to eat and crush weeds and fern.

>  <b>MACMiLLAN</b> Mob-stocking or 'hoof and tooth' treatment will eventually put sufficient pressure on fern and other weeds to virtually eliminate them if the paddocks are small enough in relation to the mob size. <i>(1980,Land Development in the Chatham islands,5</i>)

>  The 'hoof and tooth' treatment helps transform the old drystock pasture. <i>(Sep 1997,NZ Dairy Exporter,41</i>)

### hoof cultivation
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the use of grazing stock to aerate soil or to cultivate pasture.

>  i mentioned previously the hoof cultivation by sheep. <i>(Sep 1, 1937,NZ Farmer,11</i>)

>  <b>STAPLEDON</b> A Tour in Australia and New Zealand 55 Concentration of grazing carries with it other important effects - the benefits of what Bruce Levy has so appropriately termed 'hoof cultivation.' in New Zealand the soil dries so rapidly after rain that the 'hoof' seldom does harm and most frequently only does good - provided of course that a period of rest follows a period of excessive 'cultivation.' The combined application of 'hoof cultivation' and judicious feeding out is a method definitely employed for grass land improvement in New Zealand. <i>(1928,Tour in Australia and NZ,55</i>)

### hoof down
 
 <i>v.</i> (of food) to eat quickly, stuff down [ca1945 eg Hoof it down quickly so's I can clear the table.]

### hoof it
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to walk. [in SOED as general English]

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> i'd been hoofing it nearly non-stop since first light ... <i>(1974,Backblocks,9</i>)

>  <b>NGAWHiKA</b> i looked behind me to see my two mates hoofing it back to Perimeter Road. <i>(1996,Tell Them i've Gone Pighunting,26</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> We left the Landrover at the end of the road and hoofed it for a stand of pines a mile distant across the farmland. <i>(1997,Wild Boars of Oruaiti, The,108</i>)

>  They do not walk, they hoof it or ride their hobnails, and on horseback they ride the pigskin. <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion,C6</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> 'How are you travelling?' 'Hoofing it.' <i>(1981,Sunrise on Hikurangi,93</i>)

### hoof judging
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an assessment or eye-appraisal of stock in ring competitions.

>  in the Hawke's Bay Society's competition, the hoof judging of which was held in conjunction with the autumn show ... <i>(Apr 12, 1951,NZ Farmer,15</i>)

### hoofmat
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a synthetic mat upon which stock can be kept during treatment for foot rot.

>  Mr Sweetman also developed the "cow mat" and the "hoofmat" that are used on farms throughout New Zealand to prevent foot rot in cattle. <i>(Mar 10, 2001,Dominion,7</i>)

### hoojacky
 
 <i>n.</i> thingumebob. [ca1940 at least R. Mason HOOJACKY (also HOOJAH)]

### hook
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WOOD</b> The fern is fired; if it burns well, all the thick and matted dead stuff at the bottom.. will be consumed, leaving only the shrivelled 'Tutu'; and the cane-like fern stalks.. should be cut at once with a strong hook, or better still, with a short scythe, and the 'Tutu' slashed down with a bill-hook. <i>(1959,From New Plymouth to New Plymouth,138</i>) [NOTES: Quoted from HURSTHOUSE 1849]



 
 <i>v.</i> to hook up and tow away (a repossessed car)

>  Doing repos won't make you rich. Hooking a car pays $75 to $80 plus GST and maybe another $50 if tracking it has taken a lot of work. <i>(Jul 5, 1987,Sunday Star (Auckland),B1</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to ride (a horse) to lose [racing slang]

>  His owner was not satisfied with his display on the second day, and he interrogated young Cress, who stood on his `dig,' and asked owner McDonald if he thought he hooked him. Of course McDonald thought nothing of the sort, but wanted some explanation of his horse's poor showing, <i>(May 14, 1910,NZ Truth,255/2/6</i>)

>  A dodge by owners that should have been worked out ages ago is the legging up of unfashionable horsemen when they want a horse stopped, and then, later on, when they want to back him, they put up a `big gun.' it is manifestly unfair to the boys, but the latter are compelled to `hook 'em' as they get so little riding that four quid is often very handy to them. <i>(May 21, 1910,NZ Truth,256/2/7</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> That horse has got no show. He is dead... He is going to be hooked, pulled and run into the ground. <i>(1962,Half-Gallon Jar,24</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to punch [(criminals', ?boxing slang)]

>  Talk of `demons' and `jokers' being `hooked' proved too much for Mr L.G.M. Sinclair, S.M., in Auckland Court. ...Te Atatu mussel curer, Benjamin Patrick Nathan, 28, explained. `"Demons"' he said, were detectives. To be "hooked" was to be "punched". ...' <i>(Jul 30, 1963,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  ..i tipped over on the floor with a bottle of beer in me hand and spilt some. ... `She must have seen me fall over ... she started yelling at me about getting my goddamn beer out of the place. i just says: `Oh, don't go yelling at me' and i hooked her one. <i>(Jun 20, 1963,Evening Post</i>) [NOTES: date a guess, needs checking]



 
 <i>n.</i> a gumfields tool [(gumfields) Cf hooker, hooking]

>  The tools used by the digger are the spade, the spear and the hook. ... The hook is used for raising the gum from the deeper and wetter swamps. it is a rod of iron perhaps twelve feet long, looped at one end for a handle, and with the last three feet of the other turned off at right angles and given its own peculiar shape. <i>(1900,NZ illustrated Magazine,3/208</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> The hook was used for drawing up nuggets of gum from the depths of swamps, after location by the spear. The hook consisted of a galvanised pipe an inch or so in diameter, equipped at one end with a spade handle, and at the other end with a steel `toe,' about an inch and a half in length, welded to the pipe, and forming the `hook'. it is said that expert diggers could bring up with ease gum of various sizes from great depths. <i>(1972,Gumdiggers, The,50</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the tusk of a wild boar.

>  <b>CARR</b> 'Good one, too. Look at his hooks.' 110 The end result was a charred hunk, which i eventually did skin anyway, and a set of tusks so burnt that they went as soft as chalk, chipping so easily that i had broken them, ruining a good set of hooks, before i realised what i had done. <i>(1986,Diary of a Pig Hunter,63</i>)

>  <b>SWAN</b> Surprisingly, it was a very good buck, it had excellent hooks and a lovely, dark winter coat. <i>(1992,Grunter Hunters, The,156</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an open enclosure that holds sheep on dog trial courses.

>  <b>GORDON</b> The three sheep stand waiting up to 800 metres away, in the liberation area, which is also known as the hook or 'the strings'. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,107</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Every dog triallist in New Zealand has trouble keeping three sheep together in the middle of a paddock out of the hook, the open enclosure that holds them for trialling. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,147</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sheepdogs] to retrieve or manoeuvre sheep into a mob.

>  <b>HALL</b> Bonnie wanted to swing through the basin, but i kept her going up until she spotted the sheep. it was pretty simple, she hooked them, and it worked perfectly ... <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,60</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> it's the pride you feel when your heading dog hooks a mob a mile away, though he's cast on running shingle. <i>(1997,Different Worlds,20</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a riding spur.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> put in the hooks: to apply the spurs. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,64</i>)

>  <b>MARRiOTT</b> i rode one we called Lady Bob, which loved to lie down in the river; it took strength and sharp hooks (spurs) to prevent her doing so. <i>(1981,Life in the Gorge,111</i>)

### hook grass
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [grass](go-gra.md#grass))

>  The New Zealand hook grass (Uncinia unciniata, actually a sedge) has seeds with little hooks that hitch a ride on anything passing close enough by. <i>(Jun 23, 2007,Dominion Post indulgence,17</i>)

>  in the valleys you will gather.. the seeds of hookgrass in your socks, as you become the unwitting spreader of cunning plant progeny. <i>(Jul 26, 1992,Dominion Sunday Times,21</i>)

### hook knife
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an instrument used in the castration of male lambs.

>  an instrument that docks lambs testicles - a hook knife. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### hook off
 
 <i>v.</i> to clear off; make off; get away.

>  <b>SARGESON</b> And if he couldn't [get away] i'd hook off while they had their barney. <i>(1946,That Summer,153</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> hook off: go away. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,300</i>) [NOTES: Pearson Glossary]

### hook on
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>MANSFiELD</b> `Come for a walk round the esplanade, Matilda. i can't stand this any longer.' `Right-o. i'll put on my ulster. isn't it an awful day!' Bogey's ulster is just like hers. Hooking the collar she looks at herself in the glass. ... Good-bye, dears; we shall be back soon. `This is better, isn't it?' [194] `Hook on,' says Bogey. They cannot walk fast enough. Their heads bent, their legs just touching, they stride like one eager person through the town, ... <i>(1920,Stories,193,(1984 Alpers) ed.</i>)

### hook one's bait/hook one's mutton
 
 <i>phr.</i> leave, take off (at speed)

>  <b>BAKER</b> to hook one's bait or mutton, to depart (a variant of the English sling one's hook). <i>(1941,NZ Slang,53</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> Hook one's bait (or mutton). To depart; to decamp, make off: New Zealand and Australian: C. 20. Baker. Elaboration of Hook it. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1078</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Some expressions that have been claimed for New Zealand are at the rate of knots `very fast', hook your mutton `clear out', have the wood on `have an advantage over' and the oaths Blimey Charlie and (By) Christchurch (obviously a neat save suggested by the place name). <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,177</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hook your mutton clear out, similar to `slinging your hook'; eg `Let's hook our mutton, there's no welcome here, fellas.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,57</i>)

### hook one's mutton
 
<b>2</b> <i>phr.</i> take one's partner (for a dance) [cf. sense 1]

>  <b>Mrs LUSK</b> Look slippy, buddies. Hook your muttons for an extra. <i>(1929,Love and Chiffon,233</i>)

### hooker
 
 <i>n.</i> one who uses a hook to retrieve kauri gum [(gumfields)]

>  When the swamps are dry the hooker has to open up the hardened surface with a spade. <i>(1900,NZ illustrated Magazine,3/208</i>)

### Hooker's sea lion
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) [ORIGIN: eponym]

>  We came across a hooker sea lion that was very aggressive... <i>(Aug 7, 2002,Capital Times,6</i>)

>  On Enderby island, the main haunt of the New Zealand or Hooker's sealion, many pups die of suffocation in rabbit burrows where they seek shelter from the cold windy subantarctic weather. <i>(Aug 18, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),81</i>)

### hooker-on
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARR</b> Log-haulers, worked from an engine, are used in some mills. Hooking the winding rope on to suitable trees ... demands ... quickness of judgment. Wages. ... bullocky 10s to 12s a day, hooker-on 9s to 12s a day. <i>(1913,Country Work,40</i>)

### hooking
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) cropping of cocksfoot seed with a sickle or hook.

>  <b>GORDON</b> 'Hooking' was a popular method of making a bob or two right into the sixties. For someone like Jim, who grew up on a cocksfoot farm, hooking was part of your childhood, though it was around the farm, not on the roadside. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,3</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> i.e. of gum [(gumfields)]

>  <b>PATTERSON</b> in former times, even in winter, `hooking' has been practised in the swamps.....The necessary tools were a long spear and a long hook - a long iron rod with a curved end. The gum.....found by the spear.....[was] worked up through the slime with the hook. <i>(1904,Kauri-Gum,6</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> Another method of getting the gum is by `hooking' ... <i>(1913,Country Work,42</i>)

>  <b>STALLWORTHY</b> in the swamps, the method of securing the gum was by spearing or hooking or, in shallow ground, by groping. <i>(1916,Early Northern Wairoa,53</i>)

### hookweight
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the weight of an animal carcass after dressing.

>  At the moment we're selling them as two-year-olds with a hookweight of 330-350kg. <i>(Apr 26, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,15</i>)

### hool
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sheep or cattle dogs] to coax or push unco-operative sheep or cattle.

>  <b>MiDDLETON</b> it was my job to stand in the race and hool the sheep on into the dip. <i>(1953,Short Stories (various authors),19</i>)

>  <b>SiNCLAiR</b> The same [noise] occurs with the indiscriminate hooling of cattle with half a dozen dogs. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,24</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> How many millions of sheep have been 'hooled' up trucking ramps with the poor, defenceless chapeau as the ultimate weapon. <i>(1996,Trouble with Stock Agents, The,95</i>)

>  <b>ASPiNALL</b> As a last resort, every dog available was hooled on to the steer. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,184</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sheep or cattle dogs] a noisy control of sheep or cattle.

>  <b>SALiSBURY</b> By now even our dogs had got sick of them and that's saying a lot as dogs generally just love a good hool on cattle. <i>(1994,i Was So Lucky,198</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to move at speed, cf hone, hoop, hoor; and also hare (?a var. of hurl). [ca1938 Havelock, esp. children]

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> Better get a wriggle on, Kori. There's 4 or 5 Jerry armoured cars hooling along the road. <i>(1944,Troop Target,41</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>SLATTER</b> From the kick-off the Welsh forwards hool into a loose ruck ... <i>(1970,On the Ball,138</i>)

### hooler
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep or cattle dog that creates a noisy fuss to coax reluctant animals.

>  <b>GORDON</b> .. remind their dog that, even though it is a dog trial, it's not a sheep hooler's free-for-all. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,148</i>)

>  A Cattle Muster on Molesworth: You don't want a mob of hoolers. <i>(May 4, 2002,Country Calendar (TVNZ)</i>)

### hooley
 
 <i>n.</i> a wild party

>  When i got back to the bach a hooley was in full swing. <i>(Apr 2002,NZ Memories,35/17</i>)

>  There was no big cocktail hooley to mark the store's opening.. "because it's too close to fashion week right now." <i>(Oct 7, 2001,Sunday Star Times,D3</i>)

>  [Advt] Having a roughie. Have a smoothie. <i>(Dec 26, 1999,Sunday Star Times,A4</i>)

>  Pitt islanders would celebrate being the first to see the millennium dawn with a low-key ceremony involving the unveiling of four sculptures (donated by a German), a church service and a "hoolie at the woolshed." <i>(Dec 26, 1999,Sunday Star Times,A4</i>)

>  The Miner's Hall / was there for the weekend pictures, the Women's institute / Concert, and big hoolies like the Centennial Ball. <i>(Sep 28, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>) [NOTES: Jeffrey Paparoa Holman]

>  Result: a nine-hour hooley.. at Kelliher's Retreat on Puketutu island in the Manukau Harbour. <i>(Dec 21, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  "This gig" is <i>The Finn Brothers Hooley</i>, Neil and Tim, together with special guests.. next weekend at Auckland's St James Theatre. <i>(May 26, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F1</i>)

>  This one-day hooley is a celebration of the valuable resource that is the backbone of the Outback town. <i>(Jul 24, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53</i>)

>  <b>HUGHES</b> These hoolies were good fun. <i>(1999,Shooting from the Lip,213</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> The Riggerstring was the annual Boat Club Ball, a real hooley. <i>(1967,Man on the Horse, The,138</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> He must have drawn a bit of overtime because this hooley developed into one of his occasional wild nights. <i>(1965,Open Country Harvest,209</i>) [NOTES: From 1970 edition]

>  `Since going to Southland he had been shocked by the number of people who held `drunken hooleys' in their homes' said Rev. C.L. Gosling at the Presbyterian Assembly. <i>(Nov 11, 1950,Marlborough Express</i>)

>  <b>KEiNZLY</b> `it isn't a call-out, Mr Pilgrim, it's an invitation. He said to tell you he struck the double and they're having a hooley.' <i>(1960,Tangahano,71`</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> ` ... you are in time for the do tomorrow night. We haven't had a hooley since you came in, have we? ... ` `Both of us?' said Anne in alarm. `To a ... [sic] What is it you call it? A hooley?' That's right,' said Claude. `A hooley. A binge. A bash. A shindig.' <i>(1963,in the Sticks,71</i>)

>  [Pat Rockford's col.: Maori anecdotes] ...We often held a reunion event of our rugby football club. ...Puhenui Sam was at one such hooley one night... <i>(1963,NZ Truth</i>) [NOTES: ?d ?p.]

>  <b>HELMER</b> `Where's me grog, Harry?' `Funny thing about that. Few of the lads came in last night and we got a proper hooley goin', eh. Scoffed the lot, ...' <i>(1964,Stag Party,103</i>)

>  On the Friday afternoon before the intended function [party] the peace of the office was shattered when one of our fellow workers dashed in and in a loud voice announced; `The hoolie's off 'cos me cobber's sheila's crook.' <i>(Apr 7, 1979,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> A hooley is a WiLD PARTY. in NZ felt to be irish, (and perhaps associated with hooligan) but probably a development of OED hoolee (sb) [Hindi holi] E indies. The great festival or carnival of the Hindoos, held at the approach of the vernal equinox, in honour of Krishna and the Gopis milkmaids < 1687.1800 the hooly, or saturnalia of india, when liberty of speech and action towards superiors are allowed ... 1825. During all the time of Hoolee, drunkenness is common among the Hindoos. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,52</i>)

>  The bloke that bought the last ie at Saturday's hooley was quite dewy eyed about it next morning . <i>(Oct 7, 1988,Contact (Wgtn),4</i>)

>  <b>LAY</b> `Sounds like someone's having a hooley,' said Stephen. ... There was a hooley all right. At their place. <i>(1988,Fools on the Hill, The,106</i>)

### hoolie
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a to-do, fuss, commotion?? [meaning unclear from citation?]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Brandy ... was a tiger on pigs. Mount White was thick with them at the time, and there were some great `hoolies'. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,78</i>)

### Hooligan's gallstones
 
 <i>n.</i> a nickname for the Moeraki Boulders

>  Only at Moeraki, however, have concretions achieved icon status - and attracted a raft of fanciful names. Some locals refer to them as Hooligan's gallstones. Other monikers include giant gob-stoppers, aliens' brains and - somewhat more respectfully - the Stonehenge of New Zealand. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/15</i>)

### hooling
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sheep or cattle dogs] making a noisy fuss when working stock.

>  <b>GORDON</b> At the start of a huntaway run there can still be a place for the dog to do a bit of hooling, despite the fact that care is needed to get a nicely controlled lift. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,148</i>)

### hoomangamoka
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Apera arundinacea</i> Hoomangamoka <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/127</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

### hoon
 
 <i>v.</i> (informal) (often as hoon around, hoon it up) to behave in a loutish way, esp. by driving recklessly

>  He just got carried away, and hooned. <i>(Sep 27, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  it was one of my first times out and i went hooning off after the dogs with everyone else. <i>(Nov 13, 2002,Capital Times,20</i>)

>  <b>McGill</b> They were definitely doing nothing for the village, layabouts hooning around in their big old rustbuckets, rumbling, booming exhausts... <i>(1995,Gold in the Creek,10</i>)

>  hoon-as verb. He likes to hoon round on his bike. Also hoonery. <i>(Oct 21, 1983,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: 1983 Oct 21 Letter M.A. Nixon (Palmerston North) (adolescent and school slang)]

>  <b>MOORE</b> One [life] in the city, being political; the other at weekends, hooning around up north, though music, new friends in Auckland and the Vietnam protests were slowly combining to give my life more direction. <i>(1987,Hard Labour,19</i>)

>  Former deputy Mayor ... said after the race she had noticed people `hooning around' Ohariu Valley. The review could find out if road accidents increased because of the race. <i>(Nov 9, 1988,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  The Young Nats hooned it up [at the Party Conference] until the small hours of the morning. -->Hooning it up, actually, is a bit unfair. i'm told that apart from two wags who dressed up in tails this year's crop of Young Nats were an earnest bunch.... <i>(Jun 25, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> a humanitarian, ethical or political objector to military service; a person whose objection to military service is based on other than religious convictions; a political or humanitarian objector 1941-5. ACB: ca1941-45 A.C. Barrington; J. McCreary; a non-religious objector [WWII]

>  <b>GRANT</b> The third group were non-religious objectors (known as 'hoons'in camp) who were a diverse group, often individualists, who opposed war for a variety of ethical and humanitarian reasons... Christian pacifists and some 'hoons' mixed well and learned from each other. 'Hoon' George Gunn recalled that the Methodists "simply glowed with humanity." <i>(2004,Question of Faith, A,53</i>)

>  <b>FOOTE</b> However, a number of Christian pacificsts and some 'hoons', - humanitarian/ethical/political objectors - felt this argument was a cop out. <i>ibid.</i> 62 The majority of the Christian pacifists in detention were Methodists. Hoon George Gunn wrote, "they simply glowed with humanity." <i>(2000,Bread and Water,14</i>) [NOTES: Used in WWii period]

>  <b>CRYER</b> During the war the same word [sc. hoon]underwent a complete reversal of meaning. Poeple who were conscientious objectors on other than religious grounds were known to the military as hoons... <i>(2001,Curious Questions,102</i>)

>  <b>HAM,iLTON</b> i hadn't been in detention long when i heard his name mentioned ...He hadn't gone to jail with the first group ...They reckoned he'd stuck to his religion all through and the Hoons [(fn) non - religious groups] gave me the impression that he took his religion seriously ... you couldn't take much notice of the Hoons of course, not about a thing like that. Most of them just lumped the religoes together ... . <i>ibid.</i> 164 There was a sort of line drawn in the [detention] camps betwen the Hoons and the religoes. ... The religoes were divided again into God knows how many sects and amongst the hoons were absolute pacifists, pacifists, Communists, Anarchists, humanists, and plain dodgers. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,154</i>)

>  <b>SANDYS</b> `i'm lucky to be here [in a detention camp for conscientious objectors]. Reckon they must have got me mixed up with the Hoons. i'd be much worse off in a regular gaol.' `Are all the men here pacifists?' ... / `But you'd have a job getting anything like a consensus out of the lot we've got here. Some are war resisters- ... Then there are the Christians. ... They're still trying to work out how the hell they landed up [here] ... The majority are Hoons - military defaulters. Against war for reasons of sanity and humanity, not out of religious conviction. <i>(1982,Love and War,244</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a lout or hooligan, especially a young person who drives recklessly.

>  [Hdg] Cricket hoon bin urged. <i>(Jan 5, 1993,Dominion,8</i>)

>  [re. Blue Bayou film] ...hoons [ie. National Guard rednecks on patrol in a Louisiana bayou] versus Cajuns [French-descended hunter-trappers]. <i>ibid.</i> 28/3 One of the National Guard hoons rakes the Cajuns with machine-gun fire <i>(Jul 17, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  Just inside the door at Flanagans' and the police radio operator Paul Curtis races in, `Boss, there's a crowd of hoons fighting...' The `hoons' - anyone who is not a police officer ... <i>(Dec 11, 1980,Dominion,6/8</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> HOON (n) Uncultured, boorish person. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,249</i>)

>  To a hoon, the gully is the place behind the embankment, aclank with discarded beer cans, where you propel your fellow hoons when you all get drunk, and where you relieve yourself in the process of getting drunk. <i>(Mar 24, 1983,Dominion,1</i>)

>  hoon NZ & Austral. sl. 1 n. lout, hooligan. 2 v.i behave loutishly. [orig. unkn.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,360</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  But beneath that there is an entire substratum of what we might call `part-time hoons', `weekend hoons' or `holiday hoons'.The sons (and daughters) of the middle classes, they are less professional in their hooning. Tell-tale signs: car obviously belonging to mum ..., two in the front (no bench seat), four in the back with crates on their knees, zinc on their noses, . <i>(Dec 20, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  <b>HUTCHiNGS</b> Dent, a hoon ahead of his time, reacted sharply to the bowlers advancing in creams and straw boaters. <i>(1987,Tall Half-Backs,85</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hoon hooligan; in pre-WWii Australia, a procurer of prostitutes; possibly German origin. ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,57</i>)

>  Waterfront race hoons on agenda [heading] The `hoon effect' caused by the Nissan Mobil 500 is one area that could be looked at in a major review ... <i>(Nov 9, 1988,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  ... goons, hoons and loons ... <i>(Nov 7, 1989,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: John Banks MP 9.45pm]

>  They minced past hoons-on-foot murmuring "Happy New Year" as if it was a promise. <i>(Jan 16, 1993,Dominion,10</i>)

>  He installed equipment in the late TVNZ boss Neil Roberts' Rolls Royce and Ferrari - "he was the perfect example of what i'd call the new-age hoon. <i>(Jan 19, 2000,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  We also wanted to impress the Poms with an elegant hoon who could tell a great drawing-room joke so off he [sc. Paul East] went. <i>(Oct 7, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  <i>Bread and Water</i> is an interesting piece of social history with an intriguing footnote. Apparently the word "hoon" was prison slang for a conscientious objector who had ethical, but not religious, convictions. <i>(Aug 5, 2001,Sunday Star Times,f2</i>)

>  <b>CRYER</b> Before the second World War a hoon was a loutish exhibitionist... [After the war] the word remained in use, but reverted to its earlier sense. A few grammatical extensions also exist: hoonish, hoonism, hooning, hoondom, hoon around, hoon it up, a bit of a hoon, hoon-chaser (traffic police) and hoon-bin(place where drunken football hoons are put to cool off). <i>(2001,Curious Questions,102</i>)

>  Crooks, hoons and yahoos seemed to be zeroing in on Pauanui like flies to a jampot... <i>(Feb 13, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),25</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a spell of reckless driving

>  Golden haired boy, ..., head motor salesperson ... `borrowed' Aunty Astle's Roller for a bit of a hoon ... and roared away to a Parnell pizzeria, where he parked haphazardly on a pedestrian crossing ... The heat was called, and guess what they discovered, ... <i>(Mar 1986,Metro,27</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> a (boisterous) fool [(J- or K-Force) ca1951 F.M. O'Brien, In J- or K-force yakker, a fool, clot; hence aj. hoony, foolish, dumb by Wellington tramwaymen e.g. Those hoony bastards will lose us our overtime. cf Gook]

>  <b>LELAND</b> hoon: TURKEY (human variety). This expression is largely confined to the southermost part of the South island. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,52</i>)

### hoon-ethos
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The best-known primer for this kind of activity, and indeed an integral part of the hoon-ethos itself, is the drinking game. Designed to take the monotony out of rapid, heavy drinking, the drinking game perhaps found its purest expression in the version entitled Next. Down a drink as fast as possible (commonly known as `sculling'), turn to the bloke on your left and say `Next', whereupon he does the same ... very efficient. <i>(Dec 20, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

### hoon-nation
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The haka shall lie heavy on the hoon-nation of Woller Bees. <i>(Feb 20, 1993,Evening Post,4</i>)

### hoonchaser
 
 <i>n.</i> a traffic cop

>  COPS Three nights on the road with the hoonchasers. [title] <i>(Sep 1986,Metro,70</i>)

### hoondom
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  On display was a wide range of these Wellingtonians - louts, larrikins, lunks, lummoxes, yahoos - the creme de la creme of hoon <i>(Mar 24, 1983,Dominion,1</i>)

### hoonery
 
 <i>n.</i> [1983 Oct 21 Letter M.A. Nixon (Palmerston North) [adolescent and school slang] hoon-as verb. He likes to hoon round on his bike. Also hoonery.]

>  The sexual frisson can be smelt, and mindless drunken hoonery is never far away. <i>(Jan 30, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  The chief executive of the Hotel Association, Tom Sheehy, said last night that students contained elements of `hoonery', just as did other groups of society. <i>(Sep 21, 1987,Dominion,3</i>)

### hooning
 
 <i>n.</i> irresponsible driving or other wild behaviour [last two citations belong with the verb to hoon]

>  They're also hooning all over the paths - how do we stop them. <i>(Sep 22, 1999,Norwester,1</i>)

>  A flatmate of [Neil Roberts'] youth, Snow recalls buckets of apricot wine brewing under the bed, weekly ceremonial lunches of curry and chips, hooning through Europe from London to Athens in four weeks in a VW. <i>(Nov 21, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  There was always motorbike noise and hooning while they were there. <i>(Oct 7, 1992,independent Herald (Wellington),1</i>)

>  But beneath that there is an entire substratum of what we might call `part-time hoons', `weekend hoons' or `holiday hoons'.The sons (and daughters) of the middle classes, they are less professional in their hooning. Tell-tale signs: car obviously belonging to mum ..., two in the front (no bench seat), four in the back with crates on their knees, zinc on their noses, ... <i>(Dec 20, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hooning wild or raucous behaviour, often phr. hooning around; eg `After losing the match, the visiting team got drunk and started hooning around town.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,58</i>)

### hoonish
 
 <i>adj.</i> loutish

>  Market research had shown the rock-oriented Windy was not retailer-friendly. it had young, hoonish connotations. <i>(Jun 10, 1993,Evening Post,29</i>)

>  Probably appeared hoonish [behaviour], but no one was sozzled. <i>(Jul 1984,Cosmo,36</i>)

>  According to Professor Taylor the capping band came in acting `loutish and hoonish' whilst dropping beer cans. One of them then picked up a student's glasses and was punched by their owner.. <i>(May 5, 1986,Salient,3</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  A lot of hoonish summer behaviour may be the very result of biorhythmical and biochemical imbalances.... <i>(Nov 5, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),70</i>)

### hoonishness
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  it's in his leisure life that he demonstrates his hoonishness, as the owner of a car with a sound system that can make a hell of a racket... it's suspected that there is only one father - the shagging megahoon. <i>(Mar 23, 2000,Dominion,8</i>)

### hoonism
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Perhaps it has become a necessity of modern living to convince ourselves that noise pollution is really a good, positive and even more ludicrously "musical" thing, instead of seeing it.. for the unadulterated mindless hoonism that it really represents. <i>(Jan 14, 2002,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>HAMiLTON</b> True Hoonism. God be praised, was rare in the camps and most of those who appraoched the standard ... were political or union men. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,208</i>)

### hoonmobile
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  i wasn't sure whether to be flattered or outraged by the stares from the rear windows of the hoonmobiles. <i>(Jan 16, 1993,Dominion,10</i>)

### hoop
 
 <i>v.</i> (informal) to speed, rush, hurry. [? variation of whoop [cf hone, hool ca1938 Havelock children ca1952 2ZB Wellington radio Cyclists hoop along and miss the scenery.]

>  ...i gave [him] one or two riding instructions - to jump quickly, hoop her out and get her into the race. <i>(Feb 8, 2004,Sunday Star (Auckland),B14</i>)

>  Mr Williams said the Fiordland area contained pockets of wapiti... `But these blokes who are breaking the law come hooping in pick out about eight or nine well-grown animals, many of them pure-breds,' he said. <i>(Dec 14, 1973,Dominion,3/1</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Harry ... is a very capable and experienced pilot and on these `spotting' trips he hooped in and out of gullies with the wing tips ... only feet clear of the slopes. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,57</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(cheesemaking)]

>  The hoops not being repaired would cause the bandages to get broken, causing the cheese to be broken, thereby damaging them. <i>(Apr 16, 1887,Auckland Provincial Gazette,21</i>)

### hoop it
 
 <i>v.</i> to depart; hop it; hook it [ca1941 eg They rang the firebell & hooped it before the cop came.]

### hoop it along
 
 <i>v.</i> hurry it up, get a move on

>  ...pious injunctions [to the gharry driver] to `yella', `igri', `hoop it along' and `set it alight' <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in the Middle East,31</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a circular net slung around a hoop and baited to attract crayfish [see also hoop-pot v.]

### hoop-pot
 
 <i>v.</i> use a hoop pot, a circular net slung around a hoop and baited to attract crayfish [also see hoop net]

 <i>adj.</i> drunk [ca1940 Wellington]

 <i>adj.</i> tipsy; tight as a hoop
