### hoor
 
 <i>v.</i> At least since ca1938 or `to speed; to hoop (along)' and used as a var. of hool, (w)hoop, hone; see hooer.

### hooray
 
 <i>exclam.</i> goodbye, cheerio [From <i>N.Z. English to U.S. English Dictionary</i> at http://nz.com/NZ/Culture/NZDic.html] (See [also hurray](../A#also-hurray))

>  His companion..thrust back his seat, stood erect, called 'Horray!' and was gone. <i>(1937,Civilian into Soldier,255</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> 'Bye-bye, Mr. Cunningham.' 'Hooray,' Gil said. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,35</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> 'Well, hooray.' 'Hooray.' <i>(1960,Maori Girl,128</i>)

>  ' Best of luck on the lake. Hooray! <i>(Oct 15, 1965,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> When he said good-bye to his mother on going to the war i expect old Clive just ... said `Hooray, Ma!' ... <i>(1924,Follow the Call,65,1975 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRiE</b> `Allo' `Fine day' `Yair' pause. `Going far?' `Aw, no. Up along'. Long Pause. `Aw well, hooray!' `Hooray!' <i>(1935,Little Country, The,66</i>)

>  <b>MiTCHELL & STRONG</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... hooray!, the New Zealander's good-bye. <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> When i said `Hooray' he called me back and gave me a few francs; i reckon i was very tinny. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>) [NOTES: 1935 A.E. Strong WW1 scene in Partridge Slang Today 287]

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> Hooray. This coll. form of hurrah, hurray is half-way between dignity and impudence: C. 18-20. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,403</i>)

>  ... and, saying `Hooray!' instead of `Good-bye,' the New Zealander might advise [the traveller] to beware of `the John Hops' ... <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>BAKER</b> Of children's terms ... we may note ... the popular farewell expression hooray! <i>(1941,NZ Slang,51</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> `See you some more' is the common phrase of casual farewell, the alternative being `hooray' (the New Zealand modification of `hurrah'). <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,91</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> `Hooray,' Gil said. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,33</i>)

>  i would include among New Zealand English words: hooray: goodbye <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> We stand there a moment. `Hooray, Ron. Come up in the hoeing season. i still got the peg there.' And he heads briskly for the stairway ... <i>ibid.</i> 152 `Cheeri,' he calls. i don't like these new slangs. Hooray is the word. Everybody in the Div said hooray. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,45</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> ROY: Okay, then. Hooray, Queenie. QUEENiE ...: Hooray, Roy. He goes off. <i>(1963,Pohutukawa Tree,15</i>)

>  <b>HARVEY</b> [farewell (Say hooray)] He opened the sliding door without saying hooray to anyone and dropped off the tram even though it was going pretty fast. <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,20</i>)

>  <b>MiDDLETON</b> `Well hooray,' i said. `and thanks for showing me the place.' <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,51</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> hooray: good-bye. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,300</i>) [NOTES: Pearson Glossary]

>  <b>TURNER</b> The word huuray (56 [occurrences in Queensland survey of 500,000 words]) is used for `good-bye', with the same intonation; it is very common in New Zealand, usually spelt hooray, but seems to be unknown or uncommon in Melbourne. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,125</i>)

>  hurray! interj. goodbye! used in N.Z. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> We learnt that at midday exactly ...the salutation is always `good-afternoon', and that either `Cheery' or `Hooray' is the colloquial way of saying goodbye. <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,108</i>)

>  <b>COWLEY</b> `See you,' said Rosalind [a 13-year-old]. `Hooray, Arthur.' <i>(1971,Man of Straw,38</i>)

>  <b>MiTCHELL</b> hooray: Lamentation and expression of regret at being parted from someone, eg `i must go now. Hooray.' <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,182</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> `Well i've got to go now. See you later,' Tina said ... `Cheerio,' Lorna said. `Hooray,' i said. <i>(1973,NZ Jack,116</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> He was as sad as Bedivere watching King Arthur sail away in the black barge as he said, `Hooray!' <i>(1978,Elver, The,18</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> hooray: (pronounced hurray) A Canadian lass, new to New Zealand, was mortally offended the other day when, as she was leaving a party, several people said `hooray'. it was much later that she discovered that in New Zealand, this is a FRiENDLY GOODBYE. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,52</i>)

>  hooray: a very common NZ term of saying `goodbye'; e.g., `Well i must go now, hooray then.' <i>(1981,NZ Police National Headquarters</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>GiLDERDALE</b> hooray Goodbye. <i>(1982,Sea Change,241</i>) [NOTES: (Note on Language)]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> <i>(1950,Our Street,79</i>)

>  hooray int. NZ goodbye. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,360</i>) [NOTES: [Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 538; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 236]]

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hooray cheerful goodbye; Bennett says modification of `hurrah'. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,58</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> `Hooray,' says the postie, cheerfully. `Hooray to you too.' <i>(1983,Bone People, The,103</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>exclam.</i> often in extended and dismissive form, hooray fuck, often said as an insult to someone [ca1951 Colin Gordon, Freezing Works, Foxton When the whistle goes there are cries of `Hooray fuck!'.]

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hooray fuck! WWii army exclamation, modified to hooray f-f-forget it, undoubtedly unmodified again at news of war's end. [Jim Henderson] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,58</i>)

### hooshter
 
 <i>n.</i> ?Camel Corps equipment. [[AND one who rides a camel, esp. Camel Corps 1917 and 1978 (hist); nf P; OED.]]

>  Riding a "hooshter" or riding a horse, We're one and the same in the line... Look at 'em. 'Ark at 'em. Swinging along, Their "hooshters" a'skimmin' the sand. <i>(Jul 5, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,iV/47 : 251</i>)

### hoot
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Ask [the Maoris] what is the hute or price. [here loot: prob a typist's error: check MS] <i>(1842,NZ Journal,3/61</i>) [NOTES: (1841) NZJ 1842 3/61-117 (A. Perry, Wellington)]

>  <b>BARNiCOAT</b> Our first endeavour was to get a native as a guide over the hills. `How much the [?] loot' of course was the answer to our enquiries <i>(1842,Journal,163,1845 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> How much the utu? <i>(1905,Tale of Bush,215</i>) [NOTES: (Northland)]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ...one man, presenting himself out of breath, asked for his "hoot," as he thought it had all gone. <i>(1918,Digger Junior, The,14 ed.</i>)

>  The natives ...will not sell their pigs and potatoes for anything else than money or the utu as they call it. <i>(1843,Letters from Settlers,122</i>) [NOTES: (R.Ross, baker, Nelson) (1842 Nov) 1843 Letters from Settlers122]

>  <b>THATCHER</b> (Sights at Grahamstown) There's Bobby Graham, who has got the Maories well in hand,/ And for a bottle or two of rum from the natives bought the land;/ Though with Whitaker and Lundon he's having a dispute,/ Old Bobby chuckles to himself and says kanui te utu; [pron u:t]]/ He lives at Sandy Tararu, and now and then comes down/ To get his rents, and collar lots of tin in Grahamstown. <i>(1869,Wit and Humour: Local Songs...,9</i>)

>  <b>PRATT</b> [The chiefs] had .... a critical knowledge of the value of the `utu' (money) <i>(1877,Colonial Experiences,51</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CRUMP</b> We ought to have something to aim at, like getting a bit of hoot together to buy a little farm. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,144</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> it's over three weeks we've been without any hoot and some of us'll have to give in soon or we'll bloody starve. <i>(1984,Blackball 08,172</i>)

>  There was a bag tied around the effigy with the word "hoot" written on it. <i>(Mar 18, 1924,Otago Witness,26</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> ...he and his people went and robbed Boyle for the (hutu) payment. <i>(1820,Journal,66,(ed Barton) ed.</i>)

>  <b>HORTON</b> They then seized one of our axes....saying that it should be the hutu or payment for what he had done. <i>(1828,New Zealand (various authors/titles),col 3. p1.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> The Cabin boy counted 8 Teeth marks [in the Chief's buttocks] and the Hout [utu] or Compensation money was 8 Figs of Tobacco. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),30,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> i dismissed her forthwith and told her that if she would call at the Coco she would receive a blanket as a Hout. <i>(1834,Journal,64</i>)

>  A native was found dead the other day. it is the opinion of the medical men that he died in a fit but some of the natives for purpose of seeking `hoot' - payment declare he has been dewitched by white men. <i>(Aug 8, 1841,Letter,154</i>) [NOTES: Revans to Chapman]

>  <b>BARNiCOAT</b> A little table top...which was..kept for ute for bringing us down. <i>(1843,Journal,11</i>)

>  <b>MAJORiBANKS</b> in trade, selling land, or selling their produce, no Jew of Monmouth Street can deal with more cunning or more persevering importunity; nothing is done, however slight, without demanding the ootu or payment, and, if instead of higgling, you at once give what is asked, they [Maoris] become uneasy at having made such a boko keeno (trade bad). <i>(1844,Travels in NZ,88</i>)

>  <b>MAJORiBANKS</b> Nothing is done, however slight, without demanding the ootu or payment. <i>(1846,New Zealand (various authors/titles),88</i>)

>  <b>STAFFORD</b> i suppose you obtained 3h [pounds] the from O'Sullivan and sent it To Weld as utu for Glender [a horse he bought] <i>(Dec 1850,Papers,1/69</i>) [NOTES: (E.J.W. Lyttleton)]

>  <b>McLEAN</b> The tribe were going to take `hutu' out of him <i>(1864,Papers,23</i>) [NOTES: (Auckland)]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> [Note] Utu, revenge or satisfaction on the principle of blood for blood; also payment in more modern times. <i>(1889,Far South Fancies,156</i>)

>  <b>H W ORSMAN</b> A. Maori contexts. Orig. a European rendering of the Maori noun utu, return (for anything ); satisfaction, ransom, reward, price, reply' (Williams); not to be used confused with uto `revenge', a confusion evident in many of the explanations of the meaning of utu). The word widely used was in all the above senses by Europeans who had dealings with Maoris, from the general `return for work, injury, etc.' to the concrete `money' it is often found constructed with the definite article `the utu, hutu' etc. translating the original Maori indefinite article, or adjective `some' he utu `an utu, some utu'. (Occas. constr with indef. article, see 1834). in later European usage the definite article was dropped to conform to English idiom eg' `give me the hoot' becomes `give me (some) hoot' The following is perhaps a `transitional' use 1828 C Davis journal Colenso Papers 3/18) `Another chief had been killed as a utu or payment'. (is this [ei utu] reflecting Maori [(h)e utu], as well as English a for an?). The phonology, resulting in the English hoot, parallels that of toot from tutu, with the final unvoiced [u] not being heard by English ears. The initial [h] derives perhaps from a syncopation of [he utu]; but more prob. from the var. or error noted on p. 4 of 1842: Maunsell Grammar of the New Zealand Language (4th ed) 1894: `in pronouncing u, the speaker will have to guard against the error of those who prefix the aspirate when no aspirate is admissable. According to the u, utu, etc., are pronounced as if spelt hu, hutu.'. <i>(1990,Personal Communication</i>)

>  The land that's waste they'll parcel oot, / ... And sell't to all that's got the hoot. [fn] Utu: Maori word for money. <i>(1964,Book of New Zealand, A (J.C. Reid),285</i>) [NOTES: (Otago Gold-miners' Song)]

>  <b>WiLSON</b> `Here's some hoot.' Don handed him a couple of fives. <i>(1973,NZ Jack,110</i>)

>  hoot n. Austral. & N.Z. a slang word for money. [< Maori utu price] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,538</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 360; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 236]]

>  <b>WiLSON</b> But we soon found out he was hitting the booze too much and spending all his hoot on the ponies. <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,47</i>)

>  For some reason this paper had deployed an investigative team to find out how much hoot the lads had received for their capers on the veldt. <i>(Nov 28, 1986,Contact (Wgtn),2</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hoot money; corruption of Maori `utu', ransom or price; also hootoo, hout and hutu; c. 1840. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,58</i>)

>  My brothers' slang and `funny' sayings delighted me. ... i remember [126] such words as `batty', `hoot' and `spondulicks' and many that ... were corruptions of Maori words. i spell them here as they sounded to me then: ehoa, harka, kupai, kure, munga munga typo, markuta, pooku, tangy. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,125</i>) [NOTES: (Meikle)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [Hoot, is first recorded as slang from Otago in 1864. This is reasonable. In the more Maori districts the use would still have primary Maori meanings and associations, while in Otago, where there were few Maoris, the transference to purely European idiom be could completed much earlier. This transference was probably helped by rhyme with loot (cf the ? mistaken interpretation of 1845 Barnicoat Journal 163. `Our first endeavour was to get a native as a guide over the hills `How much the loot' of course was the answer to our enquiries.') though op. cit 190 `bargained for utu'); loot could have been an occasional joking word for money, although it is not attested in NZ until fairly recently but cf Partridge. Supp. Loot 3. `Scottish slang for money received on pay-day' Services: ca 1925; anglicised as hoot, money, cash.]

>  Picton opens up a glorious harvest for the Dunedin harlots, whose trade here is, from want of hoot, decidedly on the wane. <i>ibid.</i> 3/12 We shall soon have no `hoot' to pay the piper <i>(1864,Saturday Review,X62</i>)

>  <b>BARR</b> The land that's waste they'll parcel oot, /...../ and sell't to all that's got the hoot. (FN) Hutu, Maori for money. <i>(1879,Old identities, The,333</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> ~ slang term for compensation, payment, money; characteristic corruption of Maori Utu(q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,201</i>)

>  `There are several specimens of bush slang transplanted from the Maori language. `Hoot' is a very frequent synonym for money or wage. i have heard a shearer at the Pastoralist Union office in Sydney when he sought to ascertain the scale of remuneration, enquire of the gilt-edged clerk behind the barrier, `What's the hoot, mate?' The Maori equivalent for money is utu, pronounced by the Ngapuhi and other northern tribes with the last syllable clipped, and the word is very largely used by the kauri-gum diggers and station hands in the North island. The original meaning of first settled in New Zealand, they found that the savage inhabitants had no conception of any recompense except the grim recompense of blood. Under Christianizing influences the natives were induced to forego the blood-revenge for injuries, on receiving a solatium in goods or land, and so utu came to have the double meaning of revenge and recompense, and eventually became recognized as the Maori word for money.' <i>(Jan 12, 1896,Truth (Sydney)</i>)

>  <i>(1899,Otago Witness,47/4</i>) [NOTES: get cit (J Thomson p.c.)]

>  <i>(Jan 17, 1917,Camp Courier , The,5</i>)

>  Pig island N.C.O.'s only go for the extra couple of bob a day ... the hoot is all they're chasing. <i>(Sep 15, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,28</i>)

>  Everybody ... must think every day of money, but although one may think `money' he may call it `gonce', `oof', `hoot', `stuff', `brass', `ooftish', `boodle', `cash', `gilt', `kale', or a dozen other ... things. <i>(Jul 11, 1921,Quick March,13</i>)

>  ~ n. money; payments; wage; compensation. [corrupt. fr. Maori utu, money] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,5</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... hoot, money, from Maori hutana; <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>) [NOTES: 1935 Mitchell and Strong in Partridge Slang Today 286]

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> Hoot. Money; payment, wage; compensation: New Zealand and soon Australia (- 1896). Ex Maori utu (money), often pronounced with clipped terminal. Morris. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,403</i>)

>  Slang words of Maori origin were once common, but most of them are dead and dying [and include] ... `kai,' `kapai,' `hoot' (utu), ... <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>BAKER</b> it was in [the 20 years after 1840] ... that hoot first came into general currency as a synonym for money or cash. This is probably one of the best known of all New Zealand's slang terms. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,22</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> in New Zealand `hoot' is a slang term for money; probably a corruption of utu. <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,26</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> Hoot, money (from Maori utu = revenge, through the intermediate sense of `compensation') <i>(1943,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,93</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include: ... hoot: money; from Maori utu (atonement) <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)

>  hoot: corruption of utu, slang expression for money. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> `Some girls are lucky, splashing their old man's hoot on a new frock every summer,' i'd say looking her up and down. <i>ibid.</i> 144 `Wonder just how much hoot does go with it,' says Uncle Ted, scratching his chin. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,87,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MUiR</b> We could do with some chips at the moment ... We need all the hoot we can muster. <i>(1960,Word for Word,143</i>)

### hooter
 
 <i>n.</i> the toilet

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Went to the hooter to shake hands with an old friend - Shot through on the inner coming into the straight - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,162</i>)

### hootin' and yellin'
 
 <i>phr.</i> [(musterers')]

>  <b>STRONACH</b> it is essential that each man should know the position of those above and below him. This is made possible by means of signals - fires, the barking of dogs, or by the shouts of the men - `hootin' and yellin'', as we used to call it. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,23</i>)

### hooya
 
 <i>exclam.</i> [(children's) ca1938 Havelock children]

>  <b>BAKER</b> hooya!, a children's expression that is probably the equivalent of `who are you?' <i>(1941,NZ Slang</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hooya! derisive juvenile exclamation; short for `who are you?'. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,58</i>)

### hoozler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... the track dangerous with challenge - from the `dark hoozler' who could leave one struggling at the last bog ... as he disappeared into the Turere, or, greater humiliation, to be left behind by the graceful feminine figure of the `Red Terror' ... whose ... speed embarrassed many a husky male. <i>(1971,VUWTC'71,32</i>)

### hop
 
 <i>v.</i> in various uses in NZ (as elsewhere) in extensions of the sense `jump, and so move quickly'.

>  <b>McLEAN</b> Little Whitmore [said] that then he would hop across to Wairoa and settle that business. <i>(1865,Papers,28/226</i>) [NOTES: (Ormond)]

>  The boys [were] having a bit of a spin up, and he hopped into the [two-up] ring and did a trot for seven hundred frames. <i>(Sep 5, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,28</i>)

>  Come on, me lucky lads, you pick 'em an' i'll pay 'em. Hop right in, diggers, and have a fly with the old man. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,105</i>)

>  P.J. Wilson ...he hopped across by the swing bridge. <i>(1950,Landfall,16/30</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>SARGESON</b> hop: hop in (a dinghy or a taxi) get in; hop up, rise from bed; hop round, move about. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,300</i>) [NOTES: 1964 Pearson Glossary]

### hop beer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WiERZBiCKA</b> We are very much interested by the [diggers'] process ... our visit being enhanced by a good swig of hop beer as they call it made of hops, sugar, ginger and water. <i>(1973,Wilson Family,158</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Jan 30 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 158]

### hop bush
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  hop bush A name given to a class of Australian trees, one of which, called by the Maoris `ake ake', also grows in New Zealand. (See ake ake. <i>(1968,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),208,8th ed ed.</i>)

### hop off with
 
 <i>v.</i> to take, or go, away with; to elope with [ca1935 Havelock.]

### hop one's frame
 
 <i>phr.</i> (informal) (often as an order) get a move on; take oneself off [. (? WW1 army)]

>  <b>BATTLE</b> Shake it up, Bill: hop your frame my lad! <i>(1918,Book of the C1 Camp, Tauherenikau,7</i>)

>  <b>BOLiTHO</b> ...not so much lead-swinging, come on, hop your frames. <i>(1918,Book of the C1 Camp, Tauherenikau,35</i>)

>  <b>DAViN</b> Got fed up ... so hopped my frame to Cairo for a spell. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,209</i>)

### hop the bags
 
 <i>phr.</i> go over the top (WW1)

>  Points of order flew about like shrapnel, and amendments, amendments to amendments and notices of further amendments were reminiscent of the barrage which descended before we "'opped the bags." <i>(1939,Warriors in Mufti,13</i>)

>  Many, nay most, New Zealand infantrymen "hopped the bags" in better heart when they knew that their own gunners were supporting them... <i>(1919,Dial Sights,[11}</i>)

>  Toasted their health while he vowed to fight, in the good old `Digger' way,/ And then `hopped the bags' with the photo fair of the girl in the estaminet. <i>ibid.</i> 78 `We're just out training for another little stunt.' `Stunt! What's that?' `Hopping the bags.' `Hopping the bags? is that `going over the top' - an attack?' `Yes.' <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,69</i>)

>  They didn't scrap with shells and bombs and tanks, / Nor did they hop the bags in serried ranks; / <i>(1919,College Rhymes,35,1923 ed.</i>)

### hop to it
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hop to it act quickly, command so to do; eg `Come on now, you lily-livered bunch of nancy boys, when i say the word, hop to it!' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,58</i>)

### hop up and down
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hop up and down agitated; eg `When the radio in the TAB went on the blink, the solitary punter with $100 on the nose began to hop up and down like a flea in a fit.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,58</i>)

### hop-spree
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> in the first decades of this century, when i was a boy in Waimea, not a school tea party (an all-district event) went by without playing Bingo and/or Drop the Handkerchief. These were also the usual games at `hop-sprees' and end-of-picking payouts where there was ginger-pop or better. [Waimea West: 1910] <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,168</i>)

### Hope disease
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a potentially-fatal disease of cattle and sheep, caused by cobalt deficiency.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> in the North island, under the name of 'bush sickness', it was found in the pumice shower area of the central plateau, while in the South island in Southland it was known as 'Morton Mains' disease, and in Nelson as 'Hope' disease. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,146</i>)

### hophead
 
 <i>n.</i> A person fond of liquor; drunkard; alcoholic (but not used unkindly), cf hop @n.

>  Private Harry Hophead <i>(Aug 17, 1942,2NZEF Times,16</i>)

>  <b>LATHAM</b> 2nd Race: HOPHEAD HURDLES Scone Doer by Demon Dan out of Good Temper Nightmare by Grim Dig out of Odd Noggin <i>(1986,WAAC Story, The,174</i>) [NOTES: 1944 Sergeant's Mess Racing Club, Bari, `Rules' in iris Latham The WAAC Story (1986) 174]

>  <b>BRUNO</b> <i>(1948,Maleesh George,31</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> it's Betty that can't hold the liquor.... She's a real lily of a hophead. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,201</i>)

>  Among young people greetings like `Hophead', `Ram', `Burglar', `Sheik', `Stopout', are accepted as flattery: `burglar' i heard only in the army. <i>(Sep 1952,Landfall,208</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers')]

>  <b>TURNER</b> in New Zealand, the term bash for a party where drink is the chief attractionis heard (or was, some years ago among Wellington students) and hophead for a person who drinks regularly. The term hophead is (or was) used in convivial greetings, `Hello, hophead', the implication that the person addressed was a persistent drinker being equivalent to calling him a decent joker. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,132</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Basil said hell here's those hopheads again. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,14</i>)

>  <b>MORRiESON</b> ...he was a dead beat old hop-head <i>(1974,Predicament,73</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Hordes of hopheads in paper hats prancing along Main Street throwing crackers and confetti. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,176</i>)

>  <b>MacKENZiE</b> hop-head an alcoholic <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,97</i>)

>  hophead n. NZ drunkard. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,360</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hophead drunkard and/or crazy person. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,58</i>)

### hopo
 
 <i>n.</i> a bird

>  ... the most important [Chatham island] edible sea-birds: AS WRiTTEN AS PRONOUNCED ENGLiSH hopo ho[macron]p' albatross hakoakoa ha[macron]ko[macron]k' mutton bird taiko tchai[macron]k' pied shear water tupoupou tchu[macron]poup' pied blue billie <i>(1928,Bishop Museum Memoirs,Vol. iX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 358</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Within minutes of play starting Hopoate was written indelibly and ever so elegantly into the language, A quite bad shot brought the threat, with the appropriate gesture, of a one-finger hopo. A rank short bowl was a two-finger hopo, an appalling misjudgment of a shot a three-finger hopo...Then the skip... looked stricken, turned ashen-faced and disappeared at unusual speed into the club-house - proof-positive the hopo is here to stay as the ultimate deterrent to poor play in any sport. <i>(Apr 5, 2001,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  At least all this made Trevor Mallard's John Hopoate-like gesture toward world rugby boss Vernon Pugh look comparatively trivial. <i>(May 4, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

### hopped out
 
 <i>adj.</i> drunk

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hopped out drunk; Gordon Slatter's A Gun in My Hand <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,58</i>)

### hopper
 
 <i>n.</i> Orig. cheese-hopper; applied by bacon-handlers to the maggots which breed in bacon; and more widely to any sort of maggot (from blow-flies in meat): from ca1910 (WHBO).



 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  TREVALLY (a) Scientific name: <i>Caranx lutescens</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Trevally; New Zealand Pompano. (c) Other common names: Hoppers (fish in uncleaned state). (d) Principal localities where taken: [Largely confined to northern waters and south to Cook Strait]. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,48</i>)

>  [species name] trevally .. [scientific name] <i>Caranx georgianus</i> .. [other name(s)] hoppers, jackfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [Cf dipper.]

>  He was what is termed in mining phraseology, a `hatter', - meaning one who works by himself. ... Alone and unaided he scooped the wash-dirt into his cradle, which he vigorously rocked with his left hand, whilst with the right he ladled water from the river into the `hopper' or seive [sic]. ... He cast aside the `dipper', and hastened towards the eddy. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/vii/95,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> Hoppers: Storage bins for ore both on the surface and underground. The stamp hoppers held sufficient crushed ore for twenty-four hours' operation. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),324</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary of Waihi Mining Terms)]

### hopple
 
 <i>n.</i>

### hoppy
 
 <i>n.</i> hopscotch [(children's) R.B. McLuskie says it was used by children on the West Coast ca1930-35 eg play hoppy; cf skippy.]

### hops
 
 <i>n.</i> (often the hops) alcohol, esp. beer [>1940-41 HMT's 23 and 23A. 22 Westralian hops may be inferior to good old Enzed]

>  <b>Sargeson</b> He was keeping strictly off the hops. <i>(1946,That Summer,34</i>) [NOTES: >1940-41 HMT's 23 and 23A. 22 Westralian hops may be inferior to good old Enzed]

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> They're a bit on the emotional side, this being a side effect of the hops, and ... they could go either way. <i>(1979,Skin Deep,146</i>)

>  hop (in pl.) NZ sl. beer. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,360</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### horahora
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Horahora. <i>Astelia Solandri</i>, A. Cunn. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/50</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

### horehound
 
 <i>n.</i> (occas.) biddy-bid.

>  <b>HiLGENDORF</b> HOREHOUND (<i>Marrubium vulgare</i>), is actually called piri piri and bidi bid in some localities. it is common in waste places and sheep camps in both islands. it is a perennial, growing one to two feet high,. . . <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,151,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>horehound [s] Marrubium vulgare </i>L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,38</i>)

 <i>n.</i> CURNOW'S CURSE (<i>Calandrinia ciliata</i> var.<i> caulescens</i>); portulaca

>  <b>HiLGENDORF & CALDER</b> CURNOW'S CURSE (<i>Calandrinia ciliata </i>var.<i> caulescens</i>) is also called portulaca, horgan's weed, and purple calandrinia. it has spreading fleshly stems and somewhat fleshy leaves. <i>(1967,Weeds of New Zealand,78,nf. 1926 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Horgan's weed [=] calandrinia [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,38</i>)

### hori
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  instead of hiding it, or changing, the comedian has decided to put it out there. His show, featuring tales of pakeha, hangi, barbecues, Maori and bad language, is a mix of things Kiwi, things hori. <i>(May 15, 2002,Capital Times,9</i>)

>  "..and they take me on tour of the most "hori", to use the local vernacular, of their two Hiaces. <i>(Jun 5, 2005,Sunday Star Times,A2</i>)

>  "Look at us. Our appearance is pretty hori," laughs frontman Laughton. <i>(Oct 14, 2005,NZ Herald,B5</i>)

>  it's wrong i know, but i'm getting that sick of being ripped off by the blimmin' hori boys. <i>(Dec 6, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> Along the Desert Road that Hori horse he never stop;/ Only once, to get some kai/, ... <i>(1974,Ballads of a Racegoer,9</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> RANGi: ... That finish the palaver? JiMMY: You filthy Hori creep. You rotten, slimy, greasy bum! <i>(1974,Hand on the Rail, The,32</i>)

>  `All right, you hori hooer! Send us one [frozen carcase] down for a try. ... if i get one of those big hooers up my arse, you'll get one of my size nine kadoodies up yours! Oh, i'll root you a bewdy with me size nine kadoody.' <i>(1979,islands,27/461</i>) [NOTES: McGee (Out in the Cold)]

>  <b>O'SULLiVAN</b> JACKO: That hori bloke. Bed next to yours. TiNY: Tai? <i>(1985,Shuriken,23</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> He's been telling hori-stories for as long as i can remember. Heh Hori, you been out of the district a year and now your wife's expecting them again, how's that Hori? <i>(1974,Maori Woman,246</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a form of address or nickname for an unknown Maori, cf George.

>  <b>BAUME</b> `Hori', [the driver] said to Paul (as a negro is `Sambo' a Maori is Hori to the poor white). `You like a drink, eh?' <i>(1933,Half Caste,26</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The Maori's a lot of things ... [for example] Dectective: i'd like to speak to you outside, Hori. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,99</i>)

>  <b>FRANCES</b> Some of them called him Johnny, others called him Hori. They called him Hori because they did not know what else to call him : impersonally, without malice, in the same way they called each Chinaman John. <i>(1964,Johnny Rapana,84</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Clarry bristled, `Don't lay odds on that one, Hori!' `Aw, you're full of Pakeha bullshit,' said a heavily built Maori ... <i>(1982,Wild Pig,89</i>)

>  ... tucked away with the other ephemera from the sixties, `Uncle Ho never called me Hori', and all the rest of it, ... <i>(1984,Landfall,150/134</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> Are all you Hori's related <i>(1944,Troop Target,89</i>)

>  <b>DAViN</b> But i dare say, there'll be some left over for us and the Horis [the Maori Battalion] <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,252</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> `Well, i suppose we can undress in the car if you ... get rid of the old hori,' said the [Pakeha] woman. <i>(1947,Brown Man's Burden,73,1973 ed.</i>) [NOTES: `Johnny Wairua's Wonderland']

>  it is the arrogance of the American labelling other peoples `gooks' and `wops': the New Zealand soldier had his `Wogs' and `ities' and `Teds', at home there are the `Chinks' and `ikes' and `Dallies' and even the `Horis'. <i>(Sep 1852,Landfall,220</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers')]

>  i would include among New Zealand English words: hori <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> `He's off a farm, your husband?' `Him and me both. All horis come off the farm, i think.' <i>(1960,Maori Girl,219</i>)

>  <b>MUiR</b> [spg Horry] `What about the Yanks? it's got Maoris in it, hasn't it? They love that sort of thing. Grass skirts and tits.' ... `You've got to drag the Horrys into a New Zealand book these days. People expect it.' <i>(1960,Word for Word,42</i>)

>  The use of the word `Hori' was becoming distasteful said ... Mr H.W. Herewini, a former officer of the 28 Maori Battalion ... addressing the annual conference of the New Zealand Returned Services Association ... appealing to the association to act as a spearhead to create better race relations in New Zealand.... As an example of deteriorating race relations ... [he] instanced the use of the word `Hori'. `That word is becoming distasteful to many - but the Kiwi and the Maori let it go because we know we don't mean it in a distasteful way.' <i>(Jun 20, 1963,Evening Post</i>) [NOTES: or Dominion - check]

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> SOME MAORi WORDS ... hori equivalent of `nigger' <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,192</i>)

>  <b>FRANCES</b> `What's being a Maori got to do with it?' Taku demanded loudly. `We're all horis. Whether we're up there or down here, we're still horis. What difference does that make?' <i>(1964,Johnny Rapana,41</i>)

>  <b>HARVEY</b> `You callin' us Maori fellas nigger, heh?' Some Wellington people and happy soldiers shouted, `Belt 'im, Hori' and `Teach 'im t' mind 'es own interference' and things like that. <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,41</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> Maoris were less sophisticated then [c 1920] than now, and the story is told of Hori, straight from the pa, who had never travelled by rail before ... [The train entered a tunnel and came out.] With the resilience of his kind, Hori beamed ... and remarked with relief - `by korry ... tomorrow, eh?' spg Hori (used by a South islander) <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,97</i>)

>  What has happened to that open-handed, good natured, tolerant fellow so well-known here and overseas as the happy hori. <i>(Sep 26, 1972,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  <b>MiDDLETON</b> `Here! Hold on Hori! Not so fast! You know you dropped that bottle. ...None of your lip Hori!' the tall [policeman] said sharply. <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,197</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a non-individualised name for Maori.

>  A sophisticated hori, as he says. And this hori is hungry. <i>(Oct 22, 2006,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),26</i>)

>  in the matter of deep water diving Hori has little in common with his relations in the South Sea islands. <i>(Jul 11, 1921,Quick March,26</i>)

>  Civility costs nothing, but you mustn't let Hori think you're soft, or he'll try to put one over you. <i>(Sep 1952,Here & Now,29</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [1950s as a stage name for Maori dupe. [cf McCallum Half-Gallon Jar] ca1963 Wellington p.c. Brian Findlay, and William Maughan usual pron. [h ri:] `horrie' as in a contemporary song catchphrase `Morrie the Horrie.']

>  Through the night he relentlessly takes the piss out of Maori sterotypes, proclaiming himself to be a "contemporary hori" ... <i>(May 8, 2005,Sunday Star Times,E7</i>)

>  Cue well-worn images of feckless horis packing the pubs on benefit day, dance-dance-dancing to their 10 guitars. <i>(Mar 22, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  [He] was caught in a subtle double bind where if clients did want obvious Maoris they were Maoris who wouldn't upset the <i>hori</i> stereotype - the very stereotype that prevents the wider use of Maori to begin with. "i didn't want to supply Maori who would be used as caricatures." <i>(Nov 12, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> The overheard remark still rankled: `The horis, they can live on pipis.' <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,24,1973 ed.</i>)

>  Eight husky Horis. <i>(Mar 1942,National Education,59</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> [WW2, italy] The Maori mule team was camped just across a gully from us, and ... one of their chaps must have sneaked across, taken one of our quiet mules ... i reckon those Hori's [sic] are still laughing over that. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,98</i>)

>  <b>FiN LAYSON</b> hori: contemptuous or self-deprecating name for a Maori; literally, George. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,140</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson's Glossary)]

>  <b>MASON</b> MiRiAM [a European]: You're the first Hori i've ever danced with. HiNGAWARU, stiffly: Say Maori, please. MiRiAM: What's the matter? You sensitive? H---: Yes. To that. <i>(1974,Hand on the Rail, The,9</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> The `Digger': `One pair, Hori?' `No, by korry, just the one trouser i want ...' <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,112</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> `You fight Nazis, too, eh Hori?' `Py corrie, yes.' <i>(1943,Passage to Tobruk,23</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [1984 17 M E114Pakuranga Coll. 11 HORI X 3 (FROM BILLY T. JAMES)]

>  Ah yes, Anika Moa, the cheeky and friendly maori girl who writes those beautiful songs, swears like a trooper and is a self-confessed hori. <i>(Jul 16, 2005,Time Out,10</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> ... this girl couldn't be more than twenty. And what a hat! Who but a hori would wear it? <i>(1974,Maori Woman,10</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> hori: A patronizing name applied to a Maori. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,53</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> Mr Waru [in 1981] elected to make the action of the play contemporary with its production .... The term `Hori' for Maori, for example, was deemed old-fashioned, ... <i>(Oct 1981,Hand on the Rail, The,Preface</i>)

>  <b>SANDYS</b> `Tuaka's no more than a good-for-nothing, loud-mouthed drunkard. if the rest of you horries,' he pointed his knife at the Maori, `can't see what he's up to, ...' <i>ibid.</i> 23 `Wiri! You lazy horrie! it's gone 3. Get your ass into gear.' <i>(1982,Love and War,17</i>)

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> `All you Horis look alike.' said Lua. `So do yous Coconuts.' <i>(1982,Other Halves,132</i>)

>  <b>O'SULLiVAN</b> the Mormons' Temple ... / it'll pick at Heaven's lock / for Hories, ... / <i>ibid.</i> 28 ... Skull worked with the Hories / who were O.K. so long as their tight-arse worries / over land rights and such didn't throw them in flurries / <i>(1982,Rose Ballroom, The,12</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> in those days racism was more blatant than it is now. ... i had become accustomed to being addressed as `snow', `darky', and even `Hori', often by adults who would have been surprised had they known they were giving offence. And at high school Stuart was known as Sam (short for Sambo). But `nigger' was the ultimate, the unforgivable epithet. <i>(1984,island to island,93</i>) [NOTES: [c1939]]

>  <b>ROSNiER-JONES</b> Emma stiffens at the word `hori', but surely he's not referring to . . .[sic] `Emma has got Dally in in her y'know, Fred,' the manager says. `Coulda fooled me. Looks like the old tar-brush t'me.' <i>(1985,Cast Two Shadows,129</i>)

>  We all roared at Billy T. James' irish joke. Could we have more please - about coconuts, dallies, poms and horis? Chows too, if possible. <i>(Jun 15, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),A8</i>)

>  The work `Pakeha' is a Maori word that is being increasingly used in a derogatory way against Europeans, and should be banned from use on radio and television and in the newspapers. its usage should be allowed only if the word `Hori' is similarly sanctioned and used by Europeans when speaking of the Maori people in radio, television and newspapers. <i>(Jul 11, 1986,Dominion,11</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDSON</b> When i was little and at school there'd been that new kid in the playground, trying to be smart. `Hey, you're a Maori. You're a Hori.' <i>(1986,Choices,79</i>)

>  is this genuine or is he just giving me the dumb `i'm just an ignorant hori' act? <i>(Sep 1986,Metro,34</i>)

>  hori n. (R) derogatory name used by some pakehas for a Maori. [M form of George] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,360</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 236]]

>  hori mildly derogatory term for a Maori, usually a male one; literally Maori transcription of `George', one of a number of excuses offered a few years ago to explain the use of the word in Parliament, without however cutting any ice. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,58</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [ca1950 Athletic Park Maoris v. Britain rugby game `Come on the Horis' a frequent cry from Maori spectators. 1951 (Christchurch) Waiarapa v. Canterbury rugby game `Up the horis' etc.]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The name `Hori', used [in Egypt] back and forth freely, implies no inferiority whatsoever, a generalisation like `Fred' or `mate'. inferiority is in the eye of the beholder. After the war, some weird specimens are pretty good at spreading inferiority. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,262</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> He laughs bitterly. `i'm a typical hori after all, made to work on the chain, or be a factory hand, not try for high places. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,240</i>)

>  <b>ROSNiER-JONES</b> `Same father, different mother,' Tui tells Emma. `That must be the hori coming out in me,' Tui laughs up at Uncle Whetu. <i>(1985,Cast Two Shadows,44</i>)

>  (John C. Mcab) Brown and white there were no differences between the colours then [in WW2]. The happy members of the 28th Battalion referred to themselves as `horis' (i've never known what the word meant, but now to use it is as big an insult as for a white to be called a Pakeha), and spoke of `Maori time' (some time) and `Maori PT' (lying on one's bedroll) and we followed their lead, not knowing that they, and we, were using such appallingly insulting terms to the Maori race that a special Race Relations Conciliator would have to be installed to quell them. <i>(Jul 1, 1988,Dominion,10</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Maude turns to Tui. `How are you?' `Kapai. Ke te pai.' Tui presses noses with the old lady, then Maude. ... `Whenever i'm out here in the wop-wops with youse jokers, the hori comes out in me. Kapai and hongi..' <i>(1985,Cast Two Shadows,88</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BOOTH</b> What a terrible time they are having with those island horis up north. <i>(1964,Footsteps in the Sea,124</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HELMER</b> `This little hori's damned hungry,' Crump said, lifting the tea billy from the dingle stick. `i could scoff a real binder, just quietly.' <i>(1964,Stag Party,80</i>)

### Hori
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori [Maori Hori[o-macron], alteration of George]. it was often used as a stage-Maori name, or as a general term of reference or address to an un-named Maori. Unlike Hongi, it was not at first so objectivable as used by English speakers (but cf the 1933 quotation). it is used by Maoris among themselves humorously or sardonically; cf George, hongi @n. (From Maori rendering of English George, formerly fairly common as a name among Maoris, esp. during reigns of Kings George iii-Vi; cf.) 1a. a particular Maori named Hori [(informal, offensive in non-Maori use) Spg horri, horry; pl often.]

>  'Would you believe it, the old fart's decided that ivan's an ignorant Hori, and a heathen, and he'll have to turn [Catholic] before he can cross our doorstep.' <i>(2007,Come the Harvest,155</i>)

>  They just don't like it because there was a hori up front. <i>(Jul 15, 1998,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  [Caption] "How did the scrap go, Hori?" "She nearly as good as Saturday night in Ruatoria, eh." <i>(Jun 7, 1943,NZEF Times,ii/102 : 11</i>)

>  "Some of the most broken down horis i know vote National," he [sc. Tamihere] says. <i>(Jun 23, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C14</i>)

>  TVNZ said "Poms" was a term used affectionately by Kiwis and Australians, and it rejected the complainants' view that the word was comparable to terms such as "Chinks", "Wogs", or "Horis". it agreed expressions such as "Pommy bastards" and "whingeing Poms" were terms of abuse. Authority chairman Sam Maling noted the Human Rights and Equal Opportunity Commission's ruling that the words "Pom" and "Pommy" were unlikely to offend of insult. <i>(Mar 24, 1999,Dominion,1</i>)

>  i had seen them as so tall and clean. But in that situation - they were just a bunch of horis. <i>(Oct 1998,Mana,No. 24: 46</i>)

>  i think we're called horis because so many of us are called Hori meaning George. And the reason so many of us are called Hori meaning George is that we're all named after the British Royal Family.." <i>(Oct 23, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  <b>ihimaera</b> 'All yours, Hori,' he said to Turei. Bad move that, calling Turei a Hori. <i>(2000,Uncle's Story, The,58</i>)

>  <b>WiLKiNS</b> And the biggest plus for our Horis was that the most popular teacher of the decade was this Hori, who actually made you forget that about him. <i>(1990,Veteran Perils,38</i>)

>  During the war, as Kiwi soldiers slogged through North Africa and italy, the services newspaper wrote stories about "horis" - Maori soldiers. One of them, future race relations conciliator Harry Dansey, protested, and asked them not to use the word because it was derogatory. Only joking, they said. <i>(Apr 29, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),102</i>)

>  Poor old Hori, the top politicians of the time said to one another in the cigar smoke-filled rooms of their gentlemen's clubs, he can't do anything for himself. <i>(Jan 26, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C7</i>)

>  <b>CRUiSE</b> [Te Uru-ti or Kingi Hori] <i>(1823,Journal,77 ff.,1957 ed.</i>)

>  ) ...[sic] the Chief Justice [Sir G. Arney] opened the criminal court with an address or charge to us ...[sic] ... i was a good deal disappointed that in speaking of Hori (the Maori or halfcaste prisoner's) case he did not say whether they were British subjects or not ... Dr Hope left the camp at Tatara without his watch (which was afterwards found on Hori) and [ampersand] went back for it ... <i>(Jul 2, 1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/53,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: A.S. Atkinson]

>  THE HALF-CASTE HORi SENTENCED TO BE HANGED. ... Hori, the half-caste, who was supposed to be concerned in the murder of the escort ... has been sentenced to be hung. <i>(Jul 18, 1864,Otago Witness,2</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a policeman; (?exciseman). [(rare)]

 <i>n.</i> <i>Bovichthys variegatus</i>

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> THORNFiSH or HORNY <i>Bovichtus variegatus roseopictus</i> Hutton This beautifully marked and coloured fish is sometimes called Horny by boys. Both the common names are derived from the sharp spines which puncture the flesh of one's hands when taking the Thornfish from a net. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,299,(2nd edn) ed.</i>)

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> THORNFiSH (Horny, dragonet) <i>Bovichtus variegatus (Bovichthys variegatus)</i> ... The thornfish is a small, cylindrical fish with scaleless body ... There is a distinctive strong spine directed upward and backward on each gill cover behind the eyes. ... Thornfish are common in tidepools ... from the Wellington area southwards ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,274,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Thornfish Bovichthys variegatus</i> Family Bovichthyidae (thornfishes). Sometimes called horny, or dragonet (which is the name of a similar but unrelated fish). Also occurs off south-east Australia. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,117</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] horny [TS] 1861-3 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### horoeka
 
 <i>n.</i> [Early spg <i>Hayroiicar</i>. See lancewood [Maori].]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> There is a yellow wood called Hayroiicar [<i>horoeka</i>]. i have a walking stick of this wood, Twists like a Creeper. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),35,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> <i>Horoeka, (aralia Zelandica</i>), is generally found on elevated lands, grows to the height of thirty feet, leaves dark green ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/399</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i>Aralia crassifolia</i>, Banks et Sol. ... Nat. names, `Horoeka', Cunn.; `Hohoeka', Middle island, Lyall. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 96</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Horoeka, <i>Col[enso]. Panax crassifolia</i>... [?Otago pron from Hector] Horoweeka, <i>Hector. Panax</i>, various sp. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  Horoeka (<i>Panax crassifolium</i>). A singular-looking plant in all stages of its growth. <i>ibid.</i> 1/iii/43 Horeka. <i>Panax crassifolium</i>, Dcne. and Planch. <i>(1869,TrNZi</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Of our shrubs and smaller timber trees, several are of strikingly beautiful growth ... (e.g.) the Houhere, <i>Hoheria populnea </i>...; the Horopito <i>Drimys axillaris </i>; the Manuka-rau-riki, <i>Leptospermum ericoides </i>; the Kohuhu, <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium </i>; the Kowhai, especially the small-leaved mountain variety <i>Sophora tetraptera </i> var.: <i>grandiflora</i>; the Koromiko-taranga, <i>Veronica </i>, several species; the Mairehau, <i>Phebalium nudum </i>; the Toro, <i>Persoonia Toro </i>; the Pukapuka, <i>Brachyglottis repanda </i>; the Northern Maire, <i>Santalum Cunninghamii </i>; the Tawari, <i>ixerba brexioides </i>; the Tipau, <i>Myrsine Urvillei </i> and <i>M. salicina </i>; the Tangeao, <i>Tetranthera calicaris </i>; the Ramarama, <i>Myrtus bullata </i>; the Ti, <i>Cordyline australis </i>; the Kahikomako, <i>Pennantia corymbosa </i>; the Pate, <i>Schefflera digitata </i>; the Horoeka, <i>Panax crassifolia </i>: and, on the sea-coast, ... the Karo, <i>Pittosporum crassifolium </i>; and the truly evergreen Ngaio, <i>Myoporum laetum </i> ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/5</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  Panax longissimum Horoeka <i>(1870,TrNZi,2/122</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>HAY</b> [?Horoeka : Hay uses <i>Lancewood</i> for a cabbage tree] The Ohoeka (<i>Panax crassifolium</i>) is a small shrub-like tree ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/195</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Panax crassifolium</i>, Dcne. and Planch. Horoeka, ivy Tree.--An ornamental, slender, and sparingly branched tree. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,127</i>)

>  Amongst smaller trees and shrubs the most prominent are the horoeka (<i>Panax crassifolia</i>), and its congeners <i>Panax simplex</i> and <i>P. edgerleyi</i>, both exhibiting such excessive luxuriance as frequently to require an examination of the fruit before their identification can be determined; the rautawhiri (<i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>, var. <i>colensoi</i>), the kotukutuku (<i>Fuchsia excorticata</i>), the mako (<i>Aristotelia racemosa</i>), the mapau (<i>Myrsine australis</i>), the pokako (<i>Eloeocarpus hookerianus</i>), also <i>Carpodetus serratus, Myrsine divaricata</i>, both of which are abundant. The remarkable `weeping tree', <i>Myrsine montana</i>, with its crowded recurved branches and small foliage, affords a picturesque effect <i>(1884,TrNZi,17/216</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  [Native name] Horoeka .. [Settlers' name] Lancewood .. [Family] Araliaceae [Native name] Horoeka .. [Settlers' name] Toothed lancewood .. [Family] Araliaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  Horoeka (grass tree) .. Pseudopanax crassifolium .. Timber dense and compact, and has been used for piles and fencing posts; suitable for ornamental work <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,9</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> Horoeka (grass tree) .. Pseudopanax crassifolium .. Timber dense and compact, and has been used for piles and fencing posts; suitable for ornamental work <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,235</i>)

>  <b>LANG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Pseudopanax crassifolium [(The Lancewood)]</i> ... Maori name <i>Horoeka. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,306,1940, P. 324 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> They cut the yellow twisted horoeka/ For sticks .../ <i>(1937,Selected Poems,37,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <i>horoeka</i>, a small tree (Pseudopanax crassifolium), lancewood. in young tree long, thick, stiff leaves turned down like ribs of an umbrella. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/151</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>horoeka n.</i> small round-headed NZ tree. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
 <i>n.</i>

### horopito
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Drimys axillaris</i> a small, slender, evergreen tree with aromatic bark. Also called pepper tree. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The kitchen gets marks for initiative in sourcing ingredients, having somehow found somebody to supply piko piko, horopito and other New Zealand bush herbs, some of which, the waitress told me, had gone into the Hubba Hubba spice rub for the salt water prawns and calamari. <i>(Aug 15, 2002,Dominion Post,D3</i>)

>  Horopito (Pseudowintera) was used for skin and venereal diseases and Mother Aubert, in a letter to the French Consul, wrote that a chemist at the University of Paris considered her medicine Karana, which contained horopito, was "superior to Quinquina (quinine). it has been very useful to me in cases of anaemia, of debility, of continuous diarrhoea etc, and in recovery from temperatures." <i>(Aug 24, 2002,Dominion Post,F16</i>)

>  <i>horopito n</i>. NZ pepper-tree. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,361</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Kawakawa is sometimes called `native pepper tree' because of its hot tasting leaves, and it is in fact related to the true pepper plant of indonesia .. Horopito (<i>Pseudowintera axillaris</i>) with its dark green shiny leaves has also been termed `pepper tree' for the same reason, but it is not in fact related to the kawakawa. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,103</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> A decoction of the leaves [sc. of Horopito or Drimys axillaris] is not uncommonly used by bushmen and others to allay pain and is honoured with the name of "Maori Painkiller". <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,13</i>)

>  Similarly, horopito (New Zealand pepper tree) leaves acquired red blotches when they were grazed by insects. <i>(Nov 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 60/62</i>)

>  Pork loin is rubbed with horopito and roasted in flax with kumara puree. Horopito is a native wild pepper made from a dried leaf with a pungent smell. <i>(Jun 25, 2003,Capital Times,18</i>)

>  6 Lithograph of horopito (pepper tree) foliage. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers</i>)

>  More recently .. He launched tea made from kawakawa (native pepper) and horopito, - both labelled as 'organically grown in nuclear-free New Zealand'. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,116</i>)

>  As he ate his pork loin rubbed with horopito (native wild pepper) and roasted in flax with sweet potato pure, Mr Tamihere recalled how nothing at all was allowed to interfere with the flavour of roast pork when it appeared on his family table as a child. <i>(Jun 21, 2003,Dominion Post,F6</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> He hit a young horopito and it shot him across into a bunch of lawyer like a dirty great coil of winch-rope. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,189</i>) [NOTES: 1970 edition]

>  Horopito, which has a chilli pepper bite and kawakawa, also known as the pepper tree or bush basil, are dried and used as rubs and seasonings. <i>(Jul 27, 2005,NZ Herald,3,ViVA ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> .. the air in the neighbouring woods was rendered quite fragrant with the <i>horopito</i> ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/23</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i>Drymis axillaris</i> , Forster .. Nat. name `Horopito', Colenso <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 12</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Horopito, <i>Col[enso]. Drimys axillaris.</i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  Of our shrubs and smaller timber trees, several are of strikingly beautiful growth .. (e.g.) the Houhere, <i>Hoheria populnea </i>.. the Horopito <i>Drimys axillaris </i>; the Manuka-rau-riki, <i>Leptospermum ericoides </i>; the Kohuhu, <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium </i>; the Kowhai, especially the small-leaved mountain variety <i>Sophora tetraptera </i> var.: <i>grandiflora</i>; the Koromiko-taranga, <i>Veronica </i>, several species; the Mairehau, <i>Phebalium nudum </i>; the Toro, <i>Persoonia Toro </i>; the Pukapuka, <i>Brachyglottis repanda </i>; the Northern Maire, <i>Santalum Cunninghamii </i>; the Tawari, <i>ixerba brexioides </i>; the Tipau, <i>Myrsine Urvillei </i> and <i>M. salicina </i>; the Tangeao, <i>Tetranthera calicaris </i>; the Ramarama, <i>Myrtus bullata </i>; the Ti, <i>Cordyline australis </i>; the Kahikomako, <i>Pennantia corymbosa </i>; the Pate, <i>Schefflera digitata </i>; the Horoeka, <i>Panax crassifolia </i>: and, on the sea-coast .. the Karo, <i>Pittosporum crassifolium </i>; and the truly evergreen Ngaio, <i>Myoporum laetum </i> ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/5</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  Horopita [sic]. Drimys colorata, Raoul. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/39</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Horopito, or `Pepper-tree' (<i>Drymis axillaris</i> ) yields also an ornamental timber. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/195</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Drymis axillaris</i> , Forst. Horopito, Pepper-tree Winter's Bark. -- A small slender evergreen tree, very Handsome. Whole plant aromatic and stimulant. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,129</i>)

>  [Native name] Horopito .. [Settlers' name] Pepper-tree .. [Family] Magnoliaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>FEATON</b> DRiMYS AXiLLARiS .. The `HOROPiTO' .. it is generally known by the colonists as the `Pepper Tree', on account of its highly pungent properties. <i>ibid.</i> 177 Horopito [Maori name] .. Peppertree [Settlers' name] .. Drimys axillaris .. Small tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,12</i>)

>  Horopito (pepper-tree) .. Drimys axillaris .. Leaves and bark aromatic and pungent; wood suitable for inlaying <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,9</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Horopito. Maori name for the New Zealand shrub</i>, Drimys axillaris, Forst., N.O. <i>Magnoliaceae</i>; called also <i>Pepper-tree</i>(q.v.). <i>(1890,Australian & NZ English [TS],203</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> <i>(1847,Savage Life,vol. ii. p. 17</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Drymis axillaris [(The Pepper-tree)] </i>.. A decoction of the leaves is often used by bushmen as a medicine, and has earned the name of `Maori Painkiller.' ... Called by the settlers the Pepper Tree. Maori name Horopito. [The plant is closely related to the South American Winter's Bark.] <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,172,1940 p. 180 ed.</i>)

>  <i>horopito</i>, a small tree (Drimys axillaris), pepper-tree; (an aromatic evergreen in Supplement). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/149</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>~ n</i>. the New Zealand aromatic pepper-tree [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (Cuisine) a pepper flavouring from leaves of the horopito tree. [ORIGIN: Maori, aka bush pepper]

>  Mussels provide a taste of New Zealand, and this soup for all occasions is complete with the unique flavour of horopito (bush pepper) and a sprinkle of manuka-smoked sea salt. .. Add the mussels, spinach leaves and horopito .. Add more horopito to taste. <i>(Mar 21, 2009,Dominion Weekend Magazine,20</i>)

>  [heading] KAi TiME. Maori chef Charles Royal gathers herbs and plants from the bush including pikopiko heads, kawakawa shoots, horopito and piripiri to make dry rubs, relishes and pickles. <i>(2005,AA Directions,Spring 2005, p 54</i>)

>  Flavours include tonic water with horopito (made by blending two separate horopito extractions to produce floral bitters with an aromatic note), bitter lemon with horopito (a blend of real lime juice and horopito essences) and Kiwi cola with kawakawa, a smooth and fragrant drink - great for mixing. <i>(Sep 26, 2010,Sunday  Star Times: Escape,15</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

### horror house
 
 <i>n.</i> = murder house q.v.

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> There is a tendency to refer to the game dentist (and the real school dentist) in an exaggerated manner. They say, `Here's for the butcher ship' or `i'm off to the horror house' or, when receiving their card, `Here's my grave number.' <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,257</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> There is a tendency to refer to the game-dentist (and to the real school dentist) in an exaggerated manner. `Here's for the butcher shop', or `i'm off to the horror house', (Waddington [19]49) or `Here's my grave number.' (Forbury), These games are reported mainly by children of mid-primary years. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,1/382</i>)

### horror-box
 
 <i>n.</i> an abusive epithet [(tramwaymen's) ca1952-1953 p.c. F.M. O'Brien: Wellington tramway workers eg The inspector's a fair old horror-box.]

### horrors
 
 <i>n.</i> the horrors, the DT's, delirium tremens; but widened often to a bad attack of the shakes, joe blakes; dingbats; creeps, etc. [usually in the phrase "in the horrors" ca1945: modern usage widened to a very bad hangover, or to `a fit of horror' (cf P: 2 coll. fr. ca1765 obs. 1860's) `creeps' (ie. that picture gave me the horrors) `first stage of DTs': low coll from ca1859; H. 1860.]

>  The rumour.. that Patterson was in the "horrors" does not appear to be true. <i>(Jan 11, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> [The Whalers] frequently became a prey to delirium tremens or `the horrors' as they aptly call that disease <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ</i>)

>  No old Maori ever had delirium tremens; no old Rangatira ever had the `horrors' - not even `hot coppers'. <i>(1879,TrNZi,12/434</i>)

>  <b>PHiLiPS</b> One day, our [ship's] doctor, who always took a tumbler of rum ... got the horrors <i>(1890,Reminiscences of Early Days,6</i>)

>  We heard he was in the horrors. <i>(1900,Colonial Turf,50</i>)

>  <b>CHAMiER</b> ... that awful condition called the horrors'. <i>(1895,South Sea Siren,85</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> drunk

### horsable
 
 <i>adj.</i>

### horse
 
 <i>v.</i> of a mare, want the stallion

>  <b>SAR GESON</b> [The mare] must have been horsing (the slut!) because she wouldn't budge an inch [when the rig was up her] <i>(1949,i Saw in My Dream,183</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> as a distiguishing epithet in the names of plants and animals often indicating one larger or coarser than the usual variety, see mussel, nettle, plantain; see also horse-mackerel.



 
 <i>n.</i> a large detached mass of rock occurring in a vein [(goldminers')]

>  HORSE.- A large detached mass of rock occurring in a vein. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,54</i>)

>  [Otago] At the place where [the lode] was cut there was a horse of mullock in the centre on the foot-wall side. <i>(1896,AJHR,C-3/107</i>) [NOTES: The Goldfields of NZ]

>  Rising to No. 2 level, a barren block of glassy quartz comes in, and the seamy gold-bearing quartz is pushed over to the two walls, and divided by clay partings from the barren centre block, which corresponds practically to a `horse' of country rock.... [The blocks] are frequently split by `horses' and the stone is generally mottled and seamy. <i>(1909,TrNZi,41/77</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the four legs of a game of forty-fives, see forty-fives

>  <b>CRUMPTON</b> <i>(1979,Spencer: the Gold Seeker,41</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Horse- Slipper or board carrying the template of the various members of a moulding for running plaster cornices and mouldings. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,64</i>)

### horse float
 
 <i>n.</i> a trailer for transporting horses behind cars or trucks. [also as horsefloat; NZ Racing Conference advises 12/6/1998 that "In racing terms a horse float is a large van like a furniture van for transporting a number of race-horses to meeting venues."]

>  The answer may be yes if a boat or a horse float have to be towed. <i>(Jul 4, 1998,Dominion,19</i>)

>  Wanted: double horsefloat. <i>(Mar 22, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,17</i>)

>  Traditionally the province of upper-crust Brits and spoilt toffy-nosed teenage girls, chaffeured \{sic] around the countryside, horse-floats in tow, by indulgent wealthy parents, equestrian sports are enjoying a renaissance, even among the less well-heeled. <i>(Jan 9, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  Tomorrow it's back to her stables, the routine of training, riding early morning work, dealing with the paperwork that being a trainer entails and driving her Detroit diesel horse float to the races. <i>(Nov 11, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A9</i>)

>  <b>DUGGAN in DAViN</b> A horse-float is four and an ambulance is three & em, but i haven't seen one. <i>(1953,NZ Short Stories,158</i>) [NOTES: check quote]

>  Our Kapiti shelter needs:.. A horse float in good condition. <i>(May 1998,Animals' Voice, The,[1]</i>)

### horse mackerel
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Scomber trachurus.</i>

>  The horse mackerel (Trachurus trachurus) or hauture of the Maori, is a fish which has a very wide range, being common to the seas of Britain and New Zealand. <i>(Jan 23, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> KOHERU ... <i>Other names: Decapterus koheru</i>; horse mackerel, scad. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,233</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> HORSE MACKEREL<i> See Mackerel, Horse or Koheru. </i> <i>ibid.</i> 140 KOHERU Commonly-caught inhabitant of our northern coastal waters who is also known as Yellowtail or Horse Mackerel. <i>ibid.</i> 162 MACKEREL ... The common Mackerel is also known as the English Mackerel, Frigate Mackerel or Southern Mackerel. The Horse Mackerel is also known as the Yellowtail. The Koheru is also sometimes known as Yellowtail and is often misnamed Horse Mackerel and Scad. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,121</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE HORSE MACKEREL Trachurus novaezelandiae (Richardson) <i>ibid.</i> 169 Horse Mackerel <i>Trachurus novae-zelandicae</i> Mackerel; Scad <i>ibid.</i> 232 HORSE MACKEREL ... <i>Other names: Trachurus novaezelandiae;</i> mackerel; hauture (Maori). <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,83</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> <i>mackerel, horse</i> hature, hauture <i>Trachurus novae-zelandiae </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family CARANGiDAE. This family contains ... the strongly-scuted herring-scad and horse mackerel ... . <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,221</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> HORSE MACKEREL [<i>Trachurus declivis</i>] ... Mackerel and yellowtail are alternative names. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,48</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>MACKEREL</i> (two species) (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Scomber australasicus </i>(ii) <i>Trachurus declivis</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) Blue Mackerel (ii) Jack mackerel. (c) Other common names: (i) Common Mackerel, Frigate mackerel, English mackerel (ii) Horse Mackerel. (d) Principal localities where taken: (both) Gisborne, Nelson, Picton ... <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,34</i>)

>  [species name] jack mackerel .. [scientific name] <i>Trachurus declivis</i> t. NZ. .. [other name(s)] horse mackerel, horsies, mackerel scads <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  Of the edible [pelagic] fishes ... such as the frostfish, barracouta, horse-mackerel, kingfish, dory, warehou, mackerel, and garfish; while only the ling, hake and haddock ... are among those of more southern types ... <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  <i>Trachurus novae-zelandicae</i> : Horse mackerel. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <i>Trachurus novae-zelandicae</i> (Richardson). Horse mackerel. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/410</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Horse Mackerel <i>Trachurus novae-zelandicae</i> Richardson (Syn. <i>Scomber dimidiatus</i> Richardson, <i>S. clupeoides</i> Richardson, and <i>Caranx sinus-obscuri</i> Richardson) <i>(1955,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,406</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 406]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> HORSE MACKEREL (HATURE) <i>Trachurus novae-zelandiae</i> Richardson <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,237</i>)

>  <b>AYLiNG</b> <i>JACK MACKEREL</i> (Horse mackerel)<i> Trachurus declivis </i>... The other New Zealand mackerel <i>Trachurus novaezelandiae</i> which is indistinguishable from the jack mackerel unless examined closely, has been reported from south-east Asia and Japan as well. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,217,19i84 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] mackerel, horse 1353-89 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> HORSE MACKEREL ... This is the Hauture of the natives, and the Scad of the northern hemisphere (<i>Trachurus trachurus</i>). <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,110</i>)

>  For some days near the beginning of the month a number of Horse Mackerel were brought to the market. <i>(1879,TrNZi,11/385</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>SHERRiN</b> HORSE MACKEREL (<i>Trachurus trachurus</i>). ... This is the hauture of the Natives, and the scad of the Northern Hemisphere. <i>ibid.</i> 61 MACKEREL (<i>Scomber Australasicus</i>). ... This valuable fish is well known as an occasional visitant in Wellington Harbour, being called by the Natives tawatawa. in colour, size, and form it closely approaches the common mackerel of England, and is very superior in delicacy to the scad or horse-mackerel, which is commonly called the mackerel in New Zealand. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,46</i>)

>  <b>PARKiNSON</b> All the coves of this bay teem with fish of various kinds, such as cuttle-fish, large breams, ... small grey breams, small and large barracootas, flying gurnards, horse-mackarel, dog-fish, soles, dabs, mullets, drums, scorpenas or rock-fish, cole-fish, the beautiful fish called chimera, and shags. [in the Stout Collection copy, Victoria University Library, an early 19th C. or late 18th C. hand has added: `*Baracootas, a fish remarkably smooth about 7 or 8 foot long [dagger] Flying Gurnards, a flying fish, of a remarkable fine Gold Colour. [double dagger] Chimeras, a fish of Silver Colour - [triple dagger] Drum fish, so call'd from the noise they make, - ' <i>(Jan 15, 1770,Journal,114</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Capt Cook ... found the fish ... which proved a <i>Horse-Mackrel</i>, [269] a kind of fish we had never caught before. [ed. fn] <i>Scomber trachurus</i> of <i>Descr. Anim.</i>, pp.155, 257 & 453 ... <i>(May 2, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/268</i>)

>  <b>WALES</b> ...there are not wanting Several Sorts [of fish] that are well known; such as Mullet, Cavallas, Guard-Fish, Horse-Mackeral. Amongsyt the crustaceous Tribes we found Craw-fish, Muscles. [sic] Cockles, Scollops, Whelks, Periwinkles, ... <i>(1773,Journal,2/787,1961 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1773 Wales Journal in Cook Journal (1961)]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> The best and most savoury fish was a species of cod, which, from its external colour, our sailors called a coal-fish : besides this we caught several species of excellent flat cavalhas (<i>sciaenae</i>), some scorpens, mullets, horse-mackrel, and many other sorts ... which were entirely unknown in Europe <i>ibid.</i> 1/181 [We] indeed caught some fish [near Long island] which are common to Europe, viz. the horse-mackarel, the greater dog-fish, and the smooth hound.* [fn] Scomber trachurus, squalus canis, & sq. mustelus, Linn. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/126</i>)

>  <b>HODGSKiN</b> Large quantities of the horse mackerel are annually caught, and dried by the natives ... <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,33</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> There are also several other kinds of fish, which are called by the sailors, from some slight resemblance to the English sorts, <i>Herrings, Horse-mackarel, Cole-fish</i>, and <i>Ling</i>. The latter is often taken of the weight of forty pounds. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,161</i>)

>  Quite a lot of Horse Mackerel, <i>Trachurus trachurus</i>, were brought to town in March last,. <i>(1879,TrNZi,11/381</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)..]

>  <b>SENiOR</b> Amongst the sea-fishes fit for food there are ... a number of periodical fishes ... and these include frost-fish. barracuda, horse-mackerel, king-fish, dory, warehou, mackerel, and garfish. <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,288</i>)

>  12. Horse-mackerel - <i>Trachurus trachurus</i>. NUMBER OF FiSH RECORDED - 49 (examined) but immense numbers left stranded on the beaches. LOCALiTY - <i>Milford Sound</i>. <i>ibid.</i> 29. Milford Sound - Horse-mackerel, rock-cod, mullet, red-cod <i>Macrurus australis</i>, pilchard. <i>(1892,TrNZi,1224/204</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  The horse-mackerel and mullet were here all the summer in shoals; <i>(1896,TrNZi,28/53</i>) [NOTES: (Henry)]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Horse-Mackerel, n.</i> The name is applied in Sydney to the fish <i>Auxis ramsayi</i>, Castln., family <i>Scombridae</i>. in New Zealand it is <i>Caranx(or Trachurus) trachurus</i>, Cuv. and Val., which is the same fish as the Horse-Mackerel of England. This is called <i>yellow-tail</i> on the Australian coasts. See <i>Trevally.</i> <i>(1898,Austral English,203</i>)

>  <i>Trachurus trachurus</i>, Linnaeus. The horse-mackerel or scad. <i>(1906,TrNZi,38/550</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  TRACHURUS NOVAE-ZELANDiAE Cuvier and Valenciennes. <i>Horse-mackerel; Hature.</i> <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,iV/117</i>) [NOTES: June Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

### horse paddock
 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  One winter we all played hockey in the horse paddock at the back of the school. <i>(Apr 1998,North & South,33</i>)
