>  <b>MCALLiSTER</b> The north-eastern half of the old school-grounds was the horsepaddock, with a shed for the saddles and bridles deposited by those pupils riding to school over distances averaging 2 and 3 miles. <i>(1976,Old Taranaki,95</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a paddock in which pack, draught, or saddle horses are grazed when not working [adjacent to a homestead, outstation or shepherds' quarters]. [also horse-paddock]

>  T.C.K. and Hughie fencing all day at the horse paddock. <i>(Jan 2, 1879,Tutira Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> .. up the track past the house and on across the horse paddock to the mouth of the bush gully. <i>(1954,On the Hill,17</i>)

>  <b>ORLOWSKi</b> The hospitality of Mr Gent of Whitcomb Station was much appreciated for his allowing us to use the hut and horse paddock. <i>(1976,Horseback Trails of the Southland & Otago,22</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> [Make] a horse-paddock, ram-paddock, ... oats or wheat-paddock, and a good, permanent washing-place. <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,90</i>)

>  <b>T.C. KiERNAN</b> Kite and C.H.S. sledged posts from Tylee's spur and cut some Kohi posts for the horse paddock. <i>(Jan 4, 1878,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1878 Jan 4 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1926 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 122]

>  <b>MANDER</b> He and Jim had fenced in a yard enclosing the stable, and were now fencing a larger area for a horse paddock. <i>ibid.</i> 118 He went through the house, the gardens, the cow paddock, the horse paddock, searching carefully in the little clumps of trees he had left for shelter. <i>(1925,Allen Adair,52,19o71 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRiEVE</b> His well-feigned astonishment that the familiar rails of the horse-paddock were not in that particular corner, placed him in the front rank of equine actors. <i>(1939,Sketches from Maoriland,60,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> They put the gig in the shade of the trees in the hotel paddock and let the pony go in the horse paddock adjoining. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,100,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLAGLAN</b> There i picked him up in his horse-paddock, dead. <i>(1965,Stethoscope & Saddlebags,88</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> The back country runs have quite a vocabulary of their own ... terms like cow paddock and horse paddock and tea-oh and township which, like most of these terms, took on their special use in Australia. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/679</i>) [NOTES: (Wall NZ Speech)]

>  <b>TURNER</b> Before the school bus was common, country schools normally provided a horse-paddock for children who rode to school. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,144</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> Barbara ... dawdled across the horse paddock by the homestead heading for the dusty, noisy, drafting yards. <i>ibid.</i> 11 The girls skirted the plantation as far as the gate and soon entered the homestead horse paddock. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,1</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> Across the lane in a very big house with a long drive, a lodge at the gates, a horse-paddock, carriages, ... <i>(1981,Black Beech & Honeydew,33</i>)

>  Chris gave his mare a slap on the rump and sent her into the horse paddock. <i>(Dec 1986,Marist Messenger,32</i>) [NOTES: (Joy Cowley)]

>  <b>CHRiSTiE</b> it [Tarawera Hotel] nestled on a flat piece of ground between hills devoid of any growth except for a thin line of exotic pines between the hotel and horse paddock. [check quot] <i>(1987,Candles and Canvas,79</i>)

### horse rake
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARR</b> Haymaking. ... The jobs to look for are `cocking up' (with a two or three pronged fork), driving the horse rake (no handrakes are used nowadays) ... loading the drays or sledges when ready ... A `crow' is also employed, whose work consists of passing the fork-fulls thrown up by the carter to the stacker. <i>(1913,Country Work,11</i>)

### horse scoop
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large metal bucket, horse-pulled, used for forming roads and for making dams.

>  <b>TAiT</b> Horse scoops were used to form roads. <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol ii,253</i>)

### horse stinger
 
 <i>n.</i> a large dragonfly [[dateline missing] This is often inappropriately called the `Horse-stinger,' for it does not sting and appears to take no special interest in horses.]

>  <b>SALE</b> i surmise from their size and general colour on the wing that they are probably what some simply call large dragonflies, or others name more colourfully Devil's Darning Needles, or Horse Stingers (or, in Scotland, Flying Adders). <i>(1982,Four Seasons,91</i>)

### horse trap
 
 <i>n.</i> [(Chathams)]

>  Went to the horse trap with Bob in the morning, ... <i>(Feb 6, 1879,Chudleigh Diary,275,1950 ed.</i>)

### horse's mane weed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>horse's mane weed [s] Ruppia megacarpa </i>Mason <i>Ruppia polycarpa</i> Mason <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,39</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & iRWiN</b> RUPPiA MEGACARPA, horse's mane weed. in Lake Ellesmere hundreds of hectares of shallow, slightly brackish water are suited to this species ... The dark fibrous plants ... are compared to horsehair but the stems are coarser and up to a metre long. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,202</i>)

### horse-backing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) riding a saddle-horse for extended periods.

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Few could have handled the horsebacking that went with a job on St James. <i>(1993,Station Country i,53</i>)

### horse-bite
 
 <i>n.</i> a punishing handgrip [ca1935 children's Havelock (HWO), also Taranaki (DEH)]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> Aw Len darling, Jack said with a grin, take it easy. And he brought his hand down smartly on Len's leg, giving him a horse-bite that made him jump and spill his tea; ... <i>(1949,i Saw in My Dream,109,(1974 ed. Rhodes) ed.</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> [of schoolboy `tortures'] `Chinese burns, full nelsons and hamalocks [sic]' (Karori), ... `Squeezing knuckles, horse bight [sic], ...punch in the chocolate which is called the paralyse' (the chocolate is the thigh) (Stockton), ... <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/685</i>)

### horse-collar
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Gilbert saw him ... with his blankets rolled up into the form of a most gigantic horse-collar, after the most approved fashion among swaggers.] <i>(1881,Waitaruna,170</i>)

>  [He] then proceeded forthwith to arrange his blankets, in the form known to the initiated as `horse-collar swag'. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/i/6</i>)

### horse-power
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYSON</b> When the clay was watered three harness bullocks were driven in the ditch as if they were in a `horse-power.' <i>(1937,Thomas,64</i>)

### horse-track
 
 <i>n.</i> a. Bridle-track (ie. track fit only for horse-travel; not, as sheep-track, track made by horses): (cp coach-track, pack-track)

### horsebane
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>horsebane [s] Oenanthe aquatica</i> (L.) Poir. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,39</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Horsehead <i>Rhombocyttus traversi</i> (Hutton) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,401</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 404]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> HORSEHEAD <i>Rhombocyttus traversi</i> (Hutton) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide,183,2nd ed.</i>)

### horses in the stable
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Creamy was even better with the aluminium ones [sc. knuckle bones]. Cutting cabbages, camels, swatting flies, clicks, little jingles, through the arch, goliath, horses in the stable; Creamy mastered them all. <i>(1982,Master of Big Jingles,56</i>)

### horseshoe fern
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Marattia fraxinea</i>, para. [(obsolesc.)]

>  The root grows with irregular-shaped scales, somewhat like a horseshoe, and that is why it is also known as horseshoe fern. Early settlers variously described it as "very indifferently flavoured potato" and "sweet and mealy". <i>(Nov 6, 2004,Dominion Post,E13</i>) [NOTES: (obsolesc.)]

>  <b>FiELD</b> (And) <i>Marattia fraxinea</i> <i>(1890,Ferns of NZ</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Horseshoe-Fern n.</i> name given in New Zealand to the fern <i>Marattia fraxinea</i>, Sm., called in Australia the Potato-Fern. <i>(1898,Austral English,203</i>)

>  <b>WiLLiAMS</b> Parareka, ... <i>Marattia salicina</i>, the horse-shoe fern, etc. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,265</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The bulbs or large scaly bracts of roots at the base of the leaves of ... the horseshoe fern (<i>para-reka</i>: Marattia salicina), ... [100] formed a delicious article of food ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,99</i>)

>  (And) <i>Marattia fraxinea</i> <i>(1916,NZ Association of Nurserymen, Trade Register</i>)

>  (And) <i>Marattia fraxinea</i> <i>(1917,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>)

>  <b>DOBBiE</b> (And) <i>Marattia fraxinea</i> <i>(1921,NZ Ferns</i>)

>  <i>para</i>, a large fern (Marattia fraxinea), horse-shoe fern, from the shape of the tuber; also paraeka, since para was also the name given to the tubers of two orchids, Gastrodia Cunninghamii and Orthoceras strictum; but if there is no distinguishing suffix para is understood to mean the fern-tuber, as in the place-name Kaipara, so called because the well-known ihenga there enjoyed his first meal of that tuber. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/149</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>MARTiN</b> The PARA or HORSESHOE FERN (<i>Marattia salicina</i>) is often spoken of as the king fern, but this name belongs more appropriately to <i>Todea barbara</i>, [check] a close relative of the British royal fern (<i>Osmunda</i>) ... <i>(1961,Flora NZ,116,(1929) ed.</i>)

>  <i>horse-shoe fern n. </i>the New Zealand national emblem. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Marattia salicina</i> Smith Common names: King fern, horseshoe fern Maori name: Para ... The `horseshoes' cut from the rhizome were baked or boiled and were a good remedy for diarrhoea. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,29</i>)

>  horseshoe fern large herbaceous NZ fern. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### horseshoe malfunction
 
 <i>n.</i> a malfunction in a parachute

>  Other parachutists described the trouble as `horseshoe malfunction'. This causes the main parachute to `Roman candle' - to stream from the pack... <i>(Nov 12, 1973,Dominion,1/8</i>)

### horseshoe swag
 
 <i>n.</i> a swag rolled in the shape of a horseshoe; cf. horse-collar.

>  <b>McHUTCHESON</b> He laid it down dogmatically that for comfort and convenience nothing equalled the `horse-shoe' swag.... The horse-shoe won't do in the bush. <i>(1892,Camp-Life in Fiordland,46</i>)

### horsetail
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HiLGENDORF</b> HORSETAiL (<i>Equisetum arvense</i>) is a non-flowering allied plant to the ferns. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,19,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>horsetail, field [s] Equisetum arvense</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,39</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a New Zealand native weed of field and garden.

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> FiELD HORSETAiL (<i>Equisetum arvense</i>). ... This cosmopolitan weed of fields and gardens was first recorded in New Zealand in 1922 near Wanganui. <i>(1980,Weeds of Crops,44</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Equisetum arvense</i>

>  The most significant of these are African clubmoss (<i>Selaginella kraussiana</i>), which occurs widely along stream banks and in damp forest, where it displaces native ferns, mosses and liverworts, and common horsetail (<i>Equisetum arvense</i>), which is an extremely invasive weed of riverbank sites in high-rainfall areas. <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/78</i>)

### horsie
 
 <i>n.</i> horse-mackerel

>  [species name] jack mackerel .. [scientific name] Trachurus declivis t. NZ. .. [other name(s)] horse mackerel, horsies, mackerel scads <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### HortResearch
 
 <i>n.</i> the old Horticultural Division of DSiR

>  ...new and improved types of kiwifruit emanating from HortResearch's breeding programme promise greater profits for growers and novel sensations for consumers, and may eventually see off older varieties... <i>(Jan 2005,NZ Geographic,No. 71/59</i>)

>  Next off the block is likely to be commercial cultivars of actinidia arguta, which produces small, hairless grape-sized fruit. Already sold in Japan as "baby kiwifruit" since 1995, it has been targeted by Hortresearch breeders who want to carry the thin skin, high sugar content and flavour over into new cultivars. <i>(Apr 29, 2004,Dominion Post,C11</i>)

### horuhoru
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Horu-horu, Lindsay. Brassica Rapa. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

### hose
 
 <i>v.</i> to fill jug with beer

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... he got up to go to the bar for a refill. While his jug was being hosed he looked about the crowded clubroom and waved at the men he knew. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,155</i>)

### hose down
 
 <i>v.</i> pour with rain

>  it also rains a lot. Just as we set off from Crow's house at 8.00am it begins to hose down, and never lets up. <i>(Jul 17, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  <b>Williams</b> it's been absolutely hosing down all morning and out on the expansive blank field not even a dicky bird ventures. <i>(1979,Skin Deep,63</i>)

### hose in
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hose in win easily; eg `Free 'n' Easy hosed in, fulfilling the faith of all those punters who had made her odds on favourite for the cup.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,58</i>)

### hose off
 
 <i>v.</i> Usu. as hosed off, cf brassed, cheesed, etc. off.

>  <b>SLATTER</b> People in those damned cloth caps waving rattles and saying innit smashing Charlie. Couldn't go it at all. Hosed me off completely. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,98</i>)

>  Hosed off firemen are fuming over reports that Members of Parliament could soon be picking up a massive 20 per cent pay hike. <i>(Feb 22, 1987,Sunday News,7</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hosed off fed up; perhaps from being hosed with water, just possibly connected with English late-C16 colloquialism `in my other hose', expressing refusal or disbelief. Current usage would be: `was i hosed off when the first decent shower of rain brought down the wooden retaining wall i'd spent a whole day building.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,58</i>)

### hose out
 
 <i>v.</i> to overcome, to swamp

>  <b>HOWiTT</b> He hosed me out completely. it was my first and last game as lock at that level! <i>(1975,NZ Rugby Greats,134</i>)

### hospital
 
 <i>n.</i> [(Otago fishermen's)]

>  <b>POWELL</b> Parts of the Otago trawling grounds are termed `hospitals' by fishermen on account of the large numbers of wounded fish which have received attention from the voracious hag-fish. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,60</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> [The hag fish or blind eel is] in habits even more revolting than the lamprey ... Parts of the Otago trawling grounds are termed `hospitals' by fishermen on account of the large numbers of wounded fish which have received attention from the voracious hag fish. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/897</i>)

### hospital block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a spelling area for large numbers of stock recovering from disease.

>  <b>HiNDMARSH</b> Widespread ill-thrift was general through the area and a hospital block was planned with this application [of cobaltised super.] <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,70</i>)

### hospital paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a spelling paddock where animals recover from disease or illness [usually located close to the homestead or working buildings and with natural shade and water.]

>  There should, in a really affected farm or run, be a secure hospital paddock where no cattle could come nearer than half a mile .. the same hospital paddock might serve for more than one herd, farm or run. <i>(May 22, 1880,Hawera Star,4</i>)

>  .. he refrained from putting any more sheep into the paddock, which he had since learned had been used by his predecessor as a 'hospital' paddock. <i>(Sep 1, 1925,NZ Farmer,1340</i>)

>  Certain paddocks should be set aside as "hospital" paddocks, and every sheep which on examination shows any signs of foot-rot, whether it be in the very early stages or in the advanced stages, should be kept isolated in these "hospital" paddocks. <i>(Jun 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,621</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> Sheep with unsound feet are put through the foot-bath and held handy in the hospital paddock. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,118</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Mustered wethers out of Hospital pdk and put them in Hutchinsons. <i>(Nov 1915,Alexander Mowat Diaries,18</i>)

>  <b>GASCOiGNE</b> The definite cases and the suspects i drove to a field which from now on would be used strictly as a hospital paddock. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,69</i>)

### hospital pass
 
 <i>n.</i> [in ODE/SOED in football sense; note esp. transferred uses below]

>  "it's a classic hospital pass - you get the ball as you're being tackled," he says. <i>(Jul 1, 2003,independent Herald (Wellington),1</i>)

>  She is used to tough assignments but there are those who say that this time she may have been thrown a well-paid hospital pass. <i>(Jul 4, 1998,Dominion,20</i>)

>  Randell has been handed a few hospital passes in his roller-coaster career, most notably the All Black captaincy in 1938 as the fourth choice of then coach John Hart. <i>(Nov 24, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B3</i>)

>  hospital pass: slow pass at rugby which causes injury (rugby union) <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  Stu Wilson ....almost given a hospital pass <i>(Jul 1983,TV1</i>) [NOTES: Commentary on 4th test [Thorne]]

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hospital pass rugby passing of ball to player about to be heavily tackled; eg `When Gary threw Barry a hospital pass with two dirty big Wallaby flankers converging on him, you could hear the collective gasp of 42,000 spectators over the anguished voice of TV commentator Keith Quinn.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,59</i>)

>  Mr Hulme described his seven-week stint as general manager as a `corporate hospital pass' and wants to quell speculation that he should carry the can for imageering's problems [leading to large losses]. <i>(Oct 3, 1988,Dominion,21</i>)

>  "He has just been a custodian who was delivered a hospital pass." <i>(Feb 4, 2003,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  When Lindsay Scott was appointed principal of Petone College four years ago she accepted what another secondary school principal says was "a bit of a hospital pass". <i>(Jun 30, 1998,Dominion,11</i>)

>  Hospital pass: When someone "has a dab".., gets into trouble and instead of taking what's coming to him passes to a team mate who is immediately hit by a barrage of tacklers travelling at the speed of Exocet missiles. <i>(Oct 2, 1999,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  Shipley said taking the leadership two years ago was not a "hospital pass." <i>(Nov 28, 1999,Sunday Star Times,A3</i>)

>  The cast does its best with the limited script and all credit to the actors for the games played on the small stage, especially the finale when it "rains". However, you can't help feeling they have been thrown a hospital pass. <i>(Sep 7, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),19</i>)

>  With the bypass, he was handed what many considered a hospital pass. <i>(Oct 20, 2001,Evening Post,26</i>)

>  By holding on till the election was only a couple of months away, Holland slipped Holyoake a real hospital pass. <i>(Aug 9, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  Fyfe denies he was thrown 'hospital pass" ... Air New Zealand's new chief Rob Fyfe denies he was landed a "hospital" pass from predecessor and Australia-bound Ralph Norris over plans to lay off up to 600 engineers. <i>(Oct 21, 2005,NZ Herald,C1</i>)

>  it might have been smarter for Miss Clark to gather with other world leaders at the Apec economic summit in South Korea, rather than knock herself out receiving an apparent hospital pass. <i>(Nov 19, 2005,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

>  Uncle Haaughk could draw two of his own players toward him before passing a bone crunching hospital pass to both of them, his timing was quite incredible. <i>(May 30, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B15</i>)

>  [He] would like to know the meaning and origin of the term 'hospital pass'. it's a rugby term and refers to a pass to a player about to be (heavily) tackled, and thus liable to be injured. Occasionally, the pass is called a "Hail mary", from a prayer needed to make it successful. <i>(Jan 27, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

>  Rugby is the sport credited with originating the term "hospital pass"... As a soccer player in the UK, i can assure you that this expression was certainly in use in the 60s when i started playing. <i>(Feb 10, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  Did he feel he had been thrown a hospital pass? <i>(Oct 6, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),77</i>)

>  Most people say [local body politics] is a hospital pass - intelligent friends look at me bemused. <i>(Aug 12, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A9</i>)

>  Originally from Ashburton, she was given a "hospital-pass" by the company's owners.. when she took control in 2001. They expected it to be "a very big ask to get the company to grow". <i>(Jun 21, 2003,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

>  it was a real hospital pass - zip down and have a go, son. <i>(May 25, 2004,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>)

>  "When we all got sworn in as ministers, Peter Davis [Clark's husband] said to me [sc. Corrections Minister Matt Robson], 'Gosh, they've given you a hospital pass!'. <i>(Jun 10, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

### hospo industry
 
 <i>n.</i> hospitality industry

>  it is a common hospo industry dream... <i>(May 27, 2004,Dominion Post,D4</i>)

>  The waiters are not in your face, but ready all the same with a snippet of hospo industry goss for the regulars they know. <i>(Dec 2, 2004,Dominion Post,D5</i>)

### hostie
 
 <i>n.</i> a female flight attendant [Also Australian (informal)] (See [also air hostie](../A#also-air-hostie))

>  hostie n. NZ & Austral. colloq. air hostess. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,362</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>McGiLL</b> hostie air hostess. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,59</i>)

>  <b>ROMANOS</b> 'i wouldn't mind giving that hostie a [sexual] treat,' declared Pitbull Smith... 'You can't say hostie any more, Pits. They are cabin attendants.' <i>(1997,Black,59</i>)

>  The hosties now have blokey Nehru jackets and trousers, school uniform suits, and something mysteriously called "a wrap", and offshoot of the straitjacket, to be inventive with - or exasperated by. <i>(Oct 20, 2005,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

### hostile
 
 <i>adj.</i> (of Maori or tribes) anti-Crown during the NZ Wars.

>  <b>T.S. GRACE</b> On my journey to and from Auckland, when i came in contact with both friendly and hostile Maoris, i found one opinion only, namely, that the war had begun hastily without the counsel of the Bishop or Missionaries having been sought <i>(May 28, 1860,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1860 May 28 T.S.Grace letter in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 91.]

>  His 400 soldiers were not fired upon by the 100 or 150 hostile Taranakis. <i>(Apr 7, 1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,ii 35,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>G.F.DAWSON</b> [E.J.Wakefield] who has lately resided ... amongst the Taupos and other hostile tribes, informed me ... that it was his intention to proceed to Auckland ... <i>(Jan 11, 1842,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Jan 11 G.F.Dawson to Colonial Secretary in GBPP 1842 (No.569) 194]

>  <b>FEATON</b> The so-called friendly natives in the stockade, as soon as the hostile natives opened fire, ran away into the bush. <i>(1879,Waikato War,44</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Maclean was kept well informed as to the movements of the hostile natives ... <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,93</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  .. young McKillop, ... who mounted again on the long-boat of the `Tyne' (wrecked shortly before at island Bay), and strolled up and down the harbour bombarding hapless hostiles... <i>(1922,TrNZi,53/24</i>) [NOTES: Best]

>  <b>COWAN</b> Day after day Atkinson led out his war-party of practised bushmen-settlers and scoured the forest and the native tracks, and soon had the country free from hostiles for a radius of many a mile from New Plymouth. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/225</i>)

### hot
 
 <i>adj.</i> uncomfortable ???NZ.

>  ... Asher Louis Murphy,. . . got it hot at Timaru the other day. `Deafy' sold scented nuts ... till he was bowled out, and the manner in which he was bowled out is unique for a deaf and dumb mute. With one Charles Wilson he ... wound up by using obscene language. <i>(Aug 2, 1911,NZ Truth,320/1/7</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> There is nothing lousy about him, and i always believe in giving a man a fair go and that's why i object to the word crayfish and i think you fellows are making it too hot when you say that. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> excessive, unreasonable

>  hot ... 21. Austral. & N.Z. inf. (of a price, etc.) excessive. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,541</i>)

>  <b>McGiLL</b> that's a bit hot! a protest against something unreasonable; eg `C'mon, Perce, that's a bit hot, no need for that sort of language. There's ladies present, in case you hadn't noticed.' c. 1910. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,112</i>)

### HOT
 
 <i>abbr.</i> High Occupancy Transport

>  <i>(May 6, 2004,Wellington City Magazine,5</i>) [NOTES: On the North Shore, officers use portable cameras to catch motorists illegally using the city's High Occupancy Transport (HOT) lane, which is reserved for buses and vehicles carrying more than four people.]

### hot dog
 
 <i>n.</i> a battered sausage or saveloy on a stick served with tomato sauce [additional NZ sense]

>  if you see a woman in a bikini swimming in a pool of hot dogs you are probably at Hotdog, a funk soul party at indigo on Saturday... The image for the show is an old-fashioned New Zealand hotdog on a stick, smothered in batter and dripping with tomato sauce. <i>(Oct 3, 2001,Capital Times,8</i>)

>  <i>(1979,Heinemann NZ Dictionary,525</i>)

>  a saveloy or sausage fried in batter, often served on a stick <i>(1984,Heinemann NZ Dictionary,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [Woolworths advert] HOT DOGS / Pre-cooked / Battered and on Sticks <i>(Mar 8, 1982,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  <b>McLauchlan ed.</b> The ground was covered with betting tickets, pie wrappers and hot dog sticks. <i>(2000,Morrieson's Motel,154</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LADY MARTiN</b> The story of the journey made me long to have been of the party. They had visited Roto Mahana (Anglice [e-grave], the Hot Lake), and slept on a little island. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,24</i>)

>  <b>LYSNAR</b> [South island] lakes are sometimes called the Cold Lakes , in order to distinguish them from the Hot Lakes in the North island. <i>(1924,New Zealand (various authors/titles),15</i>)

>  For in New Zealand we designate lakes hot or cold. Our thermal regions give us the hot lakes in the north as a set-off to the cold lakes of Alpine temperatures. <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,28,1949 ed.</i>)

### hotch-pot
 
 <i>n.</i> go into the hotch-pot, (of money) to be pooled.

>  The earnings of both parties [of diggers] `go into the hotch-pot' (ie. are thrown into a common fund) <i>(1868,Journal Saved from Shipwreck,23</i>)

### hote
 
 <i>n.</i> vegetable caterpillar,awheto

>  <b>J.B. WiLLiAMS</b> The Hotte a decided Caterpillar or worm, is found growing at the foot of the Rata tree with a plant growing out of its head; some have said that this peculiar and singular insect travels up the tree, both the rata & periri [puriri] trees, and entering into the top burrows or eats its way through the trunk of the tree until it reaches the root; it then comes out of the root and dies or remains dormant and the plant propagates out of its head, the body remains perfect and entire, ... [Glossary 118 ... Cordyceps robertsii: a fungus which attacks caterpillars . . .] <i>(1844,Journal,108,1956 ed.</i>)

### hotel
 
 <i>n.</i> in NZ, any licensed public house; the general name for a public-house, esp. (and formerly) one offering accommodation. The word inn has rarely been used in NZ, except in the name of a particular hotel. Tavern, a licence which does not offer accommodation for guests, been used since 19??. [(informal) 1960's check the amendments to Licensing Act to specify new definitions.]

>  <b>J.E.WiLLiAMS</b> i might say with propriety that six eighths of all the European houses are nothing more than grogeries. Here are in capital letters over the door `Tap'. One in particular so-called Hotel, a man and his family the only occupants, over the door a large sign one third as large as the front, with bold letters `Victoria Hotel.' Her Majesty must feel proud to have her name in blazon letters embellishing a house, where all kinds of satanical devices are practiced with [sic] impunity. <i>(1844,Journal,69,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> There are two or three Hotels [in Wellington], in which very good accommodation is to be had. ... There are several other Taverns and public-houses scattered along the main thoroughfares. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,200</i>)

>  There are some merchant stores here [at Port Chalmers], the custom house, and two hotels, or rather public houses. <i>(Apr 14, 1849,Letters from Otago,27,1978 ed.</i>)

>  Men, when in [Nelson] town, after having transacted their business, have nothing to do but lounge about the `hotel', as it is called,... <i>(1850,Emigrants' Letters from British Colonies,83</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> Wide streets [at Lyttelton], neat houses, shops, stores, hotels, coffee rooms, emigration barracks ... met our view ... <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,24</i>)

>  <b>ROCHFORD</b> There are several good hotels [in Lyttelton] facing the sea, also a custom-house and emigrant barracks. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,13</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> From the window of the hotel [at Kaiapoi] i saw a few groups of Maories ... <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,24,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> The loafer ... you'll find in hotels often lingers/And orders a glass on the bounce./ `Stick it up, you know me, it's all right'. <i>(1865,Colonial Songster,30</i>)

>  <b>COOPER</b> There is very little respect paid here to ... the Sabbath, at least among the store-keepers and hotel-keepers. The stores are almost all open; hotels have their doors closed but not fastened, ... <i>(Nov 2, 1867,Digger's Diary,7,1978 ed.</i>)

>  Landlords, as a rule, object to `public house', and prefer the more pretentious title `hotel'. <i>(1869,Punch or the Auckland Charivari,138</i>)

>  <b>G.M.WiLSON</b> We see the Elbow Hotel ... On nearing the hotel we are not prepossessed by its appearance, but its looks belie it for on getting in we find comfortable bedrooms ... and a capital parlour. <i>(Jan 24, 1873,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Jan 24 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 148]

>  <b>PYKE</b> For already there was an hotel (there are no inns in the colonies) <i>(1874,George Washington Pratt,89</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> in London the swells in the park drive all round, But the diggers drive here with a pick underground; And the props that they use, surely everyone knows Ain't the same used in London for hanging up clothes. The women use fans* there, the men use them here, Though to cockneys that statement may sound very queer, And the fellows that frequently hang round hotels, Out here are called loafers, at home are called swells. -->[p 89 fn] * A fan is a machine for pumping air down the shafts. <i>(1875,NZ Songster,No.3/88</i>) [NOTES: London & the Diggings]

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> Drinking here is fostered by the appearance and number of the `hotels'. `Hotel' sounds more respectable than `public-house'. The bars are opener, more numerous and less clandestine-looking than at home. <i>(1877,Colonial Travel,195</i>)

>  <b>CLAYDEN</b> Hotels are quite a feature of colonial life. They are mostly well-fitted up, and altogether unlike the average English public-house. They are in fact public boarding-houses. <i>(1879,England of the Pacific, The,50</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> [?=shanty] Some of the keepers of the shanties in the interior of the North island, which they dignify with the name of hotels, ...[get] the maoris to run up a long score for liquor and then secure a mortgage over their lands. <i>(1880,Far South Fancies,99</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> ... one [building in the goldfields township] being the `General Store', where every description of merchandise is sold; the other a public house, ... `The Maori Hen Hotel', where alcoholic liquors ... are vended. <i>(1881,White Hood and Blue Cap,5</i>)

>  <b>HOPEFUL</b> The town [Lyttelton] was hilly ..., and for so small a place there seemed no end of `Pubs', or Hotels as they are called here, at each corner of which stood groups of men, idling and gossipping - in fact, the male element prevailed greatly, and it was rather an ordeal for the `New Chum' to have to go through the ranks this way. <i>ibid.</i> 134 if this is agreed they will adjourn to the nearest inn, or hotels as these little match-boxes are called; ... When one friend meets another very often the greeting will [135] be, `Are you going to stand `shout'?' in other words, `Are you going to stand drink or `treat'?' if this is agreed to they will adjourn to the nearest inn, or hotels as these poor little match-boxes are called; and in fact there is a great deal of standing `shout' in the Colonies, both in public and in private. <i>(1887,Taken in,71</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> i entered a small hotel (by the way, every beer-shop in New Zealand is an hotel) ... <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,3</i>)

>  <b>D'AViGDOR</b> The business is simple and easily learnt. To enter it the first process is to hang about the bar of a popular `hotel' (Anglice:public-house) and grumble loudly at the hard times and low wages, not forgetting to abuse the Government for not finding work for everybody. <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,154</i>)

>  <b>MiTCHELL</b> One night, at the hotel where i put up, several of us had a go in at Yankee grab. i was unlucky. Next morning ... i had but one pound left. <i>(1889,Rhymes and Rambles,19</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> About the centre of the township, on the main street, two or three hotels, already mentioned, stood, with most of the other places of business scattered around in their vicinity. The hotels were at such a distance from each other as to allow the landlord of each to stand at his own door and watch with feelings of envy any unusual trend of customers seeking refreshment at the other. They were large, pretentious buildings of their class, with verandah and balcony to each, and were well-conducted inns, the accommodation afforded to travellers being all that could be expected - equal, indeed, to that of some of the best hotels in the larger centres. <i>(1899,in the Shadow of the Bush,21</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> They put the gig in the shade of the trees in the hotel paddock and let the pony go in the horse paddock adjoining. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,100,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>R. MASON</b> The practice of calling hotels `Hotel Astor' instead of `Astor Hotel' etc. dates i think from c. 1928, and was i think inspired by `Chateau Tongariro' which was built about then. i can remember a number changing, including the Astor in Auckland & all were subsequent to the name `Chateau Tongariro' <i>(1955,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>GOLDBLATT</b> ... and then the long main street, shop-lined; these make up the New Zealand township. There will be ... at least a couple of `pubs', with hotel attached ... <i>(1957,Democracy at Ease,8</i>)

>  hotel ... 2. Austral. & N.Z. a public house. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,541</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 237]]

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>D'AViGDOR</b> i ... [relate] what a New Zealand hill hotel-keeper and butcher told me:- `i buy a sheep ... i sell the meat ... to the road-men, and use the rest in my hotel.' <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,76</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> the narrator remembered that inder the butcher (father of Samuel inder, later a well-known resident of Gore) and George the hotelkeeper ... <i>(1956,Pioneer Recollections,2</i>) [NOTES: >1947-1948 Beattie Pioneer Recollections (1956) 2 ...]

>  Hotel-keepers report crowded houses still. <i>(Dec 30, 1899,NZ Times,2</i>)

### hotete
 
 <i>n.</i> a vegetable caterpillar

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> O'T|ete; A variegated worm like a caterpillar; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,144</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> To the fungi, also belongs a very curious plant, parasitical on a caterpillar, viz. the Sphaeria Robertii, called hotete by the natives. <i>ibid.</i> 2/363 Hotete - a caterpillar, the so-called vegetable caterpillar <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/422</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Amongst the damp moss at the root of the rata trees, in the shady forests not far from Auckland, and also in various parts of the Northern island, are found those extraordinary productions called vegetable caterpillars - the hotete of the natives. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/291</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> its caterpillar, called by the natives awheto or hotete, makes its appearance in the eighth month, viz. the end of January, ... <i>(1856,Traditions & Superstitions,293</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> There is also in New Zealand a very curious fungus which is parasitical on a caterpillar. it is ... called Sphaeria Robertsii, being the `hotete' of the natives. <i>(1866,Polynesia,52</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> The vegetating caterpillar (Hotete) is a singular production. <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,499</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Hotete is the so-called `vegetating caterpillar'. it is a grub two or three inches long, and out of its head there grows a parasitic fungus (Spaeria Robertsii) ... The natives eat the hotete. it is the larva of Hepialus virescens, a kind of locust. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/229</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> ... careful watch was also kept upon the growing plants to guard them from the ravages of a certain caterpillar; the larva of a large moth (anuhe, awhato, hotete; Cordiceps robertsii) which preyed on the kumara leaves ... This pest is better known as the `vegetable caterpillar' from a fungus that grows from it after death. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,86</i>)

### hotwater bottle
 
 <i>n.</i> (railways use)

### hotwater cupboard
 
 <i>n.</i> the cupboard containing the hotwater cylinder, the upper portion frequently used for linen, hence a synonym for linen cupboard [also as hot cupboard (no cit.). Jim writes: Often called an <i>airing cupboard</i> or <i>linen cupboard</i> (both in NSOD). In DNZE.This compound is not in any dictionary I have seen.]

>  <b>HOOPER</b> He reached into the hot-water cupboard and yanked out his rifle. <i>(1981,Goat Paddock,19</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> ...the room with the worn unpainted counter where we ate our meals, with the cupboards and sink and the hotwater cupboard where the curds warmed for the next morning's breakfast... <i>(1984,Angel at My Table, An,152</i>)

### hotwater cylinder
 
 <i>n.</i> a domestic hot-water tank heated from a fire or electricity or gas

>  <b>iHiMAERA & LONG</b> Washing machines, like flush toilets, modern stoves, refrigerators, gas fires, electric lights and hot water cylinders belonged to the world beyond where the hills turned blue, where the occasionally-glimpsed DC3 pointed its droning flight as it descended from the drifting clouds. <i>(1982,into the World of Light,250</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> hot water cylinder in most New Zealand homes, the HOT WATER TANK is located in a closet that doubles as a linen cupboard. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,53</i>)

### houhere
 
 <i>n.</i> a small, white-flowered, graceful tree with a fibrous many-layered underbark [Maori], the application of houhere to Plangianthus sp. is a back-formation from lacebark or ribbonwood applied to both genera; =lacebark, =ribbonwood, =thousand jacket, =houhi, also called lacebark, ribbonwood, thousand-jacket.

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> notes: The varied names given by the Maoris in different districts to this plant, are all said to spring from the same root. <i>Whau</i> signifies `wrapped about, netted - like lace', and the tree is so-called from the character of its inner bark. The settlers name it lacebark, ribbonwood or thousand-jacket' <i>Cf Williams, whawhau</i> v.t. `tie', <i>whau</i> = hou, (hohou) vt `lash, bind together'. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,264</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> [spg here =?<i> hoihere</i> (LK often omits initial H)] O'e, |ere; Name of a certain shrub. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,141</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i>Hoheria populnea</i>, A.Cunn. ... Nat. name, `Houheria', Colenso. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 30</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Hohere, Cunn. <i>Hoheria populnea</i> and <i>Plagianthus Lyallii. </i>... Houhere, Col[enso]. <i>Hoheria populnea.</i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  Houhere. <i>Hoheria populnea</i>, var. <i>angustifolia </i> <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/40</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  ) ... for net-ropes the tough stringy bark of the Houhere, and also of the Whauwhi or Houi (Hoheria populnea, and of its varieties,) was plaited together ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/35</i>) [NOTES: Colenso Botany North isd]

>  Of our shrubs and smaller timber trees, several are of strikingly beautiful growth ... (e.g.) the Houhere, <i>Hoheria populnea</i> ...; the Horopito <i> Drimys axillaris</i> ; the Manuka-rau-riki, <i> Leptospermum ericoides</i> ; the Kohuhu, <i> Pittosporum tenuifolium</i> ; the Kowhai, especially the small-leaved mountain variety, <i> Sophora tetraptera</i> var.: <i> grandiflora</i> ; the Koromiko-taranga, <i> Veronica</i> , several species; the Mairehau, <i> Phebalium nudum</i> ; the Toro, <i> Persoonia Toro</i> ; the Pukapuka, <i> Brachyglottis repanda</i> ; the Northern Maire, <i> Santalum Cunninghamii</i> ; the Tawari, <i> ixerba brexioides</i> ; the Tipau, <i> Myrsine Urvillei</i> and <i> M. salicina</i> ; the Tangeao, <i> Tetranthera calicaris</i> ; the Ramarama, <i> Myrtus bullata</i> ; the Ti, <i> Cordyline australis</i> ; the Kahikomako, <i> Pennantia corymbosa</i> ; the Pate, <i> Schefflera digitata</i> ; the Horoeka, <i> Panax crassifolia</i> : and, on the sea-coast, ... the Karo, <i> Pittosporum crassifolium</i> ; and the truly evergreen Ngaio, <i> Myoporum laetum</i> ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/5</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Howhere (<i>Populnea Hoheria</i> ) is a fine large tree of the linden kind. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/198</i>)

>  <i>Hoheria populnea</i> , A. Cunn. Houhere, Ribbonwood of Dunedin.--An ornamental shrub-tree, 10-30 feet high. Bark fibrous and used for cordage. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,130</i>) [NOTES: [Morris 120]]

>  <b>KiRK</b> in one or other of its varied forms the `houhere' is found in nearly every district of NZ. ...The bark is capable of division into a number of layers.... By settlers all forms are termed `ribbonwood', or less frequently `lace-bark' - names which are applied to other plants [Gaya Lyallii (now H. Lyallii) Plagianthus betulinus, Pennantia corymbosa]; they are also termed `thousand-jacket' <i>(1889,Forest Flora,87</i>)

>  Houhere (lacebark) .. Hoheria populnea .. Timber soft and of a perishable nature; used in cabinet making <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,9</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Houhere</i> , or <i>Hohere</i>, n. Maori name for a New Zealand tree, <i>Hoheria populnea</i> , A. Cunn., N.O. <i>Malvaneae</i> ; called also <i>Lacebark</i> (q.v.). and <i>Ribbonwood</i> (q.v.). [1883 <i>(1898,Austral English,204</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> The Maoris apply the names <i>hoihere</i> or <i>houhere</i> to varieties [of <i>Hoheria populnea</i> ] indifferently; the European settlers usually call all the forms `ribbon-wood' or `lacebark', names which are, unfortunately, also used for <i>Plagianthus betulinus</i> and <i>Gaya Lyallii.</i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,79</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> The <i>houhere</i> is one of the most beautiful of the small trees of the forest. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,264</i>)

>  <i>houhere</i> , a tree (Hoheria populnea), ribbonwood, lacebark, thousand-jacket. One of the three or four deciduous Maori trees. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/149</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> Few forest trees surpass in loveliness the white flowering houhere, from whose lacy bark the Maori made a kind of tapa cloth. <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),12</i>)

>  <i>houhere</i> The Maori name of a small tree, also called `lacebark' and `ribbonwood'. (Pronounce us. ho|-he-re.) <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),202</i>)

>  <i>houhere n.</i> New Zealand tree, the ribbonwood or lacebark [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  <i>houhere, houhi ... n</i> . N.Z. tree, lace-bark [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  houhere n. small NZ tree, lacebark or ribbonwood. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,362</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  182 Houhere (lacebark or ribbonwood) head piece <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,19</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Plagianthus Lyalli</i>, lacebark

>  Hohere, <i>Cunn. Hoheria populnea</i> and <i>Plagianthus Lyallii. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  Houhere Plagianthus betulinus, A. Cunn. <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/39</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>FEATON</b> PLAGiANTHUS LYALLii (<i>Hook</i> ., F.) ... `THE HOHERE'. - This very handsome tree is found in the mountainous districts of the Middle island. ... Owing to the flowering habit of the tree, and its fruit being a depressed sphere, hanging on a long ... stalk, it is commonly known by the settlers as the `Wild Cherry'. <i>ibid.</i> 177 Hohere [Maori name] .. Lace-bark [Settlers' name] .. Plagianthus Lyallii .. Small tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,54</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [houhere](hom-hyp.md#houhere))

### houi
 
 <i>n.</i> houhere, whau-whi, lacebark (also houhou, whauwhau)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Houi, <i>Col[enso]. Hoheria populnea. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  [Native name] Houi .. [Settlers' name] Ribbonwood .. [Family] Malvaceae [Native name Houi .. [Settlers' name] Lace-bark .. [Family] Malvaceae <i>(1886,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>FEATON</b> <i>Houi</i> or <i>Whau-Whi</i> Hoheria populnea. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,55</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Genus Hoheria. ... Hoheria</i> is a modification of the native name.) Maori names Houi, Whauwhi, Houhere. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,250,1940,. p. 264 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,156, 162, 264</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> HOHERiA POPULNEA (A.Cunn.) ... The `HOUi' or `WHAU-WHi'.- ... grow abundantly in both islands. ... Owing to the chaste [snow-white] appearance of its flowers, it has not inaptly been termed by some the `New Zealand orange Blossom'. The inner bark is peculiar, and consists of layer upon layer of laced fibre, ...From this peculiarity it is called by the settlers the `Thousand Jacket', `lace-bark', or `Ribbon-wood'. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,55</i>)

>  English Name Native name Botanical Name Ribbonwood Houi Plagianthus Betulinus <i>(1889,Catalogue NZ Exhibition,35</i>) [NOTES: 1889 Canterbury [Catalogue Canterbury Court, Exhibition)]

>  Houi (ribbonwood) .. Plagianthus betulinus .. Timber white and straight grained; suitable for manufacturing paper <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,9</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [(Featon makes the same error as with houhere.)]

>  <b>FEATON</b> The tree [<i>Plagianthus betulinus</i>] is known by the native name of `Houi' or `Whau-whi' ... <i>ibid.</i>177 Houi [Maori name] .. Ribbon-wood [Settlers' name] .. Hoheria populnea .. Small tree <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,54</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Houi</i>, n. Maori name for New Zealand tree, <i>Ribbonwood(q.v.), N.O. Malvaceae</i>, kindred to <i>Hoheria, Plagianthus Betulinus</i>, sometimes called <i>Howi</i>. in Maori, the verb <i>houwere</i>means to tie, to bind: the outer bark was used for tying. <i>(1898,Austral English,205</i>)

 <i>n.</i> lacebark

 <i>n.</i> <i>Cordyline australis</i>

### houma
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Sophora tetraptera.</i>

>  Houma, <i>Hector. Sophora tetraptera. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  <b>LiNDSAY</b> <i>S. tetraptera</i>, Aiton, var. <i>microphylla</i>, Jacq. ... The `Kowhai' of the North island Maori; the `Kowai' of settlers, - or as the word is variously corrupted and spelt by them, - `Goai' or `Ghoai,' `Goa' or `Ghoa.' According to Hector, the type is known to Otago Maoris as `Houma;' while the term `Kowhai' is applied to var. <i> grandiflora</i> in common with its other varieties. Var. <i>microphylla</i> is also sometimes designated by the colonists the Native `Laburnum' or `Mimosa,' from the similarity of its foliage, in the arboreous condition, to that of these familiar ... European trees. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,73</i>)

>  The ground is thick with hound's tongue ferns. <i>(Jan 3, 2006,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> ?in Maori use, drunk

### house
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Thos. George Casey perambulated into the dock, ... The prisoner persistently repeated every question the presiding person asked, and cheerfully admitted he had housed one or two before appearing. ... Said Kettle, `i will talk to you when you are sober,' ... <i>(Mar 13, 1910,NZ Truth,246/7/4</i>)

### house block
 
 <i>n.</i> a pile

>  <b>KiRK</b> House blocks ... rarely continue in good condition for more than ten years. <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,14</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> The Puriri (<i>Vitex littoralis</i>), sometimes called the New Zealand oak, is perhaps the next in importance to the Kauri ... it is principally used for railway sleepers, house blocks, ... and fencing posts. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,114</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> House blocks, even in dry situations, rarely continue in a good condition for more than ten years. Used for land piles it [L. ericoides] usually decays at ground level in six years, ... On the other hand, piles in marine works, have remained sound after twenty years' use, ... <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,157</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>L[eptospermum] ericioides, A.Rich.</i> ... Abundant from North Cape to the Bluff, ... <i>Kanuka; Maru</i>. ... Easily distinguished from [manuka] by its greater size, ... wood durable; much used for piles, house-blocks, posts and rails, &c. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,161</i>)

>  Hence the ... vogue which puriri enjoyed for many years for service as house-blocks ... served to [spread the white ant]. <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/13</i>)

>  <b>MANDER5</b> He forgot any doubts ... when the first load of timber arrived, nor had anything the power to damp his spirits the night the houseblocks were all in. <i>(1925,Allen Adair,61,1971 ed.</i>)

### house bus
 
 <i>n.</i> a large vehicle fitted out and used as a dwelling [Jim writes: Not in OED or other dictionaries. OED has "house train". Internet search Feb 2006: also in use in N Amer (NY), UK, Austral]

>  [advert]HOUSE BUS, BEDFORD SB3 1968, Cosy, attractive, spacious interior. <i>(Oct 15, 2005,Hawke's Bay Today,18</i>)

>  Jill and Rob Romain set up the Gypsy Fair in 1991 as a means of showing off the many stunningly decorated house-trucks and house-buses in New Zealand, and a way for their self-employed owners to sell their wares. <i>(Sep 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 59/69</i>)

### house cow
 
 <i>n.</i> a cow kept for household milk supply

>  <b>TURNER</b> [in winter] a farmer does not have to be mindful of cow-time or milking time on town day (or sale day, equivalent to English `market day'). in winter, one or two house cows might be milked by hand. <i>(1966,English as it is spoken in NZ,150</i>)

>  We had a house cow and there was always plenty of milk. <i>(Apr 1998,North & South,30</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> She emphatically and most intelligently refused to learn to milk the two or three depraved housecows, well aware if she did so she was trapped for life. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,50</i>)

>  <b>VANCE</b> When Betty and i first came here [sc. Ashburton], everyone milked a house cow and most people kept a few chooks. <i>(1976,Bush, Bullocks & Boulders,240</i>)

>  <b>MCALLiSTER</b> [Caption] Milking the house cow in 1900. <i>(1976,Old Taranaki,27</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cow kept in a home or house paddock to supply a homestead with fresh milk.

>  Everybody who has had anything to do with dairy stock knows the 'good little house-cow' that, on account of her diminutive size, would not be given a place in an up-to-date commercial herd. <i>(Oct 2, 1926,Hawera Star,16</i>)

>  Mother happened to glance out of the back door one morning, to where the house cow, with the hack, was grazing in a small paddock. <i>(Jan 24, 1929,NZ Dairy Exporter,54</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> House cows - in both islands it seems - are fast becoming a thing of the past ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North island,40</i>)

>  Janet, the old house-cum-nurse cow needs neither leg rope nor bail: she is milked in the paddock. <i>(Aug 24, 1972,NZ Farmer,113</i>)

>  Donna the house cow calved perfectly with a big black and white bull. <i>(Mar 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,37</i>)

>  We've always milked a house cow and when we came here we bought a two-bail milking plant and milked six or eight cows for milk for the house, pigs and a few calves we reared... <i>(Nov 9, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,13</i>)

>  Shadbolt wants to restore it to its original condition when Davin lives there with his family. "Well, apart from the house cow and the chooks." <i>(Sep 9, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> in the end he only kept one 'house-cow.' <i>(1990,Bryans, The: At Home on the Farm,50</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

Richmond)]

### House of Mummies
 
 <i>n.</i> [-]

### House of Pain
 
 <i>n.</i> nickname of Carisbrook stadium in Dunedin [later House of Taine, q.v.]

>  The House of Pain as Carisbrook has become affectionately known, has come a long way from the dowdy, run down, concrete jungle of my youth. <i>(Mar 8, 1998,Sunday Star Times,Focus 6</i>) [NOTES: later House of Taine, q.v.]

>  Wilson said it was crucial the Highlanders win all their home matches - they conclude their stand in a fortnight against the Hurricanes at the House of Pain. <i>(Apr 5, 1998,Sunday Star Times,Sports 1</i>)

>  Yet his focus is intensely singular at the moment, to beat England in front of nearly 40,000 fans tomorrow at his home ground, the House of Pain. <i>(Jun 19, 1998,Evening Post,20</i>)

>  i don't profess to know who first coined the "House of Pain" phrase for Carisbrook, but it is certainly not the first major stadium to boast this moniker. in the 1980s the Houston Astrodome was similarly labelled, in recognition of an intimidating defensive line sported by the Houston (now Tennessee) Oilers of the NPL. <i>(Jun 21, 1998,Sunday Star Times,B4</i>)

>  But if the second-half effort against the Highlanders at the House of Pain was streets ahead of the capitulation against the Hurricanes, the same scary sights.. did eventually emerge. <i>(Feb 19, 2006,Sunday Star Times,B3</i>)

>  Graham Mourie.. dismissed the House of Pain factor in his Wellington team's prospects against Otago. "it's just a name," he said then. <i>(Sep 27, 1998,Sunday Star Times,B6</i>)

>  Apparently one team were valiantly going down into the House of Pain - which, to me - suggests nothing so much as the glorious vision of an opposing phalanx of dental nurses, drills and Bertie Germ posters at the ready. <i>(Nov 14, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

>  [Two correspondents] wonder why the Dunedin rugby ground, Carisbrook, is called "The House of Pain." Otago rugby historian Dave McLaren says that several people claim to have coined the nickname, but the most likely person is ex-player (and children's book author) Brent Pope. Apparently, some time in the mid to late 80s, Pope was heard to exclaim, after a particularly hard training run inflicted on them by coach Laurie Mains, "Why the hell do we keep coming to this bloody house of pain?" it went public on September 27, 1992, when North Harbour coach Brad Mourant said much the same thing in his after-match function speech. <i>(Feb 12, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

>  There was no prouder man in the bowels of the House of Pain later than All Black skipper Anton Oliver... <i>(Apr 6, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

>  "This is our House of Pain. Whoever comes here, we show them the way home." <i>(Aug 21, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

>  A group of South African rugby supporters in the pub, polite as hell, still fuming after the All Blacks destroy the Springboks at Carisbrook, one sporting his new "House of Pain" T shirt. <i>(Aug 21, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  The House of Pain, it was said, would fill the Ocker hearts with ice. <i>(Aug 12, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A2</i>)

>  <b>PALENSKi</b> it is often assumed that the phrase [sc. the "House of Pain"] came to be applied by visiting teams because of the perceived difficulty of winning at Carisbrook. it actually began, however, in the late 1980s within the Otago team when players referred to Carisbrook as the House of Pain, because of exhausting training sessions. it was once attributed to an Otago player of the time, Brent Pope, and he happily claimed the credit for it, but it's difficult to pin down to one person. it's only in recent years that it's been seen as the House of Pain for visiting teams - a belief encouraged by Radio Sport (which began in 1998) commentators who had little or no acquaintance with either the ground or the phrase. <i>(Sep 28, 2004,Letter</i>)

### house of parliament
 
 <i>n.</i> a toilet

>  A `john' means a cop, not a toilet, and the [38] latter is sometimes called (between men only) a `dyke' or `house of parliament'. <i>(1944,US War Dept. Short Guide to NZ,37</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> So i travelled out to the woolshed and shearers' quarters, where the station-owner met me and told me that his entire shearing was held up because all his men had `the backdoor trot'. He said, `We have only two `houses of parliament' down here, and there has been a queue for both all night.' <i>(1964,Doctor in the Mountains,90,1974 ed.</i>)

### House of Representatives
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... it shall be lawful for Her Majesty ... to constitute ... a General Assembly in and for the islands of New Zealand, to be called the General Assembly of New Zealand, which said General Assembly shall consist of and be holden by the Governor ..., and a Legislative Council, and a House of Representatives; ... <i>(Aug 28, 1846,NZ Statutes</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Aug 28 An Act to make further provision for the government of the New Zealand islands (9 and 10 Vict. c. 103) in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 20 Vi.]

>  <b>BARLOW</b> Then we have a House of Representatives, consisting of a Speaker, Chairman of Committees, Clerk of Committees, ... and ninety members. The M.H.R.'s are elected for three years ... <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,177</i>)

>  <b>SiNCLAiR</b> The 1852 constitution was extremely democratic, more so than those then in force in the Australian colonies. The six Provincial Councils, the House of Representatives, and the Superintendents of the Provinces were all elective, though the members of the Legislative Council (the Upper House) were nominated for life by the Governor. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,87</i>)

>  1. the building in which parliament sits 2. the assembly of representatives, one from each electorate, which meets to make laws, control taxation and expenditure, etc. -->cit from Scott - <i>(1979,Heinemann NZ Dictionary,526</i>)

>  House of Representatives ... 3. the sole chamber of New Zealand's Parliament. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,543</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 363]]

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> ... the authority of the executive derives from the supremacy of Parliament, or, to apply the correct terminology for New Zealand, the General Assembly. That is the `... bipartite body consisting of the House of Representatives and the Governor-General', which exercises legislative function. <i>(1987,Politicians, Public Servants & Public Enterprise,12</i>)

### House of Taine
 
 <i>n.</i> a play on House of Pain, referring to Taine Randell, captain of the Highlanders rugby team and All Blacks. [Heard on Television from Sports Commentator "Some are now referring it to Carisbrook as the House of Taine" following Randell's appointment in June, 1998, as All Black Captain.]

>  [Hdg] Entering the House of Taine. <i>(Jun 19, 1998,Evening Post,19</i>) [NOTES: Heard on Television from Sports Commentator "Some are now referring it to Carisbrook as the House of Taine" following Randell's appointment in June, 1998, as All Black Captain.]

>  They call it "The House of Paine" - the suffering is done by the opponents of the All Blacks - but when Randell played his last international there, against Australia last year, the scarfies flaunted banners heralding the House of Taine. <i>(Jun 19, 1998,Times (London),51</i>)

### house paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fenced area close to a homestead or house [often occupied by a house cow or saddle-horse.]

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> After removing our pikaus, saddle bags and swag, we unharnessed the horses and turned them loose in the `house paddock'. <i>ibid.</i> 148 i allowed my new flock to rest ... in the house paddock before mustering then into the yards for earmarking. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,66</i>)

>  Getting the mob out of house paddock all seemed to be going smoothly until four of the Suffolk cum racehorse lambs cut back. <i>(Oct 2000,Growing Today,74</i>)

>  The hill leasehold is grazed by halfbred sheep and the house paddocks with crossbreds of very superior class. <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,40</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Mustered the sheep out of the house paddock, put them in the yards and drafted them. <i>(1914,Alexander Mowat Diaries,8</i>)

>  in fact, in the house paddock where all the mother ons, we tailed over 100%. <i>(Nov 27, 1975,NZ Farmer,28</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  The "house shout," which in the majority of hotels follows the fourth drink, has again been abolished. <i>(Feb 6, 1948,Evening Post,12</i>)

### housetruck
 
 <i>n.</i> a large self-propelled vehicle fitted out and used as a dwelling. [Jim Cameron writes : This term (or house bus)may be used in England but I have no evidence of this.]

>  Only a single surfer - at least a hundred years old, with a wild look in his eyes - gazes longingly out to sea from his purple housetruck. <i>(Nov 2002,AA Directions,No. 54/19</i>)

>  The housetrucks make for colourful and interesting subjects, and the photography of the housetruckers is of the highest standard. <i>(Mar 15, 1993,Napier Daily Telegraph,00</i>)

>  [Hdg] Housetrucks may get a home at last. Housetruck families working in Nelson during the fruitpicking season may no longer be hounded out of camping grounds. <i>(Feb 5, 1989,Dominion Sunday Times,00</i>) [NOTES: check page]

>  Appleby Domain - for several years home to itinerant families in Nelson - lay empty yesterday for the first time in years, with access to all housetrucks and buses cut off. <i>(Sep 29, 1988,Dominion,00</i>)

### housetrucker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A somewhat different group of New Zealanders spend a significant portion of their lives on the road... They are members of the Gypsy Fair, a band of house-truckers who have joined together to peddle their wares in a an annual tour as a way of making a living while indulging their love of travel. <i>(Jul 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 58/96</i>)

>  Large parts of the book are aimed at disproving "myths" about housetruckers: Housetruckers aren't all hippies - so to prove it we meet a former "top executive with Telecom" who now lives in a housetruck. <i>(Mar 15, 1993,Napier Daily Telegraph,00</i>) [NOTES: check page]

>  To join the fair, a house-trucker must ply a trade that won't compete with anyone else's. <i>(Sep 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 59/70</i>)

### housetrucking
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  HOME FREE - <i>Housetrucking in New Zealand</i> by Fiona Cunningham. <i>(Mar 15, 1993,Napier Daily Telegraph,00</i>) [NOTES: check page]

### housie
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  With a tape recorder secreted in his raincoat pocket, my husband would follow me into the venues and we immediately felt like SiS agents and supposed the Housie players might think the same. <i>(Oct 3, 2001,Capital Times,3</i>)

>  ...the young musician just wanted to capture the sound of the Housie callers, not just bust illegal Housie rings... in the South island they speak Housie numbers - all the twos, 22 - while Northerners sing them... Recording Housie callers posed a particular challenge... <i>(Sep 26, 2001,Capital Times,5</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> housie: bingo <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,140</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson's Glossary)]

### Housing Corporation
 
 <i>n.</i>

### how
 
 <i>n.</i> a howitzer shell [(WW1)]

>  <b>TREADWELL</b> Shell after shell burst round our ears as we clambered down. `Whiz-bangs' give no chance of escape. The first thing is the `whiz', and if it is coming straight there is no chance to escape the `bang'. it is different with the `how.', for ... a soldier can tell by the note of its flight if the shell is coming near or not. <i>(1936,Recollections of an Amateur Soldier,112</i>)

### how are the bots biting?
 
 <i>phr.</i> an obs. greeting. `How's things?, How are you getting on?'. [P: NZ medical: ca1924 ca1940 (WW2) p.c. G. Tuohy 1951]

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> <i>Bots biting?, How are the</i>. How are you?: New Zealand medical: from ca1929. See <i>bot</i>, 2 and 3. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,83</i>)

>  <i>bot n</i>. annoying type of fly, hence a troublesome person, particularly a persistent borrower of money; <i>to have the bot</i>, to us;have tuberculosis; <i>how are the bots biting? </i> how are you? <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

### how are you popping up?
 
 <i>phr.</i> how are you doing? [see AND]

### how would you be?
 
 <i>phr.</i> [see AND]

>  <b>AGNEW</b> `How'd you be?' i greeted them cheerfully. `We're the civil police, Joe,' one of them said. <i>(1974,Loner, The,178</i>)

>  <b>DUDER</b> 'How'd ya be?' he muttered. <i>(1987,Alex,42</i>)

>  <b>KEiNZLY</b> A man came down the road behind them, whistling. ... `Howja be, Mrs Pilgrim?' <i>(1960,Tangahano,150</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> `And how would you be?' `All right'. variant of How are you? as a greeting (especially by men) <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,271</i>)

>  <b>MciNNES</b> Talk. A man's voice. - G'day kiddo! How'd y'be? <i>(1969,Castle on the Run,124</i>)

>  `Gidday, Ginger,' i said ... `How would you be?' <i>(1981,Solo,126</i>) [NOTES: 1976 Mason `Not Christmas' in 1981 Solo 126]

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> `is that Phil?' ... `How would you be? i'm a first-time caller.' <i>ibid.</i> 164 `Good morning, Phil. How are you, today?' `Box of birds, Miss. How would you be yourself?' <i>(1978,Talkback Man,7</i>)

>  How would you be?: Hi! <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  how ya doin' how'd ya be <i>(1981,Auckland Secondary Teachers College</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

### how ya goin', mate?
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  how ya goin', mate? Kiwi greeting, fairly quintessential. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,59</i>)

### how you doing?
 
 <i>phr.</i> as term of greeting, 'how's things?'. [ca1949 Havelock (frequent) -->ca1950 at least (RM) Wellington, Marlborough, Timaru, Dunedin. b. how you doing (cobber), var. of above, imitative of Ma. Eng.]

>  'How you doing Cobber,' is the way we greet any other Anzac we may chance to meet. <i>(1941,Kiwi Magazine,38</i>)

>  Knock it off, pack a sad, have a nice one Get stuck in, boots and all, have a coke, Half a shake, how ya doin', have a drop, of mother's ruin Hell's bells, blow that for a joke. <i>(Dec 2004,NZ Memories,p19,No 51 ed.</i>)

### how you going?
 
 <i>phr.</i> [as a greeting (how ya ...) ca 1950 at least]

### how's it goin', mate?
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>McGiLL</b> how's it goin', mate? Kiwi greeting; quintessentially so. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,59</i>)

### how's that?
 
 <i>phr.</i> an interrog. phrase or `Why?'; occas. an indication of imperfect hearing or understanding: `What did you say?', phs from `How does that come about?' As intej.= `why' us. pron. [ha'uz set] with intonation pattern [B ]. Perhaps assoc. with the cricketer's' coy Howzat?.. Phs dial in origin: cp EDD How adv. ii 1. 22 Howat, How that how was it that why was it? Cum. [through the quot. suggests a different constr & use from the NZ] or ii 2. =Why? how comes it? (Sc. Lan Cor) e.g. `How didn't you tell'ni would come?' (Pen). Cf OED adv. 1.c. Obsolete pregnant use = How is it that? How comes it that? Why? Last quot. 1611 Bible `How said thou, She is my sister?' cf 4 interject (esp US) =What? What! [(esp. Southland/Otago) 1953 Sep 1 Dunedin eg. `I think Marist haven't got a chance.' `How's that?' ca1955 `I'm going home after this beer' `How's that?' `I said I'm going home after this beer.']

### how's the way?
 
 <i>phr.</i> [ca1955 Rotorua, from a Maori]

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> How's the way, Joe? <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ...how's the way?, how are you (faring); -->as a term of greeting, How are you? Good-day to you? cf how's things, how's yours <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> How's the way? How are you?; good-day to you!: New Zealand coll.: C. 20. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,411</i>)

>  how's the way? colloquial greeting earlier this century. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,59</i>)

### how's your father
 
 <i>phr.</i> (1) rough play or fisticuffs (2) sex [ODE has (2) above as Brit., not (1), SOED5 has neither]

>  Bit of how's ya father: Traditional TVNZ mealy-mouthed descripition of a violent punch-up resembling a street riot. <i>(Oct 2, 1999,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  Sadly, even a bit of how's your father is apt to trigger subliminal political anxiety. <i>(Jan 11, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  Often, too, when there's a bit of "how's your father" to sort out, he's right there too, doing the sorting. <i>(Nov 3, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

>  Among the lot interviewed is a 13-year-old who discusses the tricks the local lads try to use to get a bit of how's your father. <i>(Jul 12, 2001,Evening Post,24</i>)

>  When a waitress who serves one ofthe practice's drinkies becomes embroiled in some strange how's your father with, well, her father,Rick finds his libidinous predilections have suddenly dropped him smack dab in the middle of some very nasty family fallout. <i>(Oct 18, 1998,Sunday Star Times,F5</i>)

>  Just one more round of red wine and it would have been out in the lobby for a bit of how's your father. <i>(Mar 4, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  So the undergarment represented the possibility of howsyafaver. <i>(Jun 7, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  He had dealt to Hika Reid, and he and Hika were having a bit of a how's-your-father, so i thought, 'Blow this, that was pretty ugly Stan ... i'll join in.' <i>(Jul 22, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  There was no more flirting on <i>Breakfast</i>, no winking and twinkling over the weather satellite maps and not even any how'syafather on the new late news, <i>Tonight</i>. <i>(Jan 31, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),78</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> what are you trying to pull?

>  how's your form: what are you trying to pull <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

### how's yours?
 
 <i>phr.</i> as a greeting, how's things? [(Form of colloquial greeting, from reply to greeting How's things? `Mine's all right. How's yours?' The reference is politely obscene.) ca1949 J. Richards]

### Howard Smither
 
 <i>n.</i> [(seamen's) slang]

>  <b>PUGH-WiLLiAMS</b> if a vessel sails at any time in the evening and to avoid payment of overtime to personnel who would have an excess of eight hours if they worked all day and their watch in the evening, they are given the afternoon off and report back on board just prior to sailing. Such time off is called `a Howard Smither'. One of the first companies to bring this in was one Howard Smith and Co. of Australia, a firm seafarer noted for means to save money at the so-called expense of the [hand]. <i>(May 1951,Letter</i>) [NOTES: (seamen's) slang]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Howard's Marlin <i>istiompax howardi </i>Whitley <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An identification Guide</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 410]

 <i>n.</i> midwife [(Otago,?obs) [Refce c1898.]]

>  <b>KEENE</b> The local midwife [at Owaka, Southland, ca1898] (`Howdie' - in Scottish) arrived and shared Ethel's room, while i was turned out into the `lean-to'. <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,62</i>)

### Howicker
 
 <i>n.</i> resident of Howick in Auckland

>  i would ask, Are the Auckland members asleep, like the Howickers, or are they gone "ratty" on the Stratford route? <i>(Oct 25, 1898,Evening Post,6</i>)

### howlibag
 
 <i>v.</i> grizzle, complain (See [howlybag (noun)](../H#howlybag-(noun)))

>  i had a word with Manawatu hill country farmer Darrell Shellard, after he was quoted saying there was no use howlibagging about flood damage; you just had to get on with cleaning up the mess. ... Howlibagging is a great word, presumably a mixture of howling and bagging (criticising), but Darrell says he has no idea where it came from. <i>(Mar 9, 2005,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### howlo
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BiLLiNG & MANNERiNG</b> A dog joins her, a low howl deep in a thick-furred throat, rising to the long wavering cry of the wolf ancestor in the Arctic homeland. The "howlo" begins as all the dogs throw back their heads to mourn the night which never comes. <i>(1964,South : Man and Nature in Antarctica,72</i>)

### howlybag
 
 <i>n.</i> a person who makes an unnecessary fuss; a crybaby

>  .. using it ostentatiously whenever i hear someone making an unnecessary fuss. And i have received two messages both from South island males, recalling a howly-bag was a woman who wept too much. Heather Hapeta .. chimed in with an e-mail saying "howly-bag" had been in her family since the mid-1970's, meaning cry-baby. The setting she recalls, was in Maori groups in Christchurch. <i>(Mar 23, 2005,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Hmm, heres what Elephant, AKA Tony(the)Frew posted before his big howlybag tantram <i>(Apr 22, 2002,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.computerchat.co.nz/stories.php?story=02/04/21/1130455]

### HRC
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Human Rights Commissioner / Human Rights Commission

>  Sexual harassment is still a problem in New Zealand. A big one, according to the Human Rights Commission's NFO-CM research survey... in a separate report, the HRC's "Unwelcome and Offensive", 284 sexual harassment complaints to the commission from 1995-2000 were examined. <i>(Mar 27, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  When Jenny Shipley complained to the Human Rights Connissioner about the community services card, the HRC commented that the new policy was unfair, but that it couldn't rule on it for various reasons. <i>(May 13, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C6</i>)

Until swabs were analysed HRNZ did not know if the substance had been used here on racenight [sic] <i>(May 9, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B16</i>)

### HSC
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Higher Salaries Commission (See [Higher Salaries Commission](hea-hom.md#higher-salaries-commission))

Currently HSC determines pay levels, but Speaker Jonathan Hunt and Minister Responsible for Ministerial Services, Prime Minister Helen Clark, determine entitlements and privileges. <i>(Jul 24, 2001,Evening Post,1</i>)

Hazardous substances used to be controlled under many separate pieces of legislation. But in 2001 they came under one umbrella, the HSNO Act. <i>(Oct 20, 2004,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

### huahua
 
 <i>n.</i> birds preserved in their roasting fat (often in a carved calabash).

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> There was `mango' or shark, `koura' or cray fish, `huahua' or potted birds, besides preserved eels, and a variety of other dainties. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ, The,70</i>)

>  HUAHUA, OR PRESERVED PiGEONS ... Snare your pigeons when the kahikatea berry is ripe, and the birds ... fat ... Roast ... The huahua should be brought in and served with much ceremony ... <i>(1908,Souvenir of All Nations Fair,13</i>)

>  They could [22] have satisfied most of the `kinaki' [ed fn: Relish eaten with other food] wants from the coast, but preferred forest products. You found the incoming Ngati-Porou along the coast following the fishing grounds and the warm areas for kumara and taro cultivation, while the `tangata whenua' stuck to the hinter-land and forests, faring on `mamaku' [ed fn: Edible tree fern (Cyathea medullaris)], `aruhe', eels and huahua. <i>(May 22, 1930,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/21</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

### huai
 
 <i>n.</i> pipi

>  <b>HAAST</b> Then follows a series of shell beds, consisting of the remains of the following species, now still inhabiting the estuary;- <i>Chione stutchburyi</i> (Cockle); Huai or Pipi, <i>Mesodesma chemnitzii</i>, Pipi; <i>Amphibola avellana</i> (Periwinkle) Hetikutiku, and <i>Mytilus smaragdinus</i> (Mussel) Kuku; <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,416</i>)

### hub
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [fencing] to scribe or join a stay-head to a post.

This method may be termed 'hubbing' the stay in, or as carpenters call it - 'scribing 'it in. <i>(1927,Sheep-Farming, Fencing etc.,76</i>)

### huckery
 
 <i>adj.</i> grotty, esp. a huckery person:= `a scrubber', cf also hakari in some spgs. [(Occas. @n huck.) ca1979 heard from Hawkes Bay and Dunedin]

>  huckery unwell, jaundiced; possibly developed from Maori `pakaru', ruined, via its corruption `huckeroo'. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,59</i>)

>  <b>Mike White</b> Nobody denies how dreadful times are - even the industry's biggest boosters admit it's the worst situation since the mid-eighties when the government paid people $500 an acre to root out huckery muller thurgau vines which were drowning the market in sickly sweetness. <i>(Oct 2010,North & South,80</i>)

### huckery moll
 
 <i>n.</i> a slovenly or promiscuous woman
