>  Why not cut to the chase, find a really shatteringly plain huckery moll and out her, week by week, under the surgeon's knife for total reconstruction? <i>(Sep 26, 1998,Dominion,23</i>)

### huckleberry
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>KEiNZLEY</b> Kathy smiled him out. On a huckleberry morning like this it would be disastrous to let a schoolboy get near a river. <i>(1960,Tangahano,159</i>)

### hucklebones
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMiTH</b> Knucklebones (K-34) was played in both Europe and Polynesia, independently at first. in the nineteenth century distinct European [177] and Maori versions were known. More recently, the two types have merged, and today the Maori children, who are mainly responsible for perpetuating the game in New Zealand, include in their games movements borrowed from the European tradition. ... in most of the provinces the game was known as Knucklebones, and it is widely reported as having been played by New Zealand children of European origin until about 1900. Occasionally it was known as Chucks, Chuckstones, Chuckystones, Fingerstones, Hucklebones, Jacks, Jackstones, Knuckles, or Knucklestones. Children still played it here and there throughout the country in 1949, but they knew little about it until plastic knuckles were imported in 1954 in large quantities and revived the game. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,176</i>)

### huddling
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the crowding of chickens into a small area.

>  As to huddling, complaints have reached me regarding chicks crowding in corners of the house when being reared with colony-brooder stoves .. The huddling brings on a sweating condition, and this is fatal to chicks at any time. <i>(Aug 20, 1924,NZ Journal of Agriculture,130</i>)

### hue
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEE & KENDA;LL</b> U'e, s. A melon, cucumber, or anything that matures upon the ground. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,146</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> Hue, or ue - a gourd, calabash <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/363</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i>Hue, Col[enso]. Curcurbita</i> sp. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  These were - two roots, and one gourd-like fruit; the Kumara, or sweet potato, (<i>Convolvulus batatas</i>), the Taro (<i>Caladium esculentum</i>), and the Hue, a large kind of gourd ... <i>(1869,TrNZi,1/iii/29</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North isd)]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Upon the stages were piled baskets of <i>kumara, taro, hue</i>, dried fish ... etc. ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,113</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> The staple cultivated foods of the ancient Maori ... were: Kumara, taro, and hue. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,12</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>hue</i>: gourd plant, the hard shell of which was used for water jars. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,191</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> The gourd, hue, which was brought to New Zealand and cultivated, was eaten when young and was called kotawa. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,7</i>)

>  [Title] Hue (gourds) Young fruit of the gourd plant were cooked in the hangi and eated, but their main use was as eating and drinking vessels <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,4</i>)

### huer
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [hooer](hom-hyp.md#hooer))

### Huey
 
 <i>n.</i> [(trampers')] (See [Hughie](hom-hyp.md#hughie))

>  <b>PiCKERiNG</b> So to cope with the rituals of tramping, its pains and camaraderie, a slang language has developed to suit. Lots of quick short words to fit in the breathless pauses as you pant uphill. `Crud' (cloud), `bush-bash', a `gunn' tramper (which must have originated from Davy Gunn's famous trek up the Hollyford Valley to help victims of a plane crash in1936; he travelled 40 km by rowing boat, horse and foot in 21 hours), `windfall' (a collapsed pile of trees), `pit' (the tramper's true friend, a sleeping bag), `pog' (porridge), `bastard grass' and `lawyer' (two unpleasant plants), `piker' (one who stayed home), `Huey' (all-powerful god of weather), and many, many more. <i>ibid.</i> 46 New Zealand weather, sometimes called `Huey', is fast and fickle, with abrupt and noisy changes of mood. <i>(1988,Hills, The,8</i>) [NOTES: [Not so: HWO]), `windfall' (a collapsed pile of trees), `pit' (the tramper's true friend, a sleeping bag), `pog' (porridge), `bastard grass' and `lawyer' (two unpleasant plants), `piker' (one who stayed home), `Huey' (all-powerful god of weather) ...]

>  Unfortunately, Huey the surf god is not co-operating - it's flat, time to retire to a cafe. <i>(Apr 11, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C11</i>) [NOTES: (trampers')]

### huffer jacket
 
 <i>n.</i> jacket by Huffer (trade name)

>  One was 1.88m to 1.9m, of solid build wearing a huffer jacket, hoodie and black shorts. <i>(Aug 10, 2005,NZ Herald,A9</i>)

### Hughie
 
 <i>n.</i> the fancied weather-god of trampers, surfers, etc. (esp. in the invocation about bad weather: send her down, Hughie!) [(trampers' and mountainers')[Has the following any connection with send her down Hughie ??doubtful; the reference is to the Thames goldfields of the 1890s c1890-1900 Edwin Edwards `Waitekauri Every Time' in 1985 Penguin Book of NZ Verse 93 Oh, the days of Hughey's tribute and the doings that they did! You had to drink your grog those times from out a billy lid.] Compare send it down David, Davy.]

>  <b>FRASER & GiBBONS</b> DAViD (or DAVY), SEND iT DOWN: A soldiers' greeting to a shower of rain likely to postpone a parade. <i>(1925,Soldier and Sailor Words,72</i>) [NOTES: (trampers' and mountainers') [Has the following any connection with send her down Hughie ??doubtful; the reference is to the Thames goldfields of the 1890s c1890-1900 Edwin Edwards `Waitekauri Every Time' in 1985 Penguin Book of NZ Verse 93 Oh, the days of Hughey's tribute and the doings that they did! You had to drink your grog those times from out a billy lid.] Compare send it down David, Davy.]

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> David (or Davy)!, send it down. Often send it down, David, send it down! A military c.p. apropos of a shower, esp. if likely to cause a parade to be postponed: C. 20. F. & Gibbons; B. & P. (Wales has a notoriously wet climate; David, the Welsh parton saint.) New Zealanders and Australians say send her down, Hughie!. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,209</i>)

>  Easter, when the mountaineer proposes and `Hughie' disposes. <i>(Aug 1950,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,5-19-51</i>)

>  it was said earlier that ... `Hughie' (the weather) had been inclement ... By the morning the weather had deteriorated ..., heavy rain squalls being `Hughie's' contribution to a happy working party; ... <i>(Jul 1952,Tararua Tramper,12</i>)

>  An outsider might guess that our weather in the hills was almost always bad, to judge by the characteristic expressions used. The derisive phrases, `Send it down, Hughie' and `Let it come down, Hughie', go back to early in this century and are variants of the British military catch-phrase `Send it down, David'. `Send her down, Hughie', common in Australia's droughty climate, may not be so derisive. Judging by a verse attached to Dekker's Shoemaker's Holiday, St. Hugh has long been associated with rain. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,27</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail]

>  ... but despite our extensive deliberations it was really `Hughie' who precipitated our departure ... bound for The Hermitage. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,65</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Wife says send her down hughie but i says cut it out. Can't rain for the footie. Canterbury like the hard ground. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,147</i>)

>  <b>DAViS</b> '... There's a walloping great shower coming.' `We'll take it. Send her down Hughie.' cf Part: David Send it down David (Davy) ... A milit c.p. apropos of a shower, esp. if likely to cause a parade to be postponed: C20: F & Gibbons: B&P: (Wales has a notoriously wet climate; David, the Welsh patron saint.) New Zealander's & Australians say send her down, Hughie. Sup Semantics: Wales is the land of leeks (leaks) <i>(1964,Watersiders,96</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> in indigenous slang the nearest to a religious concept is the use of Hughie in Send her down, Hughie said to the source of rain <i>ibid.</i> 162 Miss Mason observes that St Hugh is associated with rain in a song in Dekker's Shoemaker's Holiday ..but here `HUgh' seems to be protection against weather rather than a cause of it, and St Hugh was the patron saint of shoemakers in any circumstances. [This sounds like crap: ??evidence. Surely Crispin was p.s. of shoemakers. iAG trots this too, probably from here.] <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,128</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... he looked up at the sombre and lowering clouds and he called upon them for rain ... Send her down Hughie, for Christ's sake, he prayed. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,142</i>)

>  <b>PARTRiDGE</b> send her down, Hughie! (and ..., Steve!) This Australian - hence also New Zealand - c.p. of late C19-20, the Steve variant being used during WW1, expresses a fervent desire for rain. (AS.) Variations of the next, her for it being characteristically Australian. send it down, David (with variant Davy lad)! The variant belongs to the Regular Army: and the basic send it down, David is often intensified by the addition of a repetitive send it down: late C19-20. in the army. esp. during WW1, it was used to implore David, the Welsh patron saint, to send a preferably very heavy shower, notably when it might cause a parade to be postponed or cancelled. Parts of Wales have a notoriously wet climate: and, what is more, Wales is the `Land of Leeks' (leaks). Both F & G and B & P are eloquent about this cp. <i>(1977,Dictionary of Catch Phrases,188</i>)

>  hughie n. NZ & Austral. joc. god controlling the weather (send her down, Hughie!). [pet-form of name Hugh] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,364</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 238]]

>  <b>McGiLL</b> Hughie God, matily addressed in phr. send her down, Hughie a request for rain. ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,59</i>)

>  Take the verse of Hughie Smith, the Bard of inangahua. <i>(Sep 1952,Landfall,212</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers') [?any relation to `Send her down Hughie'?]]

>  Send down some at your very earliest, Hughie. <i>(Mar 27, 1944,NZEF Times,iii/144 : 7</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> i allus said: one of these days old Hughie's going to make a pounce and when he does there's going to be some long faces in the high country. <i>(1958,Return to Open Country,227</i>)

>  Once again, 'Hughie' decided for us and it started to snow. <i>(1958,Tararua,12 : 66</i>)

### huhu
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  huhu moths batter the screen on the kitchen door <i>(Jun 1981,islands,8(4) No. 31-32 : 14</i>) [NOTES: check volume numbering]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> When i filled in the Day Book i'd ask Mori for his tally and he'd reply, `Found a beauty huhu log, boy.' <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,45</i>)

>  This insect is a native beetle of the "hu-hu" type, but smaller and known as Ambeodontus tristis. <i>(Jul 8, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,22</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Prionoplus reticularis</i>, the grub (esp), and the beetle (pron. [hu:hu.], Maori [huhu]). A native beetle or its edible grub, found in decayed wood [Maori];, larva of the beatle found in decayed timber

>  Rover, with the expertise of a truffle hound, dived for the iconic huhu in a native log and came up triumphantly with the goods. <i>(Dec 5, 2000,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  <b>iHiMAERA & LONG (ed.)</b> Then he pop a fat hu-hu in his mouth pinching off the black nippers. <i>(1982,into the World of Light,54</i>)

>  There were also wood pigeons, ducks, kakas, and huhus (the fat white grubs often seen in matai wood). <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,207</i>) [NOTES: Refers to 1940]

>  U'hu, s. A certain worm. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,146</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> Huhu - moth, grub <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/363</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Huhu - grub which bores into wood - a moth. <i>(1848,Leaf from Natural History,xiii ed.</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> i have ... several of the <i>Larvae</i>, which are large, wrinkled, and of a dirty - white colour, with a black head. ... The natives call them Huhu, and consider them a great delicacy! devouring them greedily when roasted. <i>(1848,Excursion in the Northern island of NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Colenso Excursion Nthn isd in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 51]

>  <b>BULLER</b> it was amusing to see [the Huia's] treatment of the huhu. This grub, the larva of a large nocturnal beetle (<i>Prionoplus reticularis</i>), ... infests all decayed timber attaining at maturity the size of a man's little finger. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,65</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> i. its food consists largely ... of the huhu grub; ... <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/14</i>)

>  <b>OGiLViE</b> ... there is scarcely any restriction left on how you may take the fish [sc. salmon] - provided you use neither huhu grubs nor dynamite - ... <i>(1971,Moonshine County,134</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> [The Hauhaus were reduced to existing upon] the <i>hakeke</i> or wood-fungus, and the <i>huhu</i>, or large white wood-grub. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/294,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> ...big, fat yellow hou-hou grubs. <i>(1937,Wednesday's Children,135</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> After dinner he walked back to the back of the shack, where two little brown men were still fiddling with the earth-oven. `Looks like maggots to me,' he said ... `<i>Hou-hou grub</i>. Pretty good, eh?' ... `Pretty good, eh? No <i>hou-hou</i>, no 'possum, no wild pig, pretty soon we starve. ...' <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,147</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> <i>huhu</i>, grub of a beetle (Prinonus reticularis). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/151</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> The children had gathered ferns and learned their names, and the names of the trees, and looked for wetas and huhus, ... <i>ibid.</i> --273 (gloss.) hu hu: (huhu) grub, larva of beetle found in rotting timber. <i>(1949,i Saw in My Dream,44,(1974 ed. Rhodes) ed.</i>)

>  <i>huhu n -s</i> [Maori] : a large creamy white roundheaded grub that is the larva of a yellowish brown New Zealand beetle (Prionoplus reticularis), bores in dead trees and timber, and was formerly much used as food by the Maori <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1099</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>huhu</i>: The EDiBLE LARVAL form of a moth found in downed and decaying trees. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,54</i>)

>  Heaps of huhu - raw or roasted and dipped in chocolate - were part of the smorgasbord. <i>(Dec 1997,Mana,No. 11 :</i>)

>  And he pondered that ridiculous business of the business-confidence indices last year, the ones that cast us as low as a huhu's navel when there really wasn't very much wrong at all. <i>(Aug 4, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> You.. watched.. weird flying things enter.. one night, a plague of droning huhus, which caused an early adjournment as everyone jumped up, hats and caps flying, to crush the intruders underfoot. <i>(1984,Blackball 08,31</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> hair-cutter, horny-bug, crusty back, humbug

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>(1848,Leaf from Natural History</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>(1848,Leaf from Natural History</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> A certain larva (<i>huhu</i>) found in rotten timber was much relished by the Maoris; and they esteemed as food the small green beetle (<i>kekerewai</i>) found on the tea-tree (<i>manuka</i>) shrub. it was called as a compliment Te Manu a Rehua, `the bird of Rehua' - Rehua was a god. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,109</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Huhu-beetle. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,449</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> HUHU BEETLE (<i>Prionoplus reticularis</i>). The largest beetle native to New Zealand ... The larva is a large fat grub known to the Maoris as the `huhu'. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,48</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Huhu beetle larva .. .. huhu tungahaere - <i>ibid.</i> Huhu beetle pupae .. .. tataka -<i>ibid.</i> <i>Huhu (longhorn) beetle ... Prionoplus reticularis </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The larvae [of longhorn beetles] are timber-borers. The huhu, <i>Prionoplus reticularis</i>, also known as `horny bug', `crusty back' and `hum-bug' in adult life, is said to be a toothsome morsel in the grub stage-but should not be washed down with alcohol as its tissues already carry a fair amount from the wood it consumes. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,150</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> `Huhu bugs? i felt hysterical. `What are huhu bugs?' <i>(1982,Myrtle and Sophia,34</i>)

>  <i>huhu n.</i> a New Zealand beetle with a hairy body. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,544</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 364; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 238]]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> larvae of matai beetle [(West Coast) [?Separate, or merely local name.]

 <i>n.</i>

>  it's like performing a dare with the crowd egging you on to part with a few dollars to try leathery crocodile, a quite pleasant ostrich in kebab form, snail that looks like nose meat or the infamous separate-the-men-from-the-boys huhu grub. <i>(Mar 13, 2002,Evening Post,22</i>)

### hui
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori community gathering, meeting, or social occasion [ORIGIN: Maori, [Ma. <b>hui (-a)</b> gather, meeting] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 60. [W3 1. Hawaii 2. Hawaian & Maori: community gathering: Assembly [quot NZJAg].]]

>  Then in the New Year [the Government] called Maori leaders to a <i>hui</i> in Rotorua to get their approval for a deal that was not negotiable. <i>(Apr 8, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> A number of worthless natives came to the Hui like the people who followed Jesus for the sake of the loaves ... <i>(Dec 23, 1846,Journal,iV/153</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Most of the Otawhao natives are now .. planting kumeras for the great hui hui, or feast, to be given by old Te Whero Whero during the next season. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/152</i>)

>  <b>McLEAN</b> We started at 9a.m. for Otaki .. where the hui is assembled ... <i>(Jul 15, 1849,Diary,47</i>) [NOTES: in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean]

>  <b>TAYL.OR</b> i have felt much pleased with this hui. <i>(May 30, 1853,Journal,8/108</i>)

>  i attended the First Maori feast (hui) supplied by the products of the Station, and conducted in English style in order to celebrate the `Harvest Home.' <i>(1928,Grace Pioneer Missionary,31</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Aug 16 T.S.Grace Report for Turanga District in 1928 Grace A Pioneer Missionary 31. 1 March, 1852]

>  The hui at Rangiaohia to promote the Maori King movement broke up on Friday last, but Potatau is still there. <i>(Jun 21, 1858,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/408,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J. Morgan)]

>  Candid comments may be made on any species of public entertainment, in which class huis, certainly, and runangas and koreros with the Governor [Grey], probably, are included. [A semi-transf. use: the context is pakeha -official]. <i>(Mar 8, 1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/29</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  <b>MOSER</b> The term `Hui' .. though strictly meaning a feast, is used commonly by the West Coast natives, for a sacramental gathering, a `Hui Hakara Meta'' ... <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,58</i>)

>  A hui is a gathering of the tribe, the hapu, or the family, and may be held for any purpose of common interest ... <i>(1908,TrNZi,40/164</i>)

>  HUi AT PiCTON MAORi WELCOME TO THE GOVERNOR GENERAL <i>(Jan 18, 1934,Press, The,8</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> Now, there are races, shows .. football .. dances, cinemas, tangis, huis and boxing ... <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,186</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> [Masses of grub and long orations] were inevitable at their tangis and huis. The tangi was a funeral, the hui a feast; but there wasn't much difference. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,121</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Starkie was glad to see the Maoris of Tenoe .. They had a dance coming on, a real hui, with an orchestra and a strange pakeha drink called claret cup (the boys would stick to beer). <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,131</i>)

>  On a large [52] grassed marae behind the store are two meeting-houses. You may be lucky enough to see a hui in progress. Perhaps a welcome home to one of the many Ruatoki boys who are serving with the Maori Battalion .. to collect parcels for Ruatoki boys overseas. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,51</i>)

>  <b>COMPTON</b> At a big `hui' in Waitete Pa, the Maori believers had been praying for Jim. <i>(1951,Through the Open Door,67</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> i mean it's common knowledge what happens at the pa when there's a big hui. <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,20,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (The Woman's Story)]

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> War was declared. Several Matiti men volunteered for service with the Maori Battalion and there was a three-day hui. [fn] Hui - assembly with feasting. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,44</i>)

>  hui n -s 2 [Hawaiian & Maori] : community gathering : ASSEMBLY doubt - New Zealand Jour. of Agric.> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1099</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> During the Christmas hui of 1924, when about two thousand Morehu were gathered on the marae, Ratana made a last effort to cooperate with the recognised churches. <i>(1963,Ratana,45,1972 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HiLLiARD</b> SOME MAORi WORDS .. hui assembly with feasting <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,192</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> hui: gathering of tribes for discussion and social intercourse, which included a feast. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,191</i>)

>  <b>MiDDLETON</b> .. i had often fooled around with girls .. at a local hui or tangi. <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,160</i>) [NOTES: (Not for a Seagull)]

>  <b>MASON</b> Pera. He's been diggin' that damn' haangi ever since the first hui, seven years ago. <i>(1969,Awatea,16,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FiNLAYSON</b> hui: an assembly of people from a wide district, related by family or other membership. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,140</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson's Glossary)]

>  <b>SORRENSON</b> But as Anne Salmond has pointed out, at hui on maraes throughout the country, the Great Fleet lives on ... <i>(1979,Maori Origins and Migrations,86</i>)

>  hui n. N.Z. 1. a Maori social gathering. 2. inf. any party. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,544</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 364; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 238]]

>  Muldoon said that he had gone to a meeting of Maori elders and leaders at the Legislative Chamber. `i went to the hui for half an hour and, as normally takes place on these occasions, it was nearly an hour before i could get away,' he said. -->a business gathering <i>(Aug 5, 1983,Dominion,1</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> Hui - `meeting together'. Huis may be held for various reasons, but the main ones are that they give the opportunity for continued social interaction of both sexes, young and old; they allow Maoris the opportunity to keep alive precious traditions (eg, song, dance, oratory, spirituality) and remember the past. Huis allow large numbers to meet and come to a consensus. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,19</i>)

>  Great excitement among computer behaviourists about the third Unix users conference coming up in the second week of May in Rotorua. it is, in fact, a hui and will be opened by the Governor General Sir Paul Reeves. <i>(May 2, 1986,Contact (Wgtn),6</i>)

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> it's going to be a very dismal world - all asexual handholding boring huis and general consensus. <i>(1986,Then Again,160</i>)

>  Peter Tapsell .. is tired of `hui-hoppers' and wants no more State-funded huis. The goal may be laudable, but why does Stan Rodger announce a forestry hui the very same day? <i>(Jan 21, 1989,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  <b>Hilliard</b> Next stop - the hui house! <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,58</i>) [NOTES: (1961). Attributive use.]

>  <b>ARMSTRONG</b> i wanted Hone Carter .. to turn up in a beanie and a Rasta T-shirt and say 'hey, youse fullas, sorry i'm late, but me and Billy B got stuck into a few jars while we were having a hui at the pub eh'. <i>(1997,True Colours,95</i>)

>  An eclectic hui of radical groups produces a collegiate atmosphere. <i>(Nov 25, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  Professor Winiata attended the Hopuhopu hui, which endorsed the establishment of a national pan-tribal paepae (place to speak). <i>(Dec 22, 1998,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Duff had been paid $2000 to speak on the theme Do-ies not Huis at an inaugural gathering of business and education leaders as part of a move <i>(Nov 13, 1999,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  A hui is not exactly a conference, but there are similarities. A lot of dull speeches is one of them ... <i>(Jun 28, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  Brown had just retured from a 'writing hui' in Canada. <i>(May 7, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  Now at all the hui the old kuia cackled and howled at that story. <i>(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,158</i>)

>  The Fiscal Envelope, what a lot of hooey! <i>(Feb 26, 1995,Sunday Star (Auckland),C4</i>)

>  Patricia Grace's lush and provocatively new novel <i>Dogside Story</i> is a magnificent hui of a book that bubbles over with laughter, human frailty, hope and love. <i>(Jul 7, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

>  Brian Dickson, chief executive of the area's largest iwi, Ngaiterangi, said he led a mass walk-out at a hui aimed at resolving ownership rights to Mauao ... <i>(May 30, 2006,NZ Herald,A6</i>)

>  Ngati Pahauwere s30 Representatives will host consultation hui with Ngati Pahauwera whanau and hapu later this month ... <i>(May 29, 2006,Dominion Post,C11</i>)

 <i>v.</i> to hold a hui

 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, muttonbirding]

>  The [mutton] birds were gathered into a heap and made into `huis', bundles of ten strung together by the neck with a flax noose. <i>(Mar 5, 1966,Dominion,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Marine) the sweep, a fish [[Ma. <b>hui (-a)</b>maomao, sweep <i>(scorpis lineolatus)</i>]cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 60.]

>  <b>PHiLLiPPS</b> <i>Scorpis aequipinnis</i> Richardson. SWEEP; HUi. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,37,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Maomao, blue .. hui, maomao .<i>Scorpis aequipinnis</i> =blue maomao <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BLUE MAOMAO ... <i>Other names: Scorpis aequipinnis</i>; hardbelly (Australia); maomao, hui (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,244</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family SCORPiDiDAE.This group includes two species of Scorpis, the maomao (Fig. 68.17) and the hui (both northern fish) ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,221</i>)

>  HUi <i>Maori name for Blue Maomao </i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,120</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a party (loosely confused with hooley) [noun. **ca1941 1979 HNZD 528]

>  We decide to have "a bit of a hui" afterwards back at the pa, and in the early hours of the morning, a few of the Pakeha staying there frantically rehearse again a simple Maori song, to sing as a thank you to the resident.. families. <i>(Dec 24, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),119</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (See [cyber-hui](../C#cyber-hui))

>  Now we have more weeks of negotiation, consultation and what Tamihere likes to call rent-a-hui. <i>(Aug 30, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  i will not be ruled by 'Dial-a-hui'. <i>(Aug 19, 2003,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: John Tamahere on Nine to Noon]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

### hui from hell
 
 <i>phr.</i>

### hui taumata
 
 <i>n.</i> summit meeting [ORIGIN: Maori, taumata misspelt tamata in 2004 cit. below (if correctly copied) (and in some Google hits); taumata is correct = brow of hill, summit (Ryan) TD [Macalister gives taumata as council, from trad. meaning of orator's bench]]

>  The Lange government called the first Hui Taumata in 1984 and in its stocktake, told us how bad and sad we Maori were ... <i>(Feb 22, 2005,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  He shifts into galvanising-leader mode, exhorting membership drives, slamming recent government publicity "about-turns" on Maori policy, dismissing the planned 2005 hui tamata (Maori economic summit) as little more than a government publicity stunt, stressing the need to validate Maori knowledge and "take New Zealand with us. We have to do as we preach; we have to help them get over the whakama [embarrassment] of saying 'Papatoetoe' [correctly] in front of their friends ... We will Maori-ise New Zealand. ..." <i>(Dec 2004,North & South,66</i>)

<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> [[Ma. <b>hui (-a)</b> gather, meeting, add up, maomao, sweep <i>(scorpis lineolatus)</i>] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 60.]

### huia
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Heteralocha acutirostris</i>, an extinct member of the NZ wattlebird family (Callaeatidae) prized by the Maori for its tail feathers [Ma. hu[u]ia, a bird (now extinct) whose tail feathers were valued as ornaments by Maoris.]

>  As a young man, Pycroft had the distinction of eating a huia. Skilled in taxidermy, he had been sent a huia for mounting. After skinning it he handed its body to his landlady and had her skin it. <i>(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,306/35</i>)

>  A female huia had been seen recently by an old-time bushman in the Wellington district. <i>(Sep 4, 1944,NZEF Times,iV/167 : 10</i>)

>  Fifty years ago the Huia was plentiful in the Woodville-Kumaroa district. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,137</i>) [NOTES: Writing of 1940]

>  Mr Jack Stevens.. is once more on the job in the huia-hunting crusade. <i>(Dec 21, 1912,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  A female huia had been seen recently by an old-time bushman in the Wellington district. <i>(Sep 4, 1944,NZEF Times,iV/167 : 10</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> U'ia, s. A certain bird; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,146</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Huia </i>- This bird is found only in the mountainous districts of Taranaki, and [check, ?no] farther south than Waiapu or the East Cape. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),60</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Puhi ... arranged the hair of the gentlemen, placing ... the various feathers of the uia and tara, sea-fowl, that assisted the decoration. <i>ibid.</i> 1/158 ... i was convinced they were all chiefs; their hair had been neatly collected, and tied in a bunch at the top of the head, decorated with the feathers of the <i>uia nui</i> bird. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/112</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Neomorph Gold [?Gould] e houia E Uia [caption to drawing] <i>(Apr 23, 1839,Journal,2/114</i>)

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> 5.<i>Neomorpha Gouldii</i>. G.R. Gray, ... Huia, <i>Yate</i>. Uia of the natives. <i>Polack, Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/187</i>) [NOTES: -1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/187]

>  <b>DiEFFENBACH</b> But the bird ... is only found in the deepest recesses of the primeval forest covering the hills around Port Nicholson, and between the latter place and Hawke's Bay. it is called by the natives Uia, and was received into the ornithological system under the name of Neomorpha Gouldii. [90] ... the continued loud and shrill whistling of my guide, <i>uia, uia, uia</i>, in imitation of the notes of the bird, had attracted four, ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/89</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> The huia is a black bird about as large as a thrush, with long thin legs and a slender semi-circular beak, which he uses in seeking in holes in trees for the insects on which he feeds. in the tail are four long black feathers tipt with white. These feathers are much valued by the natives as ornaments for the hair on great occasions .... The natives attracted the birds by imitating the peculiar whistle, from which it takes the name of huia. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/91</i>)

>  <i>(1847,Adventure in NZ</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> ... amongst these the tail feathers of the <i>huia (Neomorpha Gouldii)</i> are the most valued, ... spg <i>Uia </i> <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/326</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> The Huia, or <i>Neomorpha Gouldii</i>, abounds on the hills near Port Nicholson. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,162</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The natives have a saying that the huia stole the plumage of the kokako, the native crow, and gave its own tail instead. <i>(Mar 6, 1849,Journal,6/37</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> if the feathers are those of the bird called <i>huia</i>, it is a sign the child will be a girl; if those of the <i>kotuku</i> (a white crane), the dream prognosticates a male child. <i>(1856,Traditions & Superstitions,143</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> ... the elegant Uia [sic, and also 2nd 1861 edition], a bird the [120] size of a magpie, ... is found in the more remote districts; ... <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/119</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The `huia' (<i>Neomorpha Gouldii</i>) is an elegant bird ... about the size of a crow ... <i>(1866,Polynesia,70</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 8. HETERALOCHA GOULDi. Gray. Huia. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,4</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> \{1888 2nd edn 1/7 HETERALOCHA ACUTiROSTRiS. (HUiA.) ... <i>Native name</i>.- Huia. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,63</i>)

>  <i>Heteralocha acutirostris</i>. - Huia. South part of North island, in Ruahine, Tararua, and Rimutaka Ranges. <i>(1886,TrNZi,18/112</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <b>MORRiS</b> <i>Huia</i>... Maori name for a New Zealand bird, like a starling, <i> Heteralocha acutirostris</i>, Gould, of limited occurrence, chiefly found in North island; having beak straight and short in the male, long and curved in female. The tail feathers are highly prized for ornament by the Maoris. <i>(1898,Austral English,205</i>)

>  <i>Heteralocha acutirostis Gould. Huia. </i> <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/96</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <i>hu[u]ia</i>, a bird (Heteralocha acutirostris): white-tipped tail-feathers valued as ornaments: bird now extinct. A small, long, narrow, carved box (equivalent of our jewel-box) called waka-huia (waka here means container), a receptacle for feathers; but only huia-feathers. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/143</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  huia : forest bird, now probably extinct, noted for difference in bills in male and female, and white band of tailfeathers. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,174</i>)

>  ...the only [bird] in which the bills of the sexes are different in shape ... now so rare that it is probably extinct ... Even in the days of the Maori its habitat was very restricted, confined as it was to parts of the Rimutaka, Tararua, Ruahine & Kaimanawa ranges. <i>(1948,NZ Forest inhabiting Birds</i>)

>  A New Zealand bird, now extinct, whose tailfeathers were much valued by the Maori as ornaments for the hair. (Pronounce us. hoo|-i-a.) <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),202</i>)

>  Huia .. .. huia <i>Heterolocha acutirostris </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> HUiA <i>Heteralocha acutiristris</i> ... has probably been extinct since c. 1907. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,235</i>)

>  <i>huia n.</i>extinct N.Z. bird, white-tipped feathers were prized by Maoris as head ornaments [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  278. <i>Heteralocha acutirostris</i> (Gould, 1837) <i>Huia </i>... probably extinct since about 1907. <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,72,1980 ed.</i>)

>  huia n. an extinct New Zealand bird, prized by early Maoris for its distinctive tail feathers. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,544</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 364; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 238]]

>  As a young man, Pycroft had the distinction of having eaten a huia. Skilled in texidermy, he had been sent a huia for mounting. After skinning it he handed its body to his landlady and had her cook it. <i>(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,305/35</i>)

>  No longer goeth he [sc. Adam Hamilton] into the mountains to snare a new kind of slug or track the absent huia to its lair. <i>(Oct 11, 1913,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  The two stuffed huia, in their glass case in the school foyer, now get noticed by the puils who once barely gave them a passing glance... Huia became collectors' items because the male and female birds have different shaped beaks. <i>(May 20, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> When he was able to hobble they crossed the Blue Range to Mikimiki, shooting a pair of huia for their final dinner. <i>(1994,Tararua : the Story of a Mountain Range,70</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred cultivar of white clover (Trifolium repens). [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  All the seed is certified, the Raunu is mother seed and the Huia is first generation. Here is the mixture he uses per acre: Ryegrass (Raunu) 4lb..White clover (Huia) 2lb. <i>(Oct 1974,NZ Journal of Agriculture,33</i>)

>  Huia white clover remains the most popular clover for pastures in the hill and high country area because of its persistence under varying management regimes. <i>(Mar 1986,Proceedings 1985 Hill & High Country Seminar,70</i>) [NOTES: proceedings: 1985 Hill & High Country Seminar]

>  in a farm trial at Taupo, kopu enjoyed an advantage of 120% to 160% over huia in autumn and winter. <i>(Mar 1991,NZ Dairy Exporter,17</i>)

>  This irish clover produced more annual drymatter than Kopu, Huia or Pitau in trials at Ruakura or Palmerston North. <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,79</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> For primary forest burn Huia white clover. <i>(1970,Grasslands of NZ,183</i>)

>  A legume crop, Huia white clover, which was undersown in the 1985 pea, grain and Nui ryegrass crops, should also prove an attractive proposition. <i>(1987,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Jan/Feb 14</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> esp. huia feather

>  imitation huia calls segue into hogget auctions. <i>(Jan 29, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

>  To get huia cells, you need a huia. <i>(Feb 25, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  <b>McLEAN</b> The elder men were dressed in their best dogskin and<i> kaitaka</i> mats, some of them had their heads decorated with <i>huia</i> and <i>kotuku</i> feathers, not neglecting their <i>mere[es acute] pounamu</i> (greenstone clubs), and every other ancient emblem of chieftainship <i>(May 26, 1848,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1848 May 26 Maclean Diary in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 36]

>  i went to the pa early and saw Wi lying in state, the greenstone mere at his head and many greenstone ornaments. His head had huia feathers on either side, flowers on his chest. <i>(Oct 30, 1897,Chronicles of the Gardens of NZ,396,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRiE</b> Being very tall, [the Maoris] presented a dignified appearance clad only in their silky white <i>Kaitakas</i> with huia feathers in their hair. These finest of Maori mats they wore tied on the right shoulder, leaving that hand ... free to grasp the greenstone `mere'. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,37</i>)

>  <b>MANSFiELD</b> in the shearing sheds - the yellow dress with huia feathers on the coat jacket with scarlet rata blossom - <i>(1907,Urewera Notebook,52,(1978 Gordon) ed.</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> <i>i was much impressed by these visitors </i>... with <i>huia</i> feathers in their hair and greenstone pendants in their ears. <i>(1948,Taina,134</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> When they reached the last known haunt of the huia bird, the Maori owned Aorangi block on the western flank of the Ruahines, John, ... , invited me to accompany his party. [1953] <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,175</i>)

>  <i>huia</i> also <i>huia bird n -s</i> [Maori <i>huia</i>] : a bird (<i>Neomorpha acutirostris</i> or <i>Heteralocha acutirostris</i>) related to the starlings, confined to a small region in the mountains of New Zealand, and having black white-tipped tail feathers prized by Maori chiefs and worn as insignia of rank <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third international,1100</i>)

>  Artifacts, including huia feathers, a whaler's powder horn from the Okains Bay Maori and Colonial Museum, are isolated, way out of context, deliberate self-importance bestowed by the Victorian photographer's black backdrop. <i>(Jun 21, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

### huku
 
 <i>n.</i> kingfish

### hula
 
 <i>n.</i> a mechanical skirt on a racing yacht

>  [HJdg] Team NZ likely to do the hula. To hula or not to hula will be the main talking point when Team New Zealand and Alinghi unveil their America's Cup boats in Auckland today. <i>(Feb 11, 2003,Dominion Post,C12</i>)

>  You would de-hula one boat, just to get some relativity... <i>(May 11, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B7</i>)

### hula-haka
 
 <i>n.</i> a child of mixed Maori-Samoan parentage

 <i>adj.</i> combining Pacific island and Maori

 <i>n.</i> something good [1953 14 M A5 Thames DHS 8 HUM -->1953 14 M A10 Thames DHS 4 HUM]
