### hump
 
 <i>n.</i> [AND a. obs. an arduous walk carrying a load on one's back 1890 Bold as only cit., but quoting a prior NZ ref: 1863 J.G.Walker Jrnl 7 Jan [MS.] 4 [this is OED ref, and needs checking and reconciling with my ref] b. a swagman 1955 only quot.] a difficult (?tiring) walk or tramp carrying a load (on the back)

>  in the course of the morning we made up our minds to cut digging and start a store, so on Saturday we started over the hill for provisions and brought over 120 lbs. principally flour. it was a precious hump, but we hope to hire a horse in the course of a day or two. <i>(Jan 26, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker),4</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> Hump, n. a long walk with a swag on one's back. <i>(1898,Austral English,206</i>)

>  <b>BOLDREWOOD</b> <i>(1890,Miner's Right,c. 3, p. 46:</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a term of abuse (phs a rhyming variant of lump). [ca1935 School children, Havelock -->ca1945 Wellington eg You dirty hump.]



 
 <i>v.</i> a. to carry (on the back or shoulders, and esp. a tiring load). Prob. introduced to NZ by Aust. gold-diggers; and phs orig. from English dial (cf EDD), phs reinforced by rhyme with lump v. of similar meaning, and similar metaphorical associations (lump/hump on back).

>  Found a log of dead wood, humped [fn] Anglice[e acute] - carried on shoulder] for a mile-and-a-half, - made a small fire, ... <i>(Jul 25, 1855,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,7</i>)

>  - lost the mare at Watts but found her coming home - could not catch her so humped the blankets &c. <i>(Jun 17, 1856,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point</i>) [NOTES: Phillips]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Oh! what fires we made - ... then we humped in three stones for seats - on one of which i am sitting writing this while the others are at work up on the house ... <i>(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,50</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> i think i told you in my last that we had started getting stores over here to sell to the diggers. We found that humping it over from the Tiviot on our backs would not do as it was too hard work, so we invested in a Black Bull. <i>(Mar 4, 1863,Journal</i>) [NOTES: [Dunstan Diggings]]

>  it is very hard work humping your blankets and tucker. <i>(May 12, 1864,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/111</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond, Nelson)]

>  <b>THATCHER</b> He must `hump' his clothes and blankets,/ <i>(1864,Songs of the War,9</i>)

>  <b>FARJEON</b> The best thing we [diggers] can do ... is to try and hump it back again tomorrow. <i>(1865,Shadows on the Snow,66</i>)

>  Humping all their belongings with them. <i>(Jul 16, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,192,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> Having cut a sufficient quantity, we `humped' it out of the bush and packed it beside our new home. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,93</i>)

>  <b>CLYDE</b> i'll hump ye up to Dr. Bauer. <i>(1872,Te Kooti,34</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> Now the grand up-country rule when you have [107] lost your horse is a colonially established maxim it is this: `Hump your saddle and look for him,' which being translated into English English. means simply, - `Lash your saddle to your shoulders ...' <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,106</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> He cannot really afford ... more, for he must hump all his belongings on his own back over ... forest and morass. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/25</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> The provisions for the distant stations were conveyed by whaleboat as far up the rivers ... as practicable, and were then `humped' over the intervening land portion of the journey. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,78</i>)

>  <b>MORRiS</b> Hump, v. to shoulder, carry on the back; especially, to hump the Swag, or Bluey, or Drum. See Swag, Bluey, Drum. [1855] <i>(1898,Austral English,205</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> ... an' he'd be d-----d if he wad hump ony mair whusky to Swinborough on Shanks's powny. ... He micht juist as weel be on the wallaby amang the stations again - ... July 25 ibid 45 Ross & Aylmer humping out their wood. <i>(1899,in the Shadow of the Bush,233</i>)

>  <i>(Dec 11, 1915,Hutt Valley independent,2</i>)

>  Hump. To carry, especially a tiring load. ([1874 Kennaway Crusts], p.70, ibid.,107;[1898 Morris Austral-English]) [This is purely Australian, unrecorded in England.--A.W.] <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The,17</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  hump To `hump' in Australia and New Zealand is to carry anything on the back. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),203</i>)

>  hump v.t. to carry on the back; to shoulder. See BLUEY. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  hump v.t. carry on the back as e.g.,hump the bluey, swag or matilda. (q.v.). <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  Before long he was fair-dinkum bushed. ...So he got on a blaze and humped his pack up towards the cage. <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  hump ... NZ & Austral. sl. carry, esp. laboriously. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,365</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 545, sense 6; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 239]]

>  Mr. Kealy met them "humping " their toms, they having found a good prospect, so they are now upon their "own hook." <i>(Nov 7, 1855,Nelson Examiner,2</i>)

### hump and hollow
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to contour a flat swamp paddock into one with humps and hollows to promote drainage.

>  Drainage can be a problem, and each year, he humps and hollows, contours and drains part of the farm. <i>(Jan 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,19</i>)

>  Some 6.5 ha has been 'humped and hollowed.' <i>(Jun 1995,NZ Dairy Exporter,105</i>)

>  One way in which it [the development programme] will do this is to 'hump and hollow' some of the pakihi soils and to 'flip' pasture. Humping and hollowing creates contoured surfaces to promote drainage. The plan at this stage is to turn all those properties that are able to be flipped or humped and hollowed into dairying. <i>(Oct 25, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,19</i>)

### hump it
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BELL</b> And here i am, ... but dead broke - without a cent - had to hump it all the way on foot. <i>(1899,in the Shadow of the Bush,30</i>)

### hump Matilda
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>MEREDiTH</b> Not relishing the prospect of `humping Matilda' round the country, looking for a job, i decided to get back home [to Tasmania] again. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,173,1935 ed.</i>)

### hump one's/a/the swag
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>J. GOLDiE</b> Digger custom, we humped our swag ... our grub.... <i>(Feb 19, 1863,Diary</i>) [NOTES: [1863 Feb 19 J. Goldie 3rd Diary] 1947 Beattie Pioneers Explore Otago 136]

>  <b>MONEY</b> it is lamentable to witness the discomfiture of your after-dinner Colonist, when he meets with some unpretending fellow (who does not even swear), but who has `humped his swag' into strange lands, and traversed the `wallaby-track' under the direst conditions. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,vi</i>)

>  <b>G.M. WiLSON</b> Humping our swags again we start very much refreshed to climb the hills, which is the hardest part of the day's journey. <i>(Jan 30, 1873,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Jan 30 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 158]

>  We ... humped our swags, and made a start for this mysterious country ... <i>(1879,Spangles and Sawdust,25</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> O'er the dreary Maniototo many a time he'd `humped' his swag,/ -->here ? swing it onto one's back. <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,63</i>)

>  <b>MYERS</b> [He finally hired] a pale-faced youth of twenty-three - Joe Evans ... The necessary arrangements were made. Joe `humped' his swag, and Daniel sent him off to catch the train ... <i>(1941,Valiant Love,37</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> in the early days a vast number of shearers and station hands moved about the country humping their swag in search of work. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,40</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKiNS</b> Different things brought people together to swag. There was i, on my Pat Malone, starting off on my first experience at humping a swag. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,68</i>) [NOTES: (Bob Edwards, bushman)]

>  humping the swag (Phr.) in search of work, [From itinerant workers who tramped the country for work]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)

>  A hardy son of toil humping a swag.. arrived at Waipara the other day. <i>(Feb 15, 1977,NZ Truth,1</i>)

### hump one's/the bluey
 
 <i>phr.</i> [cf. bluey-humper]

>  <b>WALLACE</b> [Morris] `Humping bluey' is for a workman to walk in search of work.' <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,73</i>)

>  <b>WRiGHT</b> i'd best hump my bluey on. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,30</i>)

>  Joe Potter, humping his bluey across the scrub-covered Waitui flats, when he and Steel rushed Flannigan's Find, little thought he would ever see an important town in that place ... <i>(Nov 1903,NZ illustrated Magazine,108</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> But today he taketh an unblenched heart his axe, and his billy; he humpeth his bluey and setteth his face to the wilderness. ... Put him in Queen Street today as he stands, and watch. The constable layeth a steady eye on him. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,293,[1928/298] ed.</i>)

>  Therefore, Woollcombe, get thee hence; go home thou wowser on the wallaby; hump bluey, carry Matilda, pad the hoof, thou parsonical howler, thou Wowseristic whaler Woollcombe. <i>(Nov 6, 1909,NZ Truth,228/1/3</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> Swaggers were met on the country roads, `humping their bluey', a billy for making tea tied to their rolled-up blanket. <i>(1934,Winds of Heaven,290</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> The diggers i saw `humping bluey' wore white or coloured moleskin trousers, a leather belt ... <i>(1952,Faring South,159</i>)

>  <b>WiL;SON</b> Stella ... encouraged him to ... go to Auckland by train. `No, i'll hump bluey there, but coming back ... [sic] well i mightn't own a bluey by then, ...' <i>(1955,Land of My Children,115</i>)

>  v.tr. carry on the as e.g.,hump the bluey, swag or matilda. (q.v.). <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),617</i>)

>  to hump the bluey n. to go on the tramp, carrying a swag on the back. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),611</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> if folks were reduced to walking and humping the bluey the least one could do was to give a lift, was his unvoiced attitude. <i>(1970,Life's Vagaries,73</i>)

>  We 'umped our blueys for about three miles through the mud to Terrynikow. <i>(Jul 5, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,iV/47:251</i>)

>  <b>ireland</b> in his mid twenties he went to Australia, where he spent some years 'humping his bluey' - on the swag. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,26</i>)

### hump one's/the drum
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  There we "humped the drum," consisting of a tent-fly, tucker, supplies, &c." <i>(Jan 20, 1898,Otago Witness,62</i>)

>  <b>Thompson</b> ' Twas somewhere up near Timaru - / That town whose Celtic-sounding name/ Suggests to me a wild `Hurroo!'/ To make a rhyme to fit the same -/ That many years ago began/ To `hump his drum' an irishman. ibid. 81 THE MAN WHO HUMPS HiS DRUM.* [title] [*fn] `Humps his drum' - i.e., carries his swag. <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,73</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> The man who humps his drum / Must be awful fond o' rum. <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,82</i>)

### humpback whale
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka hump back, hump-backed; incl NZ humpback, otherwise doubt NZ specificity]

>  They grow about 50 feet in length, and are quite slender, only having about half to one-third of the diameter of, say, a hump-backed whale. <i>(Jul 7, 1930,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  The agreement follows, on the whole, familiar lines, and under it it will not be lawful to take .. humpback whales of less than 35ft ... <i>(Jun 10, 1937,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Tory Channel whalers struck a big humpback whale the other week, which rushed the boat and stove in three planks. <i>(Jul 8, 1896,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  At about two o'clock in the afternoon they were about a mile outside when they noticed a big humpback spouting not far away from them. <i>(Jun 26, 1913,Colonist (Nelson),2</i>)

>  News from the whaler Splendid, now at Friendly islands, says that she has taken five humpback whales in less than five weeks. <i>(Oct 4, 1881,Bay of Plenty Times,3</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> 5 boats in chased a humpback <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,453</i>) [NOTES: 1836 Aug 7 Capt. Joy <i> Mary Mitchell </i> log in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 453]

>  MEGAPTERA NOVE-ZEALANDiAE. The New Zealand Humpback. <i>(1875,TrNZi,7/255</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  The humpback (<i> Megaptera nodosa </i>) is quite the most common of whales inhabiting the New Zealand seas: indeed, it is found plentifully in all portions of the world. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/132</i>) [NOTES: (Oliver)]

>  The [whales] most commonly known to Tory Channel whalers are the "hump back." <i>(Apr 29, 1924,Otago Witness,71</i>)

### humpie
 
 <i>n.</i> a humpback whale

>  All binoculars are on the whale. "Something odd about that spout," says Johnnie, once a gunner like his brother. "it's not a humpie," says Joe. <i>(Aug 7, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  <b>GRADY</b> ] Humpies - Humpback whales. A species of whalebone or baleen whale, the Megaptera longimana. it is distinguishable by its low rounded dorsal fin and its humps. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,227</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (later) a rough shack

>  [Gum-fields] seated on an empty Kerosene-case outside his bark humpy, a man was sorting and scraping....gum. <i>(1906,Red Funnel,1/1/42</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> We'll run up a sort of `humpy' for the ladies tomorrow [wrecked on desert island] <i>(1915,Grocer Greatheart,73</i>)

>  i gaze on the moon through the holes in the roof,/ Which shows that the `humpie' is not weatherproof/ ... some calls it a `lean-to', a `humpie', a `shack'.... <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,123</i>)

>  ~ n. a native's hut; a settler's house if small all primitive. [corrupt. fr. Maori compi, a hut] [incorrect HWO] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  <b>RAWLiNSON</b> Like a hermit crab among the winding roots,/ his humpy's two shaky walls of tin/ propped between the rockface and a sack,/ <i>(1963,Of Clouds and Pebbles,12</i>)

>  humpy n. native hut, gunyah or mia-mia; by extension, any primitive dwelling [Abo.]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GUTHRiE-SMiTH</b> the perturbed bird [snipe] would merely emerge with full spread pinions from its isolated humpie of manuka. <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,186</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [ca1985 Nov 21 Nicholas Ransom A small crib-room (q.v.) at the Tiwai Aluminium Smelter, Invercargill, to accommodate four persons]

>  <b>ADCOCK104</b> ... had his own quarters, the two-roomed shop ... The humpy, as we called it, was lighted only by a lamp ... <i>(1979,Butter in a Lordly Dish</i>)

>  <b>HALEY</b> Then again a modest bach or humpy on the edge of the mere would be ideal. <i>(1986,Settlement, The,60</i>)

### humping
 
 <i>n.</i> a lifting and (back-)carrying (of a load) [two of the three citations below are of the verb to hump]

>  [? ptc] ... take the lot by `humping' them out and home. <i>(Dec 19, 1870,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  it was too far from the river, he said, and would necessitate such an amount of `humping'. Bosh about humping! returned the majority. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/117</i>)

>  There's also a competition to find the man/woman who can carry a dead pig the fastest over an obstacle course and if no pigs are shot then they'll end up humping a deer. <i>(Dec 28, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>) [NOTES: Original in Weekend Sun, 25/10/2002]

### humpty
 
 <i>n.</i> the sack, dismissal

>  "Hey, they've given me the humpty," he told the <i>New Zealand Herald</i>. <i>(Sep 2, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> physical retaliation

>  When the All Blacks copped a walloping in Marseilles before Christmas, it was because Fabien Pelous, the Tricolore captain, decided that his side would revert to type, give it some old-fashioned humpty and to hell with the consequences. <i>(2001,Rugby 2001,36</i>)

### humpy
 
 <i>n.</i> a shack (See [also humpie](../A#also-humpie))

>  You couldn't tie women up with a length of chain and leave them out in the paddock, tied to a corrugated iron humpy for days at a time. They'd scream and cry, which would be damned irritating. <i>(Feb 14, 1999,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

>  When i was digging you had to get a licence, and that entitled you to dig anywhere and to put up a humpy (shack). <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,22</i>)

>  The 'humpy' - an Australian name for this basic type of building - was a small four-roomed house, each room running into the other with no passage. <i>(Apr 2004,NZ Memories,No. 47/53</i>)

### Hun
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARK</b> Anyone could be a Hun - a peevish cow, a blowfly in the meatsafe, the stove that wouldn't light. it was also a cover word for all foreigners... The more expensive of the Chinese fruiterers achieved notoriety as Ah Hun... Many of the foreigners were pleasant, industrious, decent people. in this case they were merely 'a bit of a Hun'. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,17</i>)

### hunangamoho
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Hunangamoho, <i>Col[enso]. Apera arundinacea. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

### hundred
 
 <i>n.</i> a subdivision of agricultural land; Historical: a subdivision of some English, American and NZ counties or provinces [(Otago/Southland land subdivision) See Listener Nov. 18, 2000, p. 48.]

>  <b>WAKEFiELD</b> [`Terms of Purchase of Land in the Settlement of Otago' agreed on by the `Otago Association' and the Company] 11. Reservations to be made ... of the Sites of Villages and Towns ... in the Several Parishes and Hundreds ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,317</i>)

>  We hesitate not to confess ourselves admirers of the Hundred system and of agriculture rather then runholding, ... 1947 Note: The whole leader is an argument against declaring Hundreds before it becomes absolutely necessary, ... <i>(Dec 4, 1858,Otago Witness</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Dec 4 Otago Witness in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 10]

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> [The runanga] will bear the same relation to other political machinery ... as the township in the United States of America bears to the county and state organization, and ministers suggest the name of township as the most suitable English term for the community that will be represented by a single runanga. it will correspond with the division of hundreds proposed by his Excellency. [Grey's annotation] i should have preferred the term `hundred'. it is already in use here. The natives will make a strange word out of `township'. <i>(1949,Selected Documents,112e</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Nov 1 Memorandum by Ministers on Governor Grey's Proposed institutions for the Native Race in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 112e]

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> Each district [of the proposed Maori portion of the North island] to be divided into about six hundreds from the runangas of each of which will be selected two persons to represent such hundred in the runanga of the district, and to act as assessor or native magistrate. <i>(1949,Selected Documents,112A</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Nov 2 Sir George Grey Memorandum in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 112a]

>  <b>HEYWOOD</b> in Otago no land can be bought unless within the boundary of some hundred a sit is called - .... The Government of the Province proclaim hundreds when they deem necessary. <i>(1863,Vacation Tour at the Antipodes, A,224</i>)

>  .. as these runs are likely to be wanted for settlement, i should not recommend their being thrown into a gold field till after they have been thrown into Hundreds when the Waste Lands Board could refuse to sell any portion ... considered highly auriferous. <i>(Apr 20, 1869,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1869 May 20 letter in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 16]

>  The Provincial Government have the power of declaring any portion or the whole of a run into `hundreds' as they are called, the land in this hundred can be bought by any person at [pound]1 per acre for the cheapest price. <i>(Feb 9, 1873,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Feb 9 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 173]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> [in Otago] a `hundred' and the land is then opened up for sale at 1 [pound] per acre.... The purchases of land within a `hundred' have the exclusive privilege of depasturing stock upon the unsold lands within the `hundred' in proportion to their purchased property. A small fee or assessment of so much per head, is paid to the Government for this privilege. [Until the land is sold. All land unsold after a few years may be brought at ten shillings per acre] <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,197</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> The old Otago Block had already been divided into Hundreds, at first three in number, but was now subdivided for greater convenience into eight. The first extended from the Heads to the Taieri river, ... Within these Hundreds the rural lands were open for sale at 10s. and acre, with the 40s. improvement conditions. Outside them were 600,000 acres, open for sale at 10s. an acre, in blocks of not less than 2000 acres, without the improvement clause. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,167</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Grey and his Ministers introduced also a system of local government; under this plan the Maori country was to be divided into districts and `hundreds', over each of which a Civil Commissioner was to be placed to grapple with the task of governing the natives in his zone of influence ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/233</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> He had to find rent of ground to give him the right to run cattle on `the hundred,' and had to pay two and sixpence assessment a head to the Wardens to be spent on road-making. <i>(1937,Thomas,94</i>)

>  <b>WAiTE</b> Whatever were the ancient-English meanings in Otago [a `hundred'] came [in 1850s-60s] to signify run country surveyed and cut up for small settlement freeholds. And one can appreciate what the term meant to a runholder who had just got stocked up and his run working efficiently. <i>(1948,Pioneering in South Otago,38</i>)

>  <b>SCOTTER</b> in 1869, ... the administration of the hundreds was centralized in Dunedin (Times June 18, 1869). <i>(1948,Run, Estate and Farm,15</i>)

>  HUNDRED Under the old land-subdivision system in New Zealand, a subdivision of a County. Still used in parts of Southland in the place of Survey District. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  <b>ELDRED-GRiGG</b> The [Otago] provincial government also empowered itself to confiscate grants and create small farming settlements called 'hundreds'. <i>(1980,Southern Gentry,66</i>)

>  According to Dr Ray Hargreaves.. the hundred line road relates to the subdivision of land in the 19th century, and was borrowed from an English tradition... "On payment of an annual license fee, landowners within a hundred had the privilege of running stock on the unsold portions. The first three hundreds for Otago were proclaimed in October, 1854." <i>(Nov 18, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

### hundred-inch country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a land area with a heavy rainfall and leaching, requiring particular pasture management.

>  We call this part of Taranaki the '100-inch country.' in the 100-inch country the sooner the land is converted from its rough state into permanent pasture the better ... Another point discovered by the settlers of the 100-inch country was the necessity for heavy liming. <i>(May 21, 1942,NZ Farmer,16</i>)

### hundred-legs
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  These [Myriapoda] are mostly of the harmless `thousand-legged' species, whose only protection appears to be to curl themselves into a spiral and emit an evil-smelling fluid; but one occasionally gets one of the `hundred-legs,' or `Meggy-monny-legs,' who, with his well-armed, fierce appearance, has gained the reputation of being poisonous. <i>(1901,TrNZi,33/220</i>)

### hundy
 
 <i>n.</i> elliptical for "a hundred per cent"

>  "i'm not quite a hundy yet but, ah, i just enjoyed it out there," he grinned. <i>(Mar 16, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

### hune
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>JACKSON</b> Mattresses were made from the down on the seed of the raupo, <i>hune</i>, thoroughly dried in the sun, ... <i>(1935,Annals of a NZ Family,86</i>) [NOTES: (c1860)]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Hune, Col[enso]. Pappus of the seeds of <i>Typha angustifolia. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),765</i>)

>  As a cataplasm for ulcers they used the leaves of the kohoho or poroporo (<i>Solanum aviculare</i>) - and for wounds ... they used ... also the hune, or pappus down of the large Bulrush, but merely as a protection against dust, etc. <i>(1869,TrNZi,38</i>) [NOTES: 1869 Colenso TrNZi(On the Botany of the North island) in 1981 Brooker et al. NZ Medicinal Plants 15]

### hunga kainga
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  The manuhiri are now called hunga kainga (people of the house). <i>(Dec 19, 1999,NZ Catholic,7</i>)

### hungahunga
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> One house contained some tons of hungahunga [a's-acute], or silken flax; the others with muka maori [o-diaresis], or common native flax. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/140</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The flax (<i>korari</i>,) forming the dresses is of two or [176] three kinds; the most coarse, called muka, is employed for working the common garment (<i>kakaou</i>[a1-acute] <i>maori</i>[a-acute].) This flax is also made use of for laying up ropes, lines, &c. The superior dress called Kaitaka[a2-a3-acute], is made from silken flax, (<i>hunga hunga</i>[as-acute].) <i>(1849,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/175</i>)

>  <b>LiNDSAY</b> in Otago the general name of the [flax] plant, as a whole, is `Korari,' - or as metamorphosed by the settler, pronounced and spelt `Koura<i>d</i>i,' [a-macron] or `Cora<i>d</i>i' [a-macron]. The same term is applied in the North island equally to the whole plant, and to the dried flower-stem when cut for rafts or other purposes; or exclusively to the flower-stalk. in the North [83] island, the Flax-plant is also known as `Harakeke,' or `Herekiakia,' - its usual name there. ... The lower part of the flower-stem is `Kaikaha;' the outer part of the leaf `Parakoka;' [a1, o-macron] the refuse of the leaf `Hunga-hunga;' the flax, dressed or not, or scraped fibre, manufactured from the leaf, `Whitau,' or `Muka.' <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,82</i>)

### hungerfine
 
 <i>n.</i> ultra fine wool induced by starvation [(woolgrowers')]

>  Hungerfine: Ultra fine wool induced by starvation. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,56</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of wool] fine and weak due to drought or a dietary deficiency.

>  <b>COOP</b> Since the wool at the break is not only weaker but is also finer ('hunger fine') the finest part of the staple comes at the ends instead of the middle of the staple. <i>(1950,Shearing Ewes before Lambing,25</i>)

>  Neither group grew good fine wool, much was 'hunger fine' and break was a serious fault. <i>(Mar 23, 1967,NZ Farmer,25</i>)

>  More hungerfine wool will be around in the Canterbury region and this will be something to watch for in the new season. The drought will make the wool hungerfine and this should be kept separate. [hunger fine] <i>(1985,NZ Shearer,1 2 Autumn/Winter 48</i>) [NOTES: ??NZ Shearer 1 2 Autumn/Winter 48]

>  <b>MAF</b> Hungerfine: ultra fine wool induced by starvation. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,56</i>)

### hungry
 
 <i>adj.</i> of land, soil, in poor condition, needing much fertiliser [ca1920 W.H.B. Orsman. -->(country)]

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> Just where Kingston Crossing is now the country was of such a poor hungry type that it was popularly called Starvation Flat. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,39,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> ...into the headwaters of the Maunganuiohau River, which was famous as one of the hungriest areas on the lake block <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> also hungry-looking, of quartz or other potentially auriferous material, non-auriferous. [(goldfields)]

>  Nevertheless, a glance will convince the experienced miner that [the reef] is free from the peculiar appearance there is about non-auriferous quartz, which is denominated by miners as `hungry-looking stuff' <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,78</i>)

>  At the eastern face ... the good looking quartz is only from 5' to 12' in thickness, the remainder being hungry-looking. <i>(1875,AJHR,3/39</i>) [NOTES: Report Goldfields]



 
 <i>adj.</i> mean, niggardly [often in phr. hungry for money (greedy)]

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Doug still remembers the hungry stations where they ate old ewe, potatoes, rice pudding and, at the weekend, one pound of `brownie' per man. ... The boss said `brownie' was cake. We knew it only as bread dyed with bullocky's joy (treacle), and sparingly dotted with currants. <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations Ni,97</i>)

>  hungry ... 5. Austral. & N.Z. greedy; mean. 6. N.Z. (of timber) dry and bare. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,546</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 239]]



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land] malnourished and with a thin sole of grass.

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> The toughest stretch of our trip was winding our weary way home over the then hungry Canterbury plains. <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,22</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> .. a poor hungry flat <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,59</i>)

>  But it [light land] is very 'hungry' - that is, it consumes humus and other manures quickly. <i>(Mar 20, 1933,NZ Journal of Agriculture,182</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i>

>  The hungry wool from poorly reared sheep, which carry very light fleeces, may appeal to him [the American buyer]. <i>(Jun 15, 1911,NZ Journal of Agriculture,349</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>adj.</i>

>  Barren Reefs, or Buck Reefs. ... in Otago they consist of very wide, massive, bold outcrops of `hungry' glassy quartz ... <i>(1908,TrNZi,41/81</i>) [NOTES: Finlayson]

>  Small pieces of quartz are included in the conglomorate bands which have already been described, but such as were seen by the writer of this report are of a decidedly `hungry' appearance. <i>(1910,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 9 (N.S.): 46</i>) [NOTES: (Adams Raukumara, Poverty Bay)]

>  hungry a. of gold ore, unpromising. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> it was encouraging to think of Bluey's nugget when working a block of `hungry' wash. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,63,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> it may have been that the white `hungry' appearance of much of the quartz on the surface discouraged earlier prospectors and they did not bother to test it by `dollying' and washing a few samples. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi,29</i>)

>  <b>NOLAN</b> Hungry: of quartz or a mining site, barren or barely payable. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,112</i>)

### hungry country
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MOORE & iRWiN</b> POMADERRiS KUMERAHO. Kumarahou is at home in the gumlands of Auckland, those broad tracts of hungry country where great kauri forests once grew, leaving deposits of resin - ... Flower heads rubbed between wet hands make a lather, hence the name `gumdiggers' soap'. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,88</i>)

### hungry Sunday
 
 <i>n.</i> Sunday overtime, applied for, and worked by one `hungry for money'. [(Wellington tramways) ca1950 F.M. O'Brien To have hungry Sunday (or h. call-back) = `Of those who, hungry for money, accept overtime joyfully, and apply for it' (call-back is a notification of extra (overtime) shift-work, ie. called back on duty)]

### hunt
 
 <i>n.</i> the ability of a dog to drive or hunt stock away from the handler; also to back on command while doing this. [(farming)]

>  Hunt: Ability of a dog to drive or hunt stock away from the handler, also to back on command while doing this. it can do a `straight' or `zig-zag' hunt. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,24</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> hunt off, get rid of.

>  25th - Loafer called; dirty loafer, hunted him; found my largest apple had fallen off the tree. <i>(Oct 25, 1904,NZ illustrated Magazine</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  ... while in the act of hunting a dog for the sheep a kea flew down to the back of a sheep ... <i>(1907,TrNZi,39/284</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to drive (sheep) away from the shepherd, or from a given spot.

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> Mike stopped hunting them [sc. sheep] up [the race] and shouted to Legge, `When are you going to start drafting? ...' <i>(1970,Tat,25</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Viper had turned her mob neatly over to Rough, who was hunting them away down the mountainside <i>(1934,Windy island,232</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>ACLAND</b> To hunt sheep off the higher parts of their winter country onto lower, safer [land] when snow is expected; e.g., `We hunted down every day for a week, but no snow came.' This is a very common expression in the Lakes district of Otago. i cannot remember hearing it in Canterbury, though the job is done often enough. <i>(1930,Early Canterbury Runs</i>) [NOTES: [1918]]

>  Dogs which take to driving sheep away are broken in to huntaway and are called htwys (q.v.). Some dogs will both hd. and htwy and are called handy ds. <i>(Nov 25, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>LiLiCO</b> [The dogs] would head, lead, hunt away, force and back. <i>(1934,Sheep Dog Memoirs,27</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> i taught him to hunt correctly and to back sheep and finally disposed of him for [pounds]22. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,2</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> He could hunt a mob away, as well as pull, and he was also a champion yard dog, barking when commanded. <i>(1970,Tat,101</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> in April 1921 Ron was taken out to help with the autumn muster. One of the musterers ... gave him his first dog ... who would hunt as well as head. Davis also gave him a stout mustering-stick and showed him how to make the best use of it when sidling across a steep face. <i>(1981,Both Sides of the River,78</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sheepdogs] to drive sheep in a particular direction [often towards gateways or yards.]

>  Handy - a dog that heads a bit, hunts with a bit of noise and possibly backs as well. <i>(Oct 15, 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,24</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Hunting sheep is all about shepherding, not chasing, and can't be carried out at pace. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,72</i>)

>  He is well bred, very forceful and will head, hunt and back. <i>(Jul 8, 1971,NZ Farmer,102</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Sheep - it is in their nature to be inconvenient - are invariably over or down or up there, and shepherds want them somewhere else. They must be hunted to that place, in dog trials off the flat and up the hill in the Straight Hunt. The ZigZag uses three sets of two poles to represent such obstacles and the dog must hunt the sheep left, then right, then left through them. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,136</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to move sheep forward or on; to use (a dog) as a huntaway. [1930 Acland.]

>  <b>STRONACH`</b> The next day we spent hunting the sheep on towards Boundary Creek, which runs into the Saxon, and the day after that we moved [15] camp to the Boundary Gully camp, and pushed all the sheep across the creek. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,14</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> `You go ahead and take the gate, and i'll hunt them up the race,' Mike said. ... `You're hunting them through too fast!' Legge shouted back. `Give a man a fair go, can't you!' [26] Legge and Ustice hunted the sheep forward, and they ran nicely for a while. <i>(1970,Tat,25</i>)

### hunt back
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to drive stock from former grazing areas [especially from high summer country.] (See [hunt down](hom-hyp.md#hunt-down))

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> Every day i walked the ridges and hunted back sheep trying to make their way up to the high country. <i>(1942,Early NZ Families,158</i>)

>  <b>MACGREGOR</b> He was boundary keeping while doing a little fencing , and every day he had to hunt the sheep back out of the snow country. <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,40</i>)

### Hunt Ball
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a popular rural social event [competing with the Dog-Trial Ball as the social event of the year in many rural districts.]

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> The kids were farmed out so we could go to the Hunt Ball and not have to rush home. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,33</i>)

>  <b>SALiSBURY</b> The Hunt Ball was held in Gisborne, which we were all hoping to attend. <i>(1994,i Was So Lucky,73</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Can't remember what ball it was, The Hunt or the Solway or what ... <i>(1996,Trouble with Stock Agents, The,11</i>)

### hunt course
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dog trial competition course for huntaway dogs.

>  Studying the country is just as important on hunt courses. <i>(1991,Constitution of NZ Dog Trial Assoc,4B</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> When there were big mobs of Merino wethers in the South island High Country they were used regularly on hunt courses and very successfully. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,101</i>)

### hunt down
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [high country farming] [see hunt back] to move sheep from the tops or basins to a lower area [for tupping or when snow threatens.]

>  Hunt down, to hunt the sheep off the higher parts of their winter country on to lower, safer spurs when snow is expected. e.g. 'We hunted down every day for a week but no snow came.' <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),17</i>)

>  <b>MCKENZiE</b> it [the work] might be mustering, eye clipping, hunting down or even snow raking. <i>(1951,Wakatipu Pioneers,111</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> it so happened that that day i was given a job 'hunting down' a block called Plenty. i had to ... hunt them down to safer levels. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,29</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Hunt down: to hunt sheep out of the high basins and spurs and down to lower country. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,44</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [(station)]

>  Hunt down. To hunt the sheep off the higher parts of their winter country on to lower, safer spurs when snow is expected, e.g., `We hunted down every day for a week, but no snow came.' This is a very common expression in the Lakes district of Otago. i cannot remember hearing it in Canterbury, though the job is done often enough. (C,1918) <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The,17</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>NEWTO</b> Hunting down: Where sheep are wintered on high unfenced country subject to snow it is a common practice to keep them hunted down from the higher, more dangerous snow levels. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,188</i>)

>  <b>Masters</b> it was built to house the hunter shepherd, whose job it was to keep sheep from straying over the unfenced Kereru boundary, and on to the Ruahine tops, and hunt down the wild dogs and pigs that infested the area. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,103</i>)

### hunt hill
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hill on a dog trial huntaway course.

>  <b>GORDON</b> Conversations at the foot of the hunt hill are usually fragmented and spasmodic .. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,150</i>)

### hunt sheep
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) three sheep used in the huntaway competition in dog trials.

>  <b>GORDON</b> Many of these [duties] concerned protocol, such as hosting at the barbecue the two landowners - the long head was on another property - and the supplier of the hunt sheep. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,191</i>)

>  if the hunt sheep caused problems from time to time ... <i>(Jun 1, 1994,NZ Farmer,45</i>)

### hunt up
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [shepherding] to hold or gather sheep together temporarily.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Many sheepmen advocate 'hunting up' (periodically mustering them into a mob) during tupping ... <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,48</i>)

>  <b>FOSTER & WRiGHT</b> Stage Two involves musterers moving out from Jack Mack's hut each morning and hunting up the sheep along the gullies and ridges, pushing them down Jack Mack's valley ... <i>(1983,Stockman Country,12</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  You do not seem to think much of your wife: why don't you keep away from her altogether and not get yourself into trouble hunting her up? <i>(Jul 14, 1906,NZ Truth,56/4/3</i>)

### huntaway
 
 <i>n.</i> a dog that barks on command, trained to control sheep; a noisy sheepdog trained to drive sheep forward, away from the musterer; a special type of NZ sheep dog used for mustering; driving and backing dog that barks on command; see also Nelson huntaway. [(farming)]

>  The affable, witty, almost Bertie Wooster-like character, Terrence, works a restaurant with all the skill of a huntaway at a dog trial. <i>(Jul 10, 2004,Dominion Post,E8</i>)

>  A huntaway should be a free-running dog, noisy, and under good command, and should lose points for not taking directions readily, insufficient noise, being too rash or too slow, and not keeping the sheep on a good course. <i>(Sep 11, 1912,Otago Witness,35</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> A new hand ... is wise investing in a good huntaway or a heading dog. He can practice [sic] on the `killers' i.e. the sheep kept handy for station use, or better still, a good noisy yard dog, as all station hands are liable to be called upon to assist in drafting, etc. <i>(1913,Country Work,33</i>)

>  Huntaway.A dog whose work is to drive sheep forward when mustering. As a verb the word is used in two sense, illustrated in the sentences: `That dog hunts away well' and `i hunt away with that dog.' (C.) <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The,17</i>)

>  Sheep dog.- Dog to work sheep; collie (q.v.). S.ds. run naturally either to bring sheep towards you or to drive them away; and when a puppy shows his preference he is broken in for that work. The dogs that bring sheep are called heading ds. (q.v.) and most of these can be taught to lead (q.v.) if desired. Dogs which take to driving sheep away are broken in to huntaway and are called htwys (q.v.). Some dogs will both hd. and htwy and are called handy ds. They are not generally high-class heading dogs but very useful about hand and sometimes very useful mustering dogs. Some men have a special slewing d., but a well-trained, hdg. dog or htwy. will usually slew (q.v.). (C.) <i>(Nov 25, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>LiLiCO</b> So far as i have heard and read, there is no other part of the world which has strains of natural huntaways, so we must accept the idea that huntaways are entirely a New Zealand product. <i>(1934,Sheep Dog Memoirs,26</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> There are breeds [of dog] for the varying types of [mustering] work,. ie, huntaways and heading dogs. The former are noisy dogs, and as the name implies, hunt sheep away, barking freely. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,13</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> HUNTAWAY: A noisy dog whose natural instinct is to hunt or chase sheep away. On account of their noise they are used for forcing mobs, work in the drafting yards, and for clearing or hunting' sheep off tracts of country. <i>(1949,High Country Days,195,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> So the shepherds soon bred dogs which had more the characteristics of the Old English sheepdogs - bouncing barking dogs which ... drive ... sheep ... They called them huntaways and this word is now so well known in New Zealand that it is part of the language. <i>(1951,High Country,5</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> heading dog, no decent handy dog, and a rough assortment of huntaways. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,15</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> He used his dogs for both rabbiting and sheep work, but only Nell, the huntaway, would have anything to do with pigs; and all she would ever do, was bail and keep her distance. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,159</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> A sheepdog is either a huntaway, a heading dog, or a handy dog. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,97</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> HUNTAWAY: A noisy dog whose natural instinct is to hunt or chase sheep away. On account of their noise they are used for forcing mobs, work in the drafting yards, and for cleaning or `hunting' sheep off tracts of country. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,187</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> The huntaway sheep dog is a New Zealand development. Original imported strains were all heading dogs whose instinctive method was to head the sheep and, working silently, to pull them back to their masters. Early New Zealand shepherds, however, needed the occasional noisy dog which could flush sheep from cover on rough country. ... Huntaway: A noisy dog, whose natural instinct is to hunt or chase sheep away. On account of their noise, they are used for forcing mobs, work in the sheep yards, and for clearing or hunting sheep off tracts of country. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/493</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Huntaway: A dog which drives sheep away, barking on command - or more frequenty at its own pleasure. <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,253</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Huntaway: A noisy dog used for driving the sheep away from the musterer. <i>(1972,Mountain World,310</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> huntaway: A huntaway is a NOiSY (barking) WORKiNG DOG used to drive sheep, cattle or recently, deer to new paddocks. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,54</i>)

>  Huntaway: Special type of New Zealand dog used for mustering. A driving and backing dog that barks on command. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,24</i>)

>  huntaway n. N.Z. a dog trained to drive sheep at a long distance from the shepherd. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,546</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 366; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 239]]

>  <b>RENNiE</b> The Beardie was developed as a barking dog because in some parts of Scotland, particularly in the Western Highlands, much of the ground is covered with bracken, which combined with the deep gullies known as corries, makes it impossible for a heading dog to see his sheep. Here the shepherd has to get out above the sheep and more or less chase them out of the thick cover. These dogs were known as hunters, and it is presumably an adaptation of this name which led to the designation, huntaway. At the turn of the century there were four well known strains of huntaways about North Canterbury and Marlborough - the Clown, Rag, Borderglen's Rose and Pollock's Old Clyde breeds. Of these the Clown strain certainly had Beardie blood, believed to have been started with a half Beardie brought out from Scotland. <i>(1985,Working Dogs,12</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> The fact that the dog learned the command that denoted he was to lead, automatically placed him in position to take up huntaway work. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,36</i>)

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> The huntaway sheep dog is a New Zealand development. [ca1901] <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/493</i>)

>  Good stock dogs those Labs, great bark. They bred them into huntaways in the old days you know, for the bark. <i>(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,238</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Maid, although by a huntaway dog, would catch sheep anywhere on the hill. <i>(1996,Boss's Story, The,141</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The sooler is another rough and tough dog of the huntaway type and is renowned for his ability to shift even the most stubborn of sheep. <i>(1967,Return to Open Country,38</i>)

>  The KZ7 team.. could improve its status with the locals by using a doggie mascot which is more authentically kiwi - a huntaway, perhaps, or a pig dog. <i>(Dec 15, 1991,Dominion Sunday Times,10</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a NZ-bred barking sheepdog that shepherds, gathers and hunts sheep towards gateways or yards.

>  <b>LiLiCO</b> .. the huntaways are entirely a New Zealand product. <i>(1934,Sheep Dog Memoirs,26</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Huntaway: A noisy dog whose instinct is to hunt or chase sheep away. <i>(1949,High Country Days,195</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Huntaways or barking dogs work with noise: chasing, blocking and pushing to herd sheep into position. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,135</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The huntaway is 100 per cent New Zealand bred .. the huntaway was developed because our early musterers and runholders saw the benefits of properly applied noise. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,73</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McLiNTOCK</b> Huntaway. in both huntaway classes three sheep are set loose at the foot of the hill. While the shepherd remains at the bottom, his dog, working and barking to command, hunts the sheep upwards on a defined line away from his master. in the zig-zag hunt, the sheep are directed through three sets of flags 1 chain apart and spaced equally up the hill on a zig-zag course of approximately 440 yards. The course for the straight hunt is about the same length, from the foot of the hill up to and through a set of flags 1 chain apart. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/493</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGiLL</b> huntaway mustering dog; boulder in upper South island rolled down steep slopes with same mustering purpose. [Jim Henderson] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,59</i>)

### huntaway country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) steep, rugged farm land that is difficult to muster.

>  <b>KERR</b> it was huntaway country where you lifted sheep onto a ridge out of bush before you could walk them anywhere; totally different to the South island's general chase-them-downhill-an'-move-them-along-the-flat. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,173</i>)

### huntaway man
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dog triallist who specialises in the huntaway breed.

>  <b>KERR</b> Some blokes are heading-dog men and some are huntaway men and some go both ways.. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,141</i>)

### hunter
 
 <i>n.</i> a spider [(Chatham Islands)]

>  On the front page of The Post (March 18) is a picture of a Chatham island spider known as the hunter and found mainly on South East or Rangatira island, southeast of Pitt island. This island was once used as a sheep station and the person who was in charge of the island told us as children that these spiders kill birds' chicks and eat them. <i>(Mar 29, 1989,Evening Post,24</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) huntaway sheepdog.

>  <b>HALL</b> You go on to a station where the owner believes you shouldn't use a lot of hunter voice and consequently when you bark up you get a great effect. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,94</i>)

>  huntaway dog: hunter. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### Hunter chain
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fence system composed of wire battens or 'chains'.

>  Hunter chain is used extensively in fencing dips, hollows, ditches, ravines and creeks. <i>(Aug 8, 1996,NZ Farmer,19</i>)

>  Clearing sale: .. 4 snig chains, spinning jenny, docking scrim, Hunter chain fence ... <i>(May 11, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,47</i>)

>  <b>SALiSBURY</b> Along came also the hunter chains concept of fencing ... <i>(1994,i Was So Lucky,186</i>)

### Hunter fence
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fence system in which battens are replaced by chains at metre intervals and posts are used only at 30 metre intervals.

>  <b>TAiT</b> The Hunter fence is now used from the North Cape to the Bluff. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol i,29</i>)

>  if concrete posts are used a Hunter fence lasts more than a man's lifetime. <i>(Mar 7, 1957,NZ Farmer,18</i>)

>  ... in the hill country cost-finding fencing tests carried out by the NZ Department of Agriculture, the Hunter fence was proven beyond a doubt to be the most economical. <i>(Sep 28, 1961,NZ Farmer,6</i>)

>  i started using Hunter fence more than 20 years ago. Hunter fence is particularly suitable for hilly country as it eliminates 'dip posts.' <i>(Jul 1971,NZ Dairy Exporter,30</i>)

>  The Hunter fence is also made by Atlas and can cut costs by 30%. <i>(Dec 23, 1976,NZ Farmer,54</i>)

>  <b>TAiT</b> The Hunter fence differs from the orthodox method of construction in many respects. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol i,30</i>)

### hunter shepherd
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MASTERS</b> it was built to house the hunter shepherd, whose job it was to keep sheep from straying over the unfenced Kereru boundary, and on to the Ruahine tops, and hunt down the wild dogs and pigs that infested the area. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,103</i>)

### hunter-pilot
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a helicopter pilot in a deer-hunting team.

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> in the front left-hand seat sat hunter-pilot Joe Collins, also clad in warm clothes, a red jumpsuit, woollen scarf, and topped with a crash helmet referred to by hunter-pilots as a 'skid lid'. A do-it-yourself hunter-pilot had arrived. <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,9</i>)

### hunters' pie
 
 <i>n.</i> [(? tramping)]

>  i attended the making of a hunters' pie. First i laid the [newly taken] skin on the ground, cutting up the meat into small pieces, not forgetting the kidney and a small piece of bacon ... salted and added a little water and then folded carefully. When the fire was well burnt down i scraped the sticks and embers away, plaining the pie with the folded side on top. <i>(1936,Hills and Valleys,2/10</i>)

### hunting block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area allocated to hunters and cullers by the Forestry Service.

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> On the way back to the hunting block, we spotted two deer, and as we had no nets with us, it was obviously bulldogging time. <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,129</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Tussock Hut then seemed, and likely was, about as remote as you could get on a Forestry Service hunting block in the North island. <i>(1996,Great Hunting Yarns,93</i>)

### hunting down
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Hunting down: Where sheep are wintered on high unfenced country subject to snow it is a common practice to keep them hunted down from the higher, more dangerous snow levels. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,188</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) mustering of stock from summer country down to lower-altitude snow-free winter land.

>  <b>MCKENZiE</b> .. William McKenzie was killed in a snow slide on his way home after a day spent in hunting down sheep. <i>(1951,Wakatipu Pioneers,118</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> it is country that calls for keen, competent hillmen - men not frightened to walk, for these sunny faces are open to high tops and basins and boundary-keeping and hunting-down are a regulation winter job. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,51</i>)

### hunting farm
 
 <i>n.</i> a game farm

>  His 2000-hectare farm.. was barely making enough money to support one person before he took it over in 1992 and converted it into a hunting farm - known today as a game estate. <i>(Aug 19, 2004,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

### hunting the lead
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [huntaway sheepdogs] controlling sheep at the front of a mob with the use of noise.

>  <b>GORDON</b> [A dog's] noise needs to be aimed directly at sheep which, the dog firmly believes, require its counselling and control. For this reason, we often talk about 'hunting the lead', though the dog is at the back of the mob. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,70</i>)

### hunting up
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Many sheepmen advocate `hunting up' (periodically mustering them into a mob) during tupping but it is my belief that ewes ... are best left alone at that time. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,48</i>)

### huntsman spider
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The huntsman spider - an Australian interloper which grows to between 5cm and 7cm - was first found in Christchurch last month <i>(Jan 14, 2006,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  The latest New Zealand sensation to hit Hollywood is a bark-eating spider from Avondale... Why the Avondale spider? Truth be told, the Delana is really the Australian huntsman spider, but since the species is indigenous to Australia, it could not be exported from there. <i>(Aug 12, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  Pupils at New Lynn Primary School, West Auckland, having turns at handling a live Avondale spider, an Australian huntsman spider established in the Avondale region of Auckland since the 1920s. Photograph: Chris Hoult, Western Leader, 10 August 2000. <i>(Aug 10, 2000,internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.landcareresearch.co.nz/education/insects_spiders/facts/Avondale.asp Accessed 13/02 2006.]

 <i>n.</i>

### huperei
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Gastrodia Cunninghamii (Cunningham's Gastrodia</i>) ... Maori name, <i>Huperei. </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,126</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> Fungus icicles - <i>Hericium coralloides</i> ... Beech strawberry - <i>Cytaria gunnii</i> ... Lawyer's wig ... Huperei -<i> Gastrodia cunninghamii </i> Hakeke - Jew's ear fungus - <i>Auriculuria judea </i> <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,85</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Gastrodia cunninghamii</i> BLACK ORCHiD ... Huuperei ... Flowers numerous, white within, dark brown without ... <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,294</i>)

### hupiro
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also stinkwood](../A#also-stinkwood))

>  <i>Coprosma foetidissima</i> (the hupiro of the Maori and the `stinkwood' of the settler), ... has proved a disappointment ... <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/266</i>)

>  <b>LAiNG & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Coprosma foetidissima (The Foetid Coprosma)</i>..../ Maori name <i>Hupiro.</i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,425,427</i>)

>  . such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. Toetoe, phormium, cabbage tree, with swamp coprosma and hupiro may figure as a developmental phase on wet soils. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>RiCHARDS</b> <i>C[oprosma] foetidissima</i> ... [61] it does not smell while fresh, but Maoris do not use any of the karamus as firewood as heat makes the odour worse than ever. ... Stinkwood. KARAMU, HUPiRO. <i>(1952,Chatham islands,60</i>)

>  <b>WiLSON</b> <i>Coprosma foetidissima</i> STiNKWOOD ...Huupiro ... Shrub or small tree ... Leaves strong-smelling when crushed. <i>(1982,Stewart island Plants,61</i>)

>  <b>Cockayne</b> (And) Coprosma foetidissima. <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)

### hurangi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> On emerging from Pamaki [a's-acute], we ascended another plain of some miles in circumference, covered with fern and bush, above which the tupakihi [a-acute], or native elder-berry, occasionally shewed its bending stem, yielding to the weight of the fruit, which hung in purple clusters. ... The natives, on perceiving me make use of this berry, warned me not to swallow any of the seeds, as the doing so would make me <i>ourangi</i>, drunk, or mad. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/110</i>)

### hurdy-gurdy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>REED</b> `hurdy-gurdy' [is a hand gum-washing machine, turned by a handle] -->[See also Reed Story of Kauri Gum] <i>(1948,Gumdigger, The,56</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> The machine age on the gumfield appears to have been heralded as early as 1915 when an ingenious digger, whose name has unfortunately been forgotten, introduced what came to be known as the `hurdy-gurdy' or `gurdy.' ... [83] in its simplest form it was a tub, or a section of a small galvanised iron tank, the bottom perforated with small holes. in the centre stood a strong, revolving, upright shaft, to the lower part of which was attached a wooden paddle, extending on both sides of the shaft, almost to the sides of the tank. The top of the revolving shaft was fitted with a horizontal handle, enabling the paddle to be swung to and fro in a half-circle. This simple machine was usually operated by two men; a supply of gum-bearing dirt having been put into the tank, buckets of water were thrown in by one man while the other vigorously turned the shaft handle this way and that, stirring up the soil while the muddy water flowed out through the perforations. <i>(1972,Gumdiggers, The,82</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a piano

>  You would believe that old hurdy-gurdy over there [sc. an old piano] had as much music in it. <i>(1945,Died in the Wool,49</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a hand gum-washing machine (in NZ also the roundabout at a fair see OED revision file 16/52.])

>  The golf course - we dug all that for gum with a spade, a spear, a kerosene tin and a hurdy-gurdy... You hand-picked the dirt out, put it in the gurdy to wash it and stuck it in a heap. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,22</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (esp.something which revolves). [ca1920 J. Morrison a merry-go-round]

>  <b>COOZE</b> They stuck the hurdy-gurdy [of the revolving clothes-line] seven feet off the ground. .... a hurdy-gurdy of a joke. <i>(1946,My Little State Home in Suburbia,12</i>)

### hure
 
 <i>v.</i> Used of fast or sudden motion, to hure along, go huring off (?whoring or phs a var. here of hare: P: run very fast: Shrewsbury School: late 19-20c, by 1920 fairly general SE); cf hury, cf also hone, hoop; hooer. [ca1935 Havelock, and frequently since.]

### hurepo
 
 <i>n.</i> bittern

>  The hunters caught poaka (wild pigs), parera (grey duck), putakitaki (paradise duck), kereru or kuku (pigeon), and hurepo (bittern). <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,249</i>) [NOTES: Written of 1940]

 <i>v.</i> to vomit, also to have a hurl [ca1946 University students, Weir House Wellington]

>  For a start, when some claw-handed crone in an overheated, overdecorated, overpriced dress shop used the phrase "special day" Jane didn't immediately hurl all over the zirconia-encrusted white satin bias-cut princess-length gown she was trying on. <i>(Apr 20, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),3</i>)

 <i>exclam.</i> (See [hooray](hom-hyp.md#hooray))

This year the Hurricanes and the Blues have suffered from an obvious crisis of confidence which may have had something to do with this... <i>(May 7, 2004,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm</i>)

### huru huru
 
 <i>n.</i>

The other dress, called <i>parawai</i>, is exceedingly scarce; it comes from the Southern island, and is made of strips of dog's fur, arranged indiscriminately all over a very large mat of the finest flax. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/323</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a fern

 <i>adv.</i> a coll. (? children's) occas. intensive, ? from huring (see hure) [ca1935 eg. a hury big marble.]

 <i>n.</i>

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a fish

### hustler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>OGiLViE</b> in August 1918 Hugh McCully of Waitohi showed a gathering of local farmers what his new drag harrow was capable of doing. This cultivator, which he called a `hustler', had a width of 6ft 9[and a half] ins and was fitted with fifteen tines. Hustlers are commonplace nowadays. <i>(1971,Moonshine County,74</i>)

>  Preparation Cost. ...Two grubbings with hustler (one for irrigation): one man and six horses for twelve hours <i>(1934,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,15/289</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a drag-harrow cultivator designed and built by a South island farmer in 1917.

>  <b>EVANS</b> An implement of rather unique design is the McCully 'Hustler.' it was designed in 1917 by a South Canterbury farmer. <i>(1956,History of Farm implements,18</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> With the coming of the tractor, the hustler had to be modified. <i>(1956,History of Farm implements,19</i>)

>  <b>MCCULLY</b> My reason for building the first hustler was that i had a field of turnips which had been fed off by cattle till late spring. <i>(Sep 17, 1945,Letter,18</i>) [NOTES: in Evans (1956) History of Farm implements in NZ]

>  <b>OGiLViE</b> in August 1918 High McCully of Waitohi showed a gathering of local farmers what his new drag harrow was capable of doing. This cultivator, which he called a 'hustler' had a width of 6 ft 9 and a half ins and was fitted with fifteeen tines. Hustlers are commonplace nowadays. <i>(1971,Moonshine County,74</i>)

### hut
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (in early European pre-settlement use) a Maori dwelling

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Afternoon we returned to our new Friend the natives ...; we therefore landed & went up to their hut ... <i>(Apr 9, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/250</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (in early colonial use) a simple house, usually having only one room.; (then) spec. (esp. in the mountain country) of trampers' and musterers' etc. huts, but cf and cp crib, shack, whare, cf daub and wattle hut.

>  At the end of the bush an alarming fire occurred, the high wind blowing the sparks of an open air fire against a neighbouring hut, built of dried fern and wattles. <i>(Feb 1, 1851,Lyttelton Times,3</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> You are fairly entitled to something better than a warre[e-acute], say a two-roomed hut for yourself ... and a large, strong wata, or store raised on posts to keep your provisions safe from rats. ... slabs plastered with clay are also used for walls ... One of these huts must serve you for your first year's wool-shed, with the help of a few hurdles in front. <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,90</i>)

>  Kaikora calved ... [17] Wintry weather, tried to bail up Kaikora but failed. tinkered up hut Mr Newton still there. <i>(Mar 16, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>PRiNCE</b> [tramping hut] The Beech Hut is built of slabs with a tree-fern roof, and contains eight sleeping berths ... <i>(1889,Diary of a Trip,10</i>)

>  Hut (h&ubreve.t), n. [Add.] 2. A house for shearers or other labourers on a station; as, the men's hut, there shearers' hut. This is sometimes a large, permanent structure of wood, brick, or stone. -->1898 Morris AE 207 Hut, n the cottage of a shepherd or a miner. The word is English but is especially common in Australia, and does not there connote squalor or meanness. The `Men's Hut' on a station is the building occupied by the male employees. [1844] <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third international,2024</i>)

>  [a tramping hut] The accommodation at the Routeburn Hut, although plain, is quite satisfactory. The hut consists of three rooms furnished with wooden bunks, tables, shelves, and forms .. <i>(1907,TrNZi,39/198</i>)

>  hut n. the building on the sheep station where the shearers live, often a large stone building.-hut'-keep v.t. to look after the hut of a miner, shepherd, etc. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

>  <b>ODELL</b> They will no doubt be ultimately opened up by the formation of tracks and the erection of huts, and will then allow of the fullest exploitation of the winter sports attractions of the Park. <i>(1935,Handbook Arthur Pass National Park,20</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> He [the pioneer pastoralist] has built a domicile of some sort, be it of canvas, calico, bagging, sticks, ferntrees, grass, slabs, mud, clay, stone, or wattle and daub. He may call it various names according to its materials, but three names would not enter his head - i.e., crib, back, or shack. He may call it a cabin, a shanty, a cottage, a warrie [whare], but probably he would use the commonest term of all - hut. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,33</i>)

>  <b>BEATTiE</b> There is a danger of all writers about the old days using modern terms which were not used in the period they were writing about, and i have always tried to steer clear of this pit. Young writers are particularly prone to go astray in this matter, and we see talk of pioneer shacks in Otago, using a modern Canadian word, where an Otago pioneer would have said hut or warrie. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,11</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Mustering is a big job, using two huts and holding paddocks. <i>(1982,Historic Sheep Stations of NZ,7</i>)

>  <b>367</b> hut 1 n. ... NZ & Austral. accommodation for stockmen, shearers, etc. on a station. 3 hut-keeper NZ & Austral. person who looks after huts on a station during working hours. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Hut. House where the station hands or shearers live. Some call it a whare. Until the 1930s and 40s the men usually ate, cooked and slept in a one-roomed hut with a fireplace at one end and bunks in tiers around the walls. Often there were no facilities for washing other than the creek. Today most huts are quite comfortable, with separate rooms for eating and sleeping, showers and toilets. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,96</i>)

>  indeed, accommodation in the European Alps lies closer to the hotel end of the shelter spectrum than the tin cans we call huts in the Southern Alps. <i>(Aug 16, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Jack's Hut, near the summit of Arthur's Pass, is a rare surviving example of the roadmen's huts that dotted New Zealand's highest altitude main highway. Each roadman was responsible for maintaining a section of the road - clearing slips, filling in potholes - and lived with his family in a hut along his stretch. <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Heritage,No. 90/10</i>)

### hut book
 
 <i>n.</i> [book recording information for and about users of tramping huts; visitors book with additional function of tracking trampers]

>  The hut book, always a special part of any visit to a remote site, occupies several minutes of our time. <i>(Jul 2, 2011,Your Weekend,32</i>)

### hut boss
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person in charge of a mustering hut, usually the cook/packman.

>  <b>HALL</b> There would be seven of us in a small hut: six musterers and the cook, who was also the hut boss and who packed our gear to the next hut on packhorses. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,84</i>)

### hut fever
 
 <i>n.</i> [a US term?]

>  <b>LYON</b> i had often heard of men who, living together and close pals for many years, suddenly develop a distaste for each other and even hate the sound of each other's voices. i had read the works of Bret Harte and other American writers, with frequent reference to the distaste and even hate which was dubbed `Hut Fever,' and i actually knew one of such a party. <i>(1952,Faring South,172</i>)

### hut up
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sheep] to hide in scrub to avoid muster.

>  <b>PETER</b> Sheep hide or hut up to avoid being mustered. <i>(2000,Peter Glossary,4</i>)

### hut-bagging
 
 <i>n.</i> (Sport) the tallying of the number of tramping huts one has stayed in.

>  He also contracted the hut-bagging disease rife amongst VUWTC members, and he says his tally is now somewhere in the 700s, with 300-odd to go. While he acknowledges that hut-bagging can become a silly obsession - "where one can't see the hills for the huts" - it does have the advantage of "leading me to places i would not normally think of tramping". <i>(Aug 2008,FMC - (Federated Mountain Clubs of NZ) Bulletin,41</i>)

### hut-keep
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  hut n. the building on the sheep station where the shearers live, often a large stone building.-hut-keep v.t. to look after the hut of a miner, shepherd, etc. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,6</i>)

### hut-keeper
 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL</b> The party to consist of owner, shepherd, hut-keeper, bullock-driver <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,88</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> [A new settler] engages himself as a ... hutkeeper at a station <i>(1858,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,27</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> in Australia two or three shepherds generally tend their flocks from the same outstation ... where they bring their flocks every night to be folded, while the hut-keeper watches over them. <i>(1859,Five Years' Residence in New Zealand,158</i>)

>  <b>JONES</b> April, 1861 - it is Sunday. We rose later than usual. There are five men sleeping in the hut. i sleep in a bunk on one side of the fire, Mr. Haast ... sleeps upon the opposite one; my bullock-driver and hut-keeper have two bunks at the far end of the hut, along the wall; ... The usual mutton and bread for breakfast with a pudding made of flour and water baked in the camp-oven after a joint of meat - Yorkshire pudding, but without eggs. While we were at breakfast, a robin perched on the table and sat there a good while pecking at the sugar. <i>(1861,Samuel Butler,1/87</i>) [NOTES: 1861 April Butler's Note in 1919 Jones Samuel Butler - A Memoir 1/87]

>  <b>PRENTiCE</b> [reference Otago 1850s] The [out-] shepherd had no hut-keeper for a companion and assistant as in Australia, and generally sees no human being for months at a time except the man who is sent to him at certain periods with ration supplies. <i>(1870,Tale of NZ,37</i>)

>  <b>BAiNES</b> <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,53</i>)

>  <b>WAYTE</b> Poor Tom, if he is lucky, may get employment as hut-keeper, or as `odd-man' about a station <i>(1879,Prospecting for Gold,53</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> ... the Little'un was one day detailed as hut-keeper. it so happened that he had our entire stock of crockery to wash up, ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/140</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> The station hands [c1861] comprised a shepherd, bullock driver, hut-keeper ... <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,73</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> On the opposite side of the [Matakaua] island to where the brigantine lay, was a hut belonging to the superintendent of the mine, here we had been invited to take up our quarters, ... and it was intimated that the `hut-keeper' would provide for our temporal necessities. ... The `hut-keeper' had procured some grog, and we found him dancing about as tipsy as possible; ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/167 (or 2/67)</i>)

>  Letter from Aust clergyman. ...got the stock-man and hut-keeper, the only men on the station, to come to me] <i>(1851,Church Missionary Chronicle,6/293</i>)

### hutch
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Rail and pukeka were caught by snares set in their sphagnum moss runs, ...  <i>n.</i> crutch [(shearers' rhyming slang)]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i>Acaena Sanguisorbae</i>, Vahl. ... Nat. names, `Hutiwai', Middle island, Lyall; `Pirikahu' and Piri piri', Col. <i>ibid.</i> 765 Hutiwai, Middle island, Lyall. <i>Acaena Sanguisorbae</i>. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,i Flowering Plants 54</i>)

>  <b>LiNDSAY</b> Hence the plant [<i>Acaena sanguisorbae</i>] is only too familiar, as the `Native Burr' to the sheep farmers alike of New Zealand, Australia, and Tasmania. ... To the Otago settler the plant is also known as the `Bidi-bidi,' or `Beta-beta;' to the North island Maori as `Hutiwai,' `Piri-piri,' (Colenso), or `Piri-kahu,' (Colenso). <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,59</i>)

The `HUTiWAi', or `PiRiPiRi'.- ... an infusion of the leaves is said to afford a substitute for tea, but it is the opinion of the writer that the results are not commensurate with the trouble of brewing. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,125</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> Hutiwai [Maori name] .. Burr [Settlers' name] .. Acoena sanguisorbae .. Herb <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,177</i>)

>  [query from] `Utuwai', Colyton:- Will you please let me know if there is any way of destroying utuwai, or bidabid as it is more commonly called? spg <i>hatiwai </i>in error(?) <i>(Jan 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,687</i>)

>  <b>HiLGENDORF</b> PiRi PiRi (<i>Aceara ... sanguisorbae</i>). This name is commonly corrupted into [called] bidi bid. in the North island the plant is called hutiwai or utiwai and by mistake poroporo. it is a native species well known to all New Zealanders, and is a weed chiefly on land in process of being brought into occupation. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,99</i>)

>  Hutiwai (<i>Acaena [364] sanguisorbae</i> and <i>A. novae zelandiae</i>) are indigenous weeds of hilly country devoted almost entirely to sheep grazing. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,363</i>)

>  hutiwai A Maori name for one of the small burr-bearing plants called `biddy-biddy'. This plant also grows in Australia. (Pronounce us. hoo|-ti-wai.) <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),203</i>)

### hydraulic

### hydraulic deal
 
 <i>n.</i> (hire purchase)

>  A HiGH COURT jury was told by Crown counsel Justin Smith in Wellington yesterday of a hire purchase `hydraulic deal' - a transaction, in which a hire purchase agreement stated a deposit had been paid whereas, in fact, it had not. ... Mr Smith said that during the trial the jury would hear about what was known colloquially as a `hydraulic deal'. This was a situation where there was a deal in which a dealer intended to sell a truck for, say, $50,000 and required a deposit of $10,000 by any hire purchaser. in the case of a $50,000 truck (a hypothetical example) the dealer knew the hire purchase company was going to have reservations about financing the full $50,000. `So the dealer draws up a hire purchase agreement anyway. The real price is still $50,000. But on the agreement the dealer said he had received the sum of $10,000 by way of deposit.' Mr Smith said that if a hire purchase company accepted the assignment of the agreement the dealer got his money in full and the customer got his truck (then claimed to be worth $60,000) without having to pay a deposit. <i>(Apr 12, 1985,Evening Post</i>) [NOTES: 1985? Apr? c12-24 Dom ?Evening Post - check date]
